Hypo-elasticity, Cauchy-elasticity, corotational stability and monotonicity in the logarithmic strain Thanks: Davide Bernardini, Department of Structural and Geotechnical Engineering, Sapienza University of Rome, Rome, Italy, e-mail: davide.bernardini@uniroma1.it
Patrizio Neff, Sebastian Holthausen, Marco Valerio d’Agostino, Adam Sky, Ionel-Dumitrel Ghiba, and, Robert J. Martin

Abstract
We combine the rate-formulation for the objective, corotational Zaremba-Jaumann rate D ZJ D ​ t ​ [ σ ] = ℍ ZJ ​ ( σ ) . D , D = sym ⁡ D ​ v , \displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,\qquad D=\sym\mathrm{D}v\,, operating on the Cauchy stress σ \sigma , the Eulerian strain rate D D and the spatial velocity v v with the novel “corotational stability postulate” ⟨ D ZJ D ​ t ​ [ σ ] , D ⟩ > 0 ∀ D ∈ Sym ⁡ ( 3 ) ∖ { 0 } \Bigl\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\Bigr\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\setminus\{0\} (CSP) to show that for a given isotropic Cauchy-elastic constitutive law B ↦ → σ ⁡ ( B ) B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B) in terms of the left Cauchy-Green tensor B = F ​ F T B=FF^{T} , the induced fourth-order tangent stiffness tensor ℍ ZJ ​ ( σ ) \mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma) is positive definite if and only if for σ ^ ( log B ) : ⁣ = σ ( B ) \widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sigma(B) , the strong monotonicity condition (( TSTS-M++ )) in the logarithmic strain is satisfied: sym ⁡ D log ⁡ B ​ σ ^ ​ ( log ⁡ B ) ∈ Sym 4 + ⁣ + ⁡ ( 6 ) \displaystyle\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6) (TSTS-M ++ ) ⟹ \displaystyle\implies\qquad ⟨ σ ^ ​ ( log ⁡ B 1 ) − σ ^ ​ ( log ⁡ B 2 ) , log ⁡ B 1 − log ⁡ B 2 ⟩ > 0 ∀ B 1 , B 2 ∈ Sym + ⁣ + ⁡ ( 3 ) , B 1 ≠ B 2 . \displaystyle\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{2}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,B_{1},B_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3)\,,\;B_{1}\neq B_{2}\,. Thus ( CSP ) implies ( TSTS-M++ ) and vice-versa, and both imply the invertibility of the hypo-elastic material law between the stress and strain rates given by the tensor ℍ ZJ ​ ( σ ) \mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma) , since ℍ ZJ ​ ( σ ) \mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma) is accordingly positive definite. Notably, ( TSTS-M++ ) is one way to characterize the fundamental notion of “stress increases with strain” . The same characterization remains true for the corotational Green-Naghdi rate as well as the corotational logarithmic rate, conferring the corotational stability postulate ( CSP ) together with the monotonicity in the logarithmic strain tensor ( TSTS-M++ ) a far reaching generality. It is conjectured that this characterization of ( CSP ) holds for a large class of reasonable corotational rates. The result for the logarithmic rate is based on a novel chain rule for corotational derivatives of isotropic tensor functions.



Keywords:
nonlinear elasticity, hyperelasticity, rate-formulation,
Eulerian setting, hypo-elasticity, Cauchy-elasticity, material stability, corotational derivatives, objective derivatives, chain rule, constitutive inequalities, logarithmic strain, stress increases with strain

Introduction

In the theory of finite elasticity, numerous mathematical models can be used to describe the relation between the stress acting on an elastic body and its deformation. To this day, however, it remains uncertain how to identify models which predict a
physically reasonable
material behaviour.
Although a vast number of constitutive requirements have been suggested in order to ensure mechanically plausible material behaviour of Cauchy-elastic or hyperelastic laws
[
38
,
39
,
40
,
83
]
– many of which can be described as a condition of
stress increasing with strain
– there is no consensus on which requirements should be considered necessary or even desirable. Truesdell considered this to be the
“Hauptproblem”
of nonlinear elasticity

[
122
]
.

Recently, constitutive questions in solid mechanics have increasingly been approached from a data-driven perspective
[
47
]
. In particular, machine learning techniques are being employed not only for finding numerical approximations to given problems
[
58
,
96
,
129
,
47
]
, but also for developing constitutive laws in nonlinear elasticity and related fields
[
27
,
118
,
50
,
28
,
116
,
63
,
30
]
. While this approach can indeed provide new material models which satisfy known constitutive requirements
[
50
,
30
]
, it cannot give a meaningful answer to the main question posed by Truesdell’s Hauptproblem: which requirements
should
be imposed on the relation between stress and strain?

In this contribution, we therefore continue the classical investigation of nonlinear elasticity theory from an analytical point of view. In particular, we present
a variation on a theme initially introduced by Hill
[
38
,
39
,
41
]
following first steps by Truesdell
[
121
]
and Noll
[
85
]
.

Truesdell and Noll rediscovered and formalized, in the mid fifties of the last century, the work of Hencky
[
10
,
36
,
37
]
and Oldroyd
[
88
]
on the formulation of rate-type equations encoding the constitutive law in isotropic nonlinear elasticity involving necessarily
objective time derivatives

[
64
]
of the Cauchy stress tensor
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
such that the constitutive law is expressed as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).\,D,\qquad\textnormal{with}\quad D=\sym\mathrm{D}v\quad\textnormal{the Eulerian stretching}.

(1.1)

Here,
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
is a fourth-order tangent stiffness tensor mapping symmetric tensors to symmetric tensors (minor symmetry)
[
19
,
130
]
, which does not necessarily have to be self-adjoint (major symmetric), and (
1.1
) needs to be integrated along the loading path. Such models are known as hypo-elastic
1
1

1

Today, hypo-elastic models appear prominently in rate-formulations of finite strain elasto-plasticity, splitting the stretch rate
D=D_{e}+D_{p}
additively into elastic and plastic parts and setting
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).(D-D_{p})
, see e.g.
[
3
,
12
,
20
,
91
,
128
]
and references therein. Otherwise, current FEM-software such as Abaqus™ and LS-DyNA require the input of the constitutive law for nonlinear elasticity in the rate format with
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
for some specified objective rate and some spatial stress tensor.

.

In contrast, in nonlinear
Cauchy-elasticity
, the stress-strain relation is defined in absolute terms via a stress response function

\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)\,,\quad B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)\,,

mapping the left Cauchy-Green
B=FF^{T}
corresponding to a deformation gradient
F
to the Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma(B)
. While hypo-elasticity can be applied to more general problems in continuum mechanics, it was first shown by Noll
2
2

2

Noll
[
85
]
considered only the Zaremba-Jaumann rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
.

[
85
]
, to the surprise of Truesdell
[
123
]
, that every Cauchy-elastic material is hypo-elastic if the mapping
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
is isotropic and invertible
3
3

3

It is quite remarkable that Truesdell and Noll
[
125
]
largely ignored this invertibility assumption for the Cauchy stress
\sigma
in their subsequent development for nonlinear elasticity.
. Under these conditions, hypo-elasticity can be considered an equivalent formulation of nonlinear elasticity, more specifically a
rate-formulation
in which stress increments are functionally related to strain increments in order to describe the constitutive law
[
19
]
. In that case, for any given objective time derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}
, there exists a naturally
induced tangent stiffness tensor

\mathbb{H}^{\sharp}(\sigma)
with

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\sharp}(\sigma).D=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,.

(1.2)

However, even for perfect nonlinear elasticity, there is a range of limitations and problems for such a rate-formulation:

1)

There are infinitely many different objective time derivatives which can be seen as covariant derivatives
[
52
]
(not all of them are Lie-derivatives
[
21
,
52
,
64
]
).

2)

One can choose different spatial stress tensors to encode the same constitutive law.

3)

The choice of
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
is open but a priori restricted to isotropy
[
85
]
.

4)

The rate (
1.1
) might not be integrable towards a Cauchy-elastic or hyperelastic model
[
8
]
.

We will address these issues here as they present themselves.

At the same time, in nonlinear Cauchy-elasticity, it was and is still unclear
4
4

4

Šilhavý
[
114
]

argues that
“the final solution of the Hauptproblem came in 1977 with the work of J. M. Ball [who] showed that the triplet of weakened convexity conditions, viz., polyconvexity, Morrey’s quasiconvexity, and the rank 1 convexity […] is exactly what is missing”
.
However, while these generalized convexity properties indeed provide a satisfying answer to central existence problems in nonlinear elasticity from the point of view of the direct methods of the calculus of variations, they do not fully adress the mechanical questions raised by Truesdell.

as to what constitutive assumption should reasonably be placed on the nonlinear elasticity law
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
in general to ensure
physically reasonable response
, loosely connected to the idea that
stress increases with strain
,
5
5

5

But which stress? Which strain? What does
“increase”
mean? We will arrive at a tentative answer at the end of this paper.

which Truesdell called the
“Hauptproblem”
of nonlinear elasticity
[
122
]
.

In an attempt to answer this question, Hill modified (
1.1
) into a formulation for the spatial Kirchhoff stress tensor
\tau=J\,\sigma
and required

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\tau],D\rangle>0\qquad\textnormal{for all}\quad D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}

(1.3)

for a specific subclass of objective derivatives, among them the Zaremba-Jaumann rate (cf.
[
45
,
46
,
136
]
). He continued to examine the consequences of imposing (
1.3
) for the constitutive law
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\tau(B)
and obtained with his complicated method of Lagrangian axes for the Zaremba-Jaumann rate the result that (here stated for hyperelasticity)
\tau(B)=\widehat{\tau}(\log B)
satisfies the condition
\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\tau}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
if and only if (
1.3
) is satisfied. Leblond
[
60
]
took up Hill’s development and proved a similar result for the Cauchy stress
\sigma
in the hyperelastic setting.

In
[
14
]
the authors generalize Leblond’s results to the Cauchy-elastic case and show that for isotropic nonlinear elasticity, the relation involving the logarithmic strain tensor
[
77
,
78
,
79
]

\displaystyle\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}:\penalty\ \left\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\right\rangle>0

\displaystyle\iff\quad\log B\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\;\textrm{is strongly Hilbert-monotone}

(1.4)

\displaystyle\iff\quad\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)

holds for the corotational Zaremba-Jaumann objective derivative of the Cauchy stress
\sigma
, which is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-W\,\sigma+\sigma\,W,\qquad W=\textnormal{skew}(\dot{F}\,F^{-1})\,.

(1.5)

Here,
B=F\,F^{T}
is the left Cauchy-Green tensor and
F=\mathrm{D}\varphi
is the Fréchet derivative of the deformation
\varphi
. Relation (
1.4
) implies the
T
rue-
S
tress
T
rue-
S
train strict Hilbert-
M
onotonicity (TSTS-M
+
)

\left\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{2}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\right\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,B_{1}\neq B_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3),

(1.6)

setting throughout

\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)=\sigma(V)=\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\qquad\textrm{for $V=\sqrt{B}$ \quad by abuse of notation}.

(1.7)

One goal of this paper is to show that the same characterization can be obtained when using the corotational Green-Naghdi rate as well as the corotational logarithmic rate. Hence, TSTS-M
+
is one way to constitutively characterize the notion that
“stress increases with strain”
.

Because of its key role, we will refer to the requirement

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle>0\quad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}\qquad\textnormal{as the {\bf corotational stability postulate (CSP)},}

(1.8)

where
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
denotes an arbitrary corotational derivative of the Cauchy stress
\sigma
, e.g. the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
.

Furthermore, it is important to note a complete
paradigm shift
: instead of concentrating on the hypo-elastic formulation (
1.1
) per se, we presently only consider the hypo-elastic rate-formulation to elucidate constitutive issues for a given
Cauchy-elastic
law. Indeed,

our aim is to understand what kind of given isotropic constitutive Cauchy-elastic laws
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
satisfy
\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle=\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0\quad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}
.

In order to properly answer our guiding question, also in the context of more general corotational rates, we, nevertheless, need to dive deep into the hypo-elasticity framework (
1.1
). In this way we shift attention from
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
towards
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
. Therein, following the paradigm shift, we are only considering those fourth-order stiffness tensors
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
that are
induced
by a given invertible Cauchy-elastic law, thus circumventing any integrability issue
[
19
]
and already answering one of the problems alluded to above. Moreover, we formally motivate
CSP
for the Cauchy stress
\sigma
from stability requirements in isotropic linear elasticity. For the Zaremba-Jaumann and Green-Naghdi rate, we provide a semi-explicit representation for the induced tangent stiffness tensors
\mathbb{H}^{\rm ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\rm GN}(\sigma)
, respectively. More precisely, we obtain in Section
2.1
the formulas (for an invertible law
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\rm ZJ}(\sigma).D=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,D+D\,B]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathbb{H}^{\rm GN}(\sigma).D=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[2\,VD\,V]

(1.9)

together with (see Proposition
2.9
)

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{r}\det\mathbb{H}^{\rm ZJ}(\sigma)\neq 0\\
\det\mathbb{H}^{\rm GN}(\sigma)\neq 0\end{array}\right\}\quad\iff\quad\det\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)\neq 0\quad\iff\quad\det\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\neq 0,

where the determinant of a symmetric tensor
\mathbb{C}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
is defined in the Appendix
A.1
. With the representation (
1.9
), which is true for every invertible law
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
(this invertibility
7
7

7

Invertibility of the Cauchy stress
\sigma
is independent of the used strain measure. In
[
100
]
Richter writes:
“
In Verallgemeinerung des Hookeschen Gesetzes nennt man ein Material rein elastisch, wenn die [Cauchy] Spannungen in umkehrbar eindeutiger Weise von den Dehnungen abhängen
,”
which translates to
“in generalization of Hooke’s law, a material is called purely elastic, if the [Cauchy] stresses depend in a uniquely reversible way on the stretches.”

was already required by Richter
[
34
,
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
]
for idealized perfect isotropic nonlinear elasticity), we can rewrite the characterization of
CSP
for the Zaremba-Jaumann rate as

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\rm ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\,[BD+DB],D\rangle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[D\,B+B\,D],D\rangle>0

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).D,D\rangle=\langle\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).D,D\rangle>0.

(1.12)

The latter equivalence can be viewed as a pure statement of matrix analysis for isotropic tensor functions and we provide a suitable context for this observation in Sections
3
–
5
. From the matrix analysis viewpoint it furthermore transpires that the equivalence (
1.12
) also holds true for the Green-Naghdi and the logarithmic rate and thus possibly for a large class of reasonable objective corotational rates. For the Green-Naghdi rate and an arbitrary corotational rate we formulate corresponding conjectures for any isotropic tensor function
\sigma
. If the latter conjecture is true, this would confer to the equivalence CSP
\iff
TSTS-M
++
a far reaching universality. In fact, the result for the Green-Naghdi rate will be shown in an upcoming contribution, using entirely different methods than those described here. The result for the logarithmic rate will be given in Section
4.2
following still other lines of thought owing to the exceptional structure of the logarithmic rate
[
133
]
. Thus, the equivalence of CSP with TSTS-M
++
is already not confined to the Zaremba-Jaumann rate. The problem with more general corotational rates resides in not immediately having a useful representation like the ones presented in (
1.9
).

In Section
4.1
we recall some properties of corotational rates in general and prove a new universal chain rule for corotational rates, which allows to extend our findings to the logarithmic rate.

With this chain rule at hand, we are able to find a general representation of
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
in the spirit of (
1.9
) for a large class of reasonable corotational rates defined by their spins and with the help of
[
80
]
we ascertain (
1
) for this class of spins. We propose a further subclass of these spins defining the
“positive”
corotational rates which merits future investigation based on geometrical arguments in the hope of extending our characterization of CSP.

In the Appendix, we introduce the notation and gather necessary requisites from linear algebra and matrix analysis, among them the Daleckii-Krein formula for derivatives of primary matrix functions. Moreover, we give examples that monotonicity of
\widehat{\sigma}
in
\log B
is different from monotonicity of
\sigma
in
B
. We also provide an example of a hyperelastic formulation that globally satisfies CSP (and therefore TSTS-M
++
). On the other hand, we show that a polyconvex slightly compressible Neo-Hooke model gives an invertible Cauchy stress relation for which
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
is not positive definite, such that CSP is not satisfied. In addition, we recall the implications of CSP for purely volumetric energy functions and the known problem of the Zaremba-Jaumann rate in zero grade hypo-elasticity.

It is our belief and motivation that the corotational stability postulate CSP is equivalent to TSTS-M
++
for a large class of corotational rates and that it can be used as a cornerstone for a novel local existence result in finite strain Cauchy-elasticity with pre-stress, cf. Blesgen et al.
[
11
]
. As a byproduct of our development we appreciate again the pivotal role that the logarithmic strain tensor
\log V
should play in isotropic nonlinear elasticity
[
5
,
36
,
65
,
78
,
79
,
83
]
.

2
Introduction to three-dimensional hypo-elasticity

A hypo-elastic material, in the sense of Truesdell
[
123
]
and Noll
[
85
]
, obeys a constitutive law of the form
8
8

8

Rate-formulations in terms of the Kirchhoff stress
\tau=J\,\sigma
will not be considered here. See e.g. Korobeynikov
[
54
]
, Bellini and Federico
[
6
]
, and Federico et al.
[
22
]
for results in this direction.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D\quad\iff\quad\underbrace{D}_{\textnormal{the stretching}}=[\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)]^{-1}.\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]}_{\textnormal{the stressing}}=\mathbb{S}^{*}(\sigma).\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],

(2.1)

where

•

\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
is an appropriate
objective
rate of the Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
,

•

\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
is a constitutive
fourth-order
tangent stiffness
tensor
,

•

\mathbb{S}^{*}(\sigma)=[\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)]^{-1}
is a constitutive
fourth-order
tangent compliance
tensor
and

•

D=\sym\,\mathrm{D}v
is the
Eulerian strain rate tensor
, measuring the spatial rate of deformation

or
“stretching”
, where
v
describes the spatial velocity at a point
\xi
in the current configuration.

In this formulation it can be neatly seen that the elastic stretching
D
depends solely on the current stress level
\sigma
together with the rate of stress
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
(
“the stressing”
in the parlance of Romano
9
9

9

The work of Romano et al.
[
105
,
106
,
107
]
is particularly intriguing. They use a novel differential-geometric framework for setting up rate-formulations of nonlinear elasticity using suitable Lie-derivatives
\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}
. Their final result is, however, substantially departing from classical concepts like hyperelasticity or Cauchy-elasticity, which both need a reference configuration. In our rate-formulation, to the contrary, we remain in the classical context. While Romano et al. use a different rate formulation, they arrive conceptually at constitutive equations of the type

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}(e)=\mathbb{S}(\sigma).\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}(\sigma),\qquad\textnormal{where $e$ is some spatial strain measure,}

and assume that the tangent compliance tensor
\mathbb{S}
is invertible. In
[
106
, eq. 8]
, it is assumed further that
\mathbb{S}
is positive definite as well as major symmetric, and even that
\mathbb{S}(\sigma)=\mathrm{D}_{\sigma}^{2}\Xi(\sigma)
for a convex complementary energy potential
\Xi\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

et al.
[
105
]
) as seen by the objective derivative.

Throughout this work, without loss of generality, we will start directly with a Cauchy-elastic stress response instead of a hyperelastic energy potential
10
10

10

Truesdell
[
120
, p. 88]
writes:
“[…] if the strain energy in the elastic case has an especially simple form, no particularly simple form of the resulting hypo-elastic coefficients [of
\mathbb{H}^{\sharp}(\sigma)
] is to be expected.”

(cf. Ogden
[
87
, sec. 4.2]
), since this gives us a better take on how to arrive at the corresponding hypo-elastic formulation and we can a priori determine and evaluate constitutive requirements on the Cauchy stress without being concerned by explicit calculations about integrability of the Cauchy stress-strain law towards hyperelasticity.

As for objective time derivatives
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
of the Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
, there are infinitely many different possible choices, see e.g.
[
52
,
80
]
. However, some are more reasonable than others. Paraphrasing
11
11

11

Statements are taken from a discussion on imechanica.org (
https://imechanica.org/node/1646
), regarding the
“correct”
choice of a time derivative.

Andrew Norris (cf.
[
86
]
for an example of his work), the most reasonable time derivatives are those that are
corotational
and
objective
, since
corotational

“just means that the rate is taken with respect to a frame that is rotating relative to where you are sitting”
, while
objective

“means that this extra rate of rotation, or spin, should be defined only by the underlying spins in the problem and the velocity gradient
L
”
(see e.g. also the related works by Xiao et al.
[
131
,
132
]
, Aubram
[
2
]
, Federico
[
6
,
89
]
, Fiala
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
, Govindjee
[
33
]
, Korobeynikov et al.
[
53
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
, Pinsky et al.
[
93
]
and Zohdi
[
138
]
. From now on we denote by
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
a general corotational derivative.

For this reason, here we restrict our attention primarily to two specific objective and corotational derivatives, namely the
Zaremba-Jaumann
derivative, using the spin given by the vorticity
12
12

12

It can be shown that
W=\dot{R}R^{T}+R\,\textnormal{skew}(\dot{U}U^{-1})\,R^{T}
cf. Gurtin et al.
[
35
]
, Nasser et al.
[
69
]
and the books by Ogden
[
87
, p.126]
and Truesdell
[
124
, p.21]
.

tensor
W=\textnormal{skew}\,L,\;L=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}
,

\boxed{\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,W-W\,\sigma&=Q^{W}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{W})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{W}]\,(Q^{W})^{T}\\
&\textnormal{for}\ Q^{W}(t)\in{\rm O}(3)\quad\textnormal{such that}\;W=\dot{Q}^{W}\,(Q^{W})^{T}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)\,,\end{aligned}}

(2.2)

and the
Green-Naghdi derivative
or
polar rate
(cf.
[
6
,
75
]
), using the polar spin
\Omega=\dot{R}\,R^{T}
related to the polar decomposition
F=R\,U
:

\displaystyle\boxed{\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega-\Omega\,\sigma=R\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[R^{T}\,\sigma\,R]\,R^{T}\qquad\textnormal{for}\ R(t)\in{\rm O}(3)\quad\qquad\textnormal{with}\ \Omega\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\dot{R}\,R^{T}\in\mathfrak{so}(3).}

(2.3)

Here,
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\dot{\sigma}
denotes the material or substantial time-derivative. For more information on the notation, we refer to the Appendix
A.1
.

The crucial property that characterizes
any objective derivative
[
52
]
is the so called
frame-indifference
, which means that the time derivatives do not depend on a Euclidean transformation of the observer, in the sense that (cf. Lemma
A.13
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q(t)\,\sigma(t)\,Q^{T}(t)]=Q(t)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}(t),\qquad\forall\,Q(t)\in{\rm O}(3).

(2.4)

Note that the material derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
does not satisfy this transformation law.

An important consequence of considering corotational derivatives is that any isotropic scalar function
I(\sigma(t))
of the stress
\sigma(t)
is stationary if the corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
is zero, where we call
I(\sigma(t))
an isotropic scalar function if

\displaystyle I(\sigma(t))=I(Q^{T}(t)\,\sigma(t)\,Q(t))\qquad\forall\,Q(t)\in{\rm O}(3).

(2.5)

This property is crucial when developing plasticity theories (cf. Prager
[
94
]
). In this sense, only corotational derivatives leave physical properties of the Cauchy stress tensor invariant and are therefore the only ones considered here, answering partly the first issue raised in the introduction.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
be any corotational derivative of
\sigma
, i.e. of the form

\displaystyle\hskip-8.0pt\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=Q^{\circ}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}]\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}\quad\iff\quad(Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{\circ}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}]\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\dot{Q}^{\circ}\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\Omega^{\circ},

(2.6)

with a corresponding orthogonal frame
t\mapstochar\rightarrow Q^{\circ}(t)\in{\rm O}(3)
. If
I(\sigma(t))
is any isotropic scalar invariant of
\sigma
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=0\qquad\implies\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}I(\sigma(t))=0.

(2.7)

Proof.

In order to see (
2.7
), from the representation of
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
in (
2.6
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=0\qquad\iff\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}]=0.

(2.8)

Using the standard chain rule and the isotropy of
I(\sigma(t))
we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}I(\sigma(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}I((Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ})=\langle\mathrm{D}I((Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}),\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}]\right]\rangle=0.\qed

(2.9)

Remark 2.2

(Corotational derivatives as special Lie-derivatives)
.

The corotational rate may be interpreted as the Lie derivative with respect to spatial rotation defined by
Q^{\circ}(t)
. Thus, let
\phi
define the (rotation) mapping
\phi(x)=Q^{\circ}x
, then the corotational rate is the
“Lie-type”
derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\phi_{*}[\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\phi^{*}(\sigma)]=Q^{\circ}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\circ})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}]\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}
(cf.
[
86
, p.8]
), where
\phi_{*}
is the push-forward and
\phi^{*}
the pull-back (see also
[
43
]
).

Having discussed which objective derivatives appear to be a naturally sound choice
13
13

13

In Section
4.1
we will show a universal chain rule for corotational derivatives. This possibility is absent for general objective derivatives. The additional chain rule structure gives further support for using only corotational derivatives.

– namely the corotational derivatives
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
– the next important question regards a sound choice for the tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
appearing in (
2.1
). Considering a rigid rotation
F(t)\rightarrow Q(t)\,F(t)
, we have the transformations

\displaystyle D(t)=\sym L(t)\rightarrow Q(t)\,D\,Q^{T}(t),\qquad\sigma\rightarrow Q(t)\,\sigma\,Q^{T}(t),\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q(t)\,\sigma\,Q^{T}(t)]=Q(t)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}(t),

(2.10)

such that the hypo-elastic model (
2.1
) is frame-indifferent if and only if

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{*}(Q\,\sigma\,Q^{T}).(Q\,D\,Q^{T})=Q\,(\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D)\,Q^{T}

(2.11)

is satisfied for any orthogonal tensor
Q\in{\rm O}(3)
. Thus
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D
must be an isotropic tensor function of
\sigma
and
D
. It is a well-known fact that any hyperelastic or Cauchy-elastic model in which
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\penalty B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
is bijective can be written in the format
14
14

14

The constitutive law of every isotropic hyperelastic or isotropic Cauchy-elastic model can always be written in rate-format as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(B)]=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(B).D

(2.12)

with an isotropic fourth order tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(B)
. In order to arrive rewrite this expression as
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
, one needs the invertibility of
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
. The generality claimed in (
2.13
) will be shown in Section
4.3
.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D

(2.13)

(cf. Truesdell
[
123
]
and Noll
[
85
]
), albeit with an expression for
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
that is not easily manageable. The general format of such a mapping is given as (see already Noll
[
85
]
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D=

\displaystyle b_{1}\,D+b_{2}(\sigma\,D+D\,\sigma)+b_{3}(\sigma^{2}\,D+D\,\sigma^{2})+[b_{4}\,\textnormal{tr}D+b_{5}\,\textnormal{tr}(D\,\sigma)+b_{6}\,\textnormal{tr}(D\,\sigma^{2})]\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

(2.14)

\displaystyle+[b_{7}\,\textnormal{tr}D+b_{8}\,\textnormal{tr}(D\,\sigma)+b_{9}\,\textnormal{tr}(D\,\sigma^{2})]\,\sigma+[b_{10}\,\textnormal{tr}D+b_{11}\,\textnormal{tr}(D\,\sigma)+b_{12}\,\textnormal{tr}(D\,\sigma^{2})]\,\sigma^{2},

where the coefficients
b_{i}
are scalar valued functions of the principal invariants of
\sigma
(in
[
9
]
only a subclass is considered). Note, however, that in this generality, the latter formulation is rather useless. Therefore, in the absence of other evidence zero-grade hypo-elasticity
15
15

15

Simo and Pister
[
115
]
write concerning the hypo-elastic formulation:
“[L]ack of experimental evidence supporting a particular form for [the stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
] often leads to the choice of the constant isotropic elasticity tensor
[\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}]
of the linearized theory.”

[
49
]

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D=b_{1}D+b_{4}(\textnormal{tr}D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=2\mu\,D+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D,\qquad\mu>0,\quad 2\,\mu+3\,\lambda>0,

(2.15)

has often been considered and is still sometimes considered as the definition of hypo-elasticity
[
62
]
. For more information about the motivation behind considering zero-grade hypo-elasticity we refer to the Appendix
A.8.1
.

Even though zero-grade hypo-elasticity might appear to be an easy and sound choice
16
16

16

In fact, discarded by Truesdell
[
125
, p.405]
.

– as it provides a major and minor symmetric and positive definite constant tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{*}=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}
and is motivated by linear elasticity – it is easy to demonstrate by simple shear considerations, that zero-grade hypo-elasticity together with the Zaremba-Jaumann rate is a poor choice from a physical point of view (see the Appendix
A.8.5
).

Hence the question remains open which choice for the tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{*}
is a good choice. The next more or less
“obvious”
attempt is to use the tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
that is
induced
by the chosen objective, corotational time derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
for a
given invertible
and
isotropic
Cauchy-elastic stress-stretch law
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
, i.e. to set

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],\qquad\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma].}

(2.16)

Note that any definition of a tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{*}=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
is usually expressed in
B=F\,F^{T}
, i.e.
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(B)
, hence we generally require the invertibility of the stress-stretch law
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
with inverse mapping
\sigma\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)
so that we can write
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma))
. Furthermore,
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
is now dependent only on stresses but not on strains or their rates and for each different objective rate it will be a different stiffness tensor. In general, this induced tangent stiffness tensor has minor symmetry
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
but
no
major symmetry:
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}\notin\Sym_{4}(6)
, i.e.
\langle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}.D_{1},D_{2}\rangle\neq\langle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}.D_{2},D_{1}\rangle
and the corresponding induced tangent compliance tensor
\mathbb{S}^{\circ}(\sigma)=[\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)]^{-1}
shares the same properties as
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}
.

Let us consider two examples of simple shear for this new choice of
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
.

Example 2.3
.

The isotropic, physically linear Cauchy-elastic law
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)

\displaystyle\sigma(B)=\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}),\qquad\nu=0

(2.17)

is bijective viewed as a mapping
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\textnormal{range}(\sigma)
and strictly monotone in
B
. Here,
\nu=\frac{\lambda}{2\,(\lambda+\mu)}
is the Poisson’s ratio. The Cauchy stress
\sigma(B)
has the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative (see Section
2.1
for a general formula)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mu\,(D\,B+B\,D)=2\,\mu\,D+D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D\,,

(2.18)

which, for this
“simple”
law, can be calculated directly from the definition of the Zaremba-Jaumann rate, i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\mu\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}]=\mu\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})=\mu\,(L\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})+(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})\,L^{T}+L+L^{T})

\displaystyle=(L\,\sigma+\sigma\,L^{T})+2\mu\,D=(D+W)\,\sigma+\sigma\,(D+W)^{T}+2\,\mu\,D=D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D+2\,\mu\,D+W\,\sigma-\sigma\,W

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,W-W\,\sigma=2\,\mu\,D+D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D\,.

(2.19)

Regarding the representation formula (
2.14
), here we have
b_{1}=2\,\mu,\;b_{2}=1
and for the remaining
b_{i},i=3,...,12
it is
b_{i}=0
. Thus, we may speak of a hypo-elastic formulation of grade 1. By chance, the induced tangent stiffness tensor has minor
and
major symmetry but the constitutive law (
2.17
) is not hyperelastic. For the planar case with

\displaystyle F(t)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\,t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad B=F\,F^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\,t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\gamma\,t&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1+\gamma^{2}\,t^{2}&\gamma\,t\\
\gamma\,t&1\end{pmatrix}

(2.20)

as well as

\displaystyle L(t)=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad D(t)=\sym L(t)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\
\gamma&0\end{pmatrix}

(2.21)

we see that the rate-formulation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D=\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D

(2.22)

is trivially fulfilled under stress-free initial conditions, and we conclude from

\displaystyle\sigma=\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})=\mu\,\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{2}\,t^{2}&\gamma\,t\\
\gamma\,t&0\end{pmatrix},

(2.23)

that the shear stress
\sigma_{12}(B(t))=\mu\,\gamma\,t
is linear increasing in the amount of shear and therefore physically reasonable when using the Zaremba-Jaumann rate
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
. Of course,
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
is now not constant in the Cauchy stress
\sigma
.

Example 2.4
.

Consider the physically non-linear Cauchy-elastic law
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
,

\displaystyle\sigma(B)=\frac{\mu}{2}(B-B^{-1})+\frac{\lambda}{2}(\log\det B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},

(2.24)

with the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative
[
136
,
46
]
(see Section
2.1
for a general formula)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mu}{2}\,\{B\,D+D\,B+B^{-1}\,D+D\,B^{-1}\}+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(B).D\,,

(2.25)

which again happens to be minor and major symmetric but is also not hyperelastic. Since the constitutive law (
2.24
) is now invertible
[
31
]
, we can write
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma))
. Similarly to the previous example we can determine the shear stress
\sigma_{12}(B(t))
. With
F(t),L(t)
and
D(t)
as in the previous example,

\displaystyle B=F\,F^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\,t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\gamma\,t&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1+\gamma^{2}\,t^{2}&\gamma\,t\\
\gamma\,t&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad B^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\gamma\,t\\
-\gamma\,t&1+\gamma^{2}\,t^{2}\end{pmatrix}

(2.26)

such that the rate equation (
2.25
) is satisfied. Since
\textnormal{tr}\log B=\log\det B=0
, we can calculate the shear stress
\sigma_{12}
directly from (
2.24
):

\displaystyle\sigma_{12}(B(t))=\frac{\mu}{2}\,(\gamma\,t+\gamma\,t)=\mu\,\gamma\,t\,,

(2.27)

which is, once again, linear and increasing in the amount of shear
\gamma\,t
and thus physically reasonable.
\hfill{\square}

Considering the previous two examples, the tangent stiffness tensor induced by a suitable Cauchy-elastic constitutive law appears to be a physically reasonable choice. Since its derivation can be traced back to the derivation of the corresponding corotational, objective time derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
, we will prove next a concise formula for the calculation of this induced tangent stiffness tensor. Proceeding in this fashion we obviate any integrability issue
[
19
,
8
]
that arises if
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma)
is taken in arbitrary form according to (
2.1
).

2.1
Formulas for the corotational Zaremba-Jaumann and Green-Naghdi rates

The following calculations are motivated by the elegant early exposition in
[
23
]
. Recall that the constitutive equation of nonlinear isotropic Cauchy-elasticity can be expressed as

\displaystyle\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\subset\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\quad B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B),\quad(B=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}(3)\;\textnormal{if $\sigma(B)$ is invertible})

(2.28)

with
B=F\,F^{T}
and an isotropic tensor function
\sigma(B)
, i.e.
\sigma(B)
satisfies
17
17

17

As shown already by Richter in 1948
[
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
]
and later by Rivlin and Ericksen
[
104
]
every such isotropic tensor function
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
can e.g. be expressed as

\displaystyle\sigma(B)=\beta_{0}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\beta_{1}\,B+\beta_{-1}\,B^{-1}\,,

(2.29)

where the
\beta_{i}
are scalar functions of the principal invariants of
B

[
70
]
. However, this representation of the elastic law will not be useful for our purposes.

\displaystyle\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\subset\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\qquad Q\,\sigma(B)\,Q^{T}=\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T})\qquad\forall\,Q\in{\rm O}(3).

(2.30)

Recalling the identities
L=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}
and
L=D+W
with
D=\sym L,\;W=\textnormal{skew}\,L
, we calculate the material time derivative (cf.
[
85
, p.37]
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\dot{B}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\dot{F}\,F^{T}+F\,\dot{F}^{T}]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[L\,F\,F^{T}+F\,(L\,F)^{T}]

(2.31)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[L\,B+B\,L^{T}]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]+\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[W\,B-B\,W]\,,

where
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)
is an isotropic fourth order tensor function satisfying

\displaystyle Q\,[\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).H]\,Q^{T}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[Q\,H\,Q^{T}].

(2.32)

Taking time derivatives on both sides of (
2.30
) leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q\,\sigma(B)\,Q^{T}]=\dot{Q}\,\sigma(B)\,Q^{T}+Q\,(\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\dot{B})\,Q^{T}+Q\,\sigma(B)\,\dot{Q}^{T}

(2.33)

for the left hand side, as well as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T})]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q\,B\,Q^{T}]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[\dot{Q}\,B\,Q^{T}+Q\,\dot{B}\,Q^{T}+Q\,B\,\dot{Q}^{T}]

(2.34)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[\dot{Q}\,B\,Q^{T}+Q\,B\,\dot{Q}^{T}]+\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[Q\,\dot{B}\,Q^{T}].

Using (
2.32
) with
H=\dot{B}
shows that

\displaystyle Q\,[\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\dot{B}]\,Q^{T}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[Q\,\dot{B}\,Q^{T}],

(2.35)

leading to the identity

\displaystyle\dot{Q}\,\sigma(B)\,Q^{T}+Q\,\sigma(B)\,\dot{Q}^{T}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[\dot{Q}\,B\,Q^{T}+Q\,B\,\dot{Q}^{T}].

(2.36)

A suitable choice of the arbitrary matrix
Q(t)\in{\rm O}(3)
in combination with (
2.31
) now lead to a formula for the Zaremba-Jaumann and the Green-Naghdi time derivative, respectively.

2.1.1
Formula for the corotational, objective Zaremba-Jaumann time derivative

For the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative we choose
Q(0)={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},\;\dot{Q}(0)=W
in (
2.36
) to obtain

\boxed{W\,\sigma(B)-\sigma(B)\,W=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[W\,B-B\,W].}

(2.37)

Note that this formula was discovered by Noll in the year 1955
[
85
, p. 37]
in the same fashion. Inserting (
2.37
) in (
2.31
) yields

\displaystyle\boxed{\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-W\,\sigma+\sigma\,W=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(B).D}

(2.38)

for the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative. If
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
is invertible, this can be rewritten as
[
85
, p. 37, eq. (15.12)]

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)).[D\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)+\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)\,D]\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D

(2.39)

and determines the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
since
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
, and thus its inverse
\sigma\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)
, is assumed given.

2.1.2
Formula for the corotational, objective Green-Naghdi time derivative

For the derivation of the corresponding formula for the Green-Naghdi derivative
18
18

18

Related to this general rule, in Dienes
[
17
, p. 13]
it is proven that
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=2\,V\,D\,V
.

(the polar rate)
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}
, we first recall the following identity (cf.
[
16
]
)

Lemma 2.5
.

If
F=V\,R
is the left polar decomposition of
F
and
\Omega=\dot{R}\,R^{T}
, then

\displaystyle(D+W-\Omega)\,B+B\,(D-W+\Omega)=L\,B+B\,L^{T}-\Omega\,B+B\,\Omega=2\,V\,D\,V.

(2.40)

Proof.

We begin by pointing out the additional identities

\displaystyle V=V^{T}\quad\implies\quad\dot{V}=\dot{V}^{T}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad R\,R^{T}={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\quad\implies\quad\Omega^{T}=-\Omega,

(2.41)

where the last implication holds since
\Omega+\Omega^{T}=\dot{R}\,R^{T}+R\,\dot{R}^{T}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[R\,R^{T}]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}]=0
.
Now we have

\displaystyle L=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[V\,R]\,(V\,R)^{-1}=(\dot{V}\,R+V\,\dot{R})\,R^{-1}\,V^{-1}=\dot{V}\,V^{-1}+V\,\dot{R}\,R^{-1}\,V^{-1}

(2.42)

which we premultiply and postmultiply by
V
to obtain (with
R^{T}=R^{-1}
and
B=V^{2}
)

\displaystyle V\,L\,V=V\,\dot{V}+V^{2}\,\dot{R}\,R^{-1}=V\,\dot{V}+B\,\Omega.

(2.43)

In the next step we transpose (
2.43
) to obtain (with
B=B^{T}
)

\displaystyle V\,L^{T}\,V=\dot{V}^{T}\,V^{T}+\Omega^{T}\,B^{T}=\dot{V}\,V-\Omega\,B.

(2.44)

Adding (
2.43
) and (
2.44
) then yields (with
L+L^{T}=2\,D
)

\displaystyle 2\,V\,D\,V=V\,\dot{V}+\dot{V}\,V+B\,\Omega-\Omega\,B.

(2.45)

Hence, it remains to show that

L\,B+B\,L^{T}=(L\,B)+(L\,B)^{T}=V\,\dot{V}+\dot{V}\,V.

But with (
2.42
) and
B=V^{2}
it follows that

L\,B=(\dot{V}\,V^{-1}+V\,\Omega\,V^{-1})\,V^{2}=\dot{V}\,V+V\,\Omega\,V,

which implies

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt(L\,B)+(L\,B)^{T}

\displaystyle=(\dot{V}\,V+V\,\Omega\,V)+(\dot{V}\,V)^{T}+(V\,\Omega\,V)^{T}=\dot{V}\,V+V^{T}\,\dot{V}^{T}+V\,\Omega\,V-V\,\Omega\,V=\dot{V}\,V+V\,\dot{V},

(2.46)

and the claim follows.
∎

Knowing that (
2.40
) holds, we can substitute

\displaystyle L\,B+B\,L^{T}=2\,V\,D\,V+\Omega\,B-B\,\Omega

(2.47)

into (
2.31
) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\dot{B}]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[L\,B+B\,L^{T}]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[2\,V\,D\,V-B\,\Omega+\Omega\,B]

(2.48)

\displaystyle=2\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[V\,D\,V]+\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\Omega\,B-B\,\Omega].

Next we choose
Q(0)={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
and
\dot{Q}(0)=\Omega
in (
2.36
) and use
R\,R^{T}={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
, which implies
\Omega^{T}=-\Omega
. This leads to

\boxed{\Omega\,\sigma-\sigma\,\Omega=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\Omega\,B-B\,\Omega],}

(2.49)

which can be inserted in (
2.48
) to obtain the formula

\displaystyle\boxed{\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega-\Omega\,\sigma=2\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[V\,D\,V]=\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(V).D\,.}

(2.50)

Since
V^{2}=B=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)
in the case of invertible
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
, the latter can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=2\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)).[\sqrt{\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)}\,D\sqrt{\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)}]\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\textnormal{GN}}(\sigma).D\,,

(2.51)

determining the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
for the Green-Naghdi rate.

Remark 2.6
.

Note again that whenever we speak about the induced fourth order tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
, we need to require invertibility.
19
19

19

Saccomandi and Rajagopal put forward
[
109
]
: “There are several shortcomings with respect to the manner in which constitutive relations are usually specified currently, both from a philosophical standpoint and more pragmatic considerations. From the philosophical standpoint, expressing the stress in terms of kinematical variables turns causality on its head, as forces and stresses are the causes, and the kinematics is the effect. It makes much more sense to describe kinematics in terms of the stresses and/or their derivatives.” Not much experience is available in directly prescribing the inverted law
B=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)
apart from those restrictions that isotropy dictates, see
[
97
,
98
,
112
]
.

of
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
(since otherwise we would only have a tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(B)
). Thus, whenever the tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
appears, we silently assume that the underlying constitutive law
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
is invertible
20
20

20

Otherwise, the stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(B)
does depend explicitly on
B=FF^{T}
instead of only the Cauchy stress
\sigma
. The Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
itself is independent of the adopted reference configuration, while
B
involves the computation of the deformation gradient
F=\mathrm{D}\varphi
, defined with respect to the adopted reference configuration which is arbitrary. The independence of the reference configuration gives additional motivation for insisting on the invertibility of
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
, at least as regards the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
. Of course, assuming
\sigma=\sigma(B)
will always involve the reference configuration.

Remark 2.7

(
One formula to rule them all…
)
.

Consider an
arbitrary corotational derivative
(not necessarily objective) with spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)
for an isotropic function
\sigma=\sigma(B)
, i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma+\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}.

(2.52)

Then the equation (
2.36
) can be applied to any choice of
Q(t)
with
Q(0)={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},\;\dot{Q}(0)=\Omega^{\circ}
to obtain the general relation (remaining true for isotropic tensor functions
\overline{\sigma}\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
)

\boxed{\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma(B)-\sigma(B)\,\Omega^{\circ}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\Omega^{\circ}\,B-B\,\Omega^{\circ}],}

(2.53)

alternatively expressed via the Lie-bracket
[A,B]=A\,B-B\,A
as

\boxed{[\Omega^{\circ},\sigma(B)]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\Omega^{\circ},B].}

(2.54)

This formula represents the crucial identity used in Section
4.1.3
to prove a chain rule for arbitrary corotational derivatives.

Remark 2.8
.

Due to its importance for our exposition, a slightly different approach to also obtain (
2.53
) is given by the following idea thanks to a discussion with Sergey N. Korobeynikov:

We begin again with the relations (
2.32
) and (
2.36
), i.e.

\displaystyle Q\,[\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).H]\,Q^{T}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[Q\,H\,Q^{T}]

(2.55)

and

\displaystyle\dot{Q}\,\sigma(B)\,Q^{T}+Q\,\sigma(B)\,\dot{Q}^{T}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(Q\,B\,Q^{T}).[\dot{Q}\,B\,Q^{T}+Q\,B\,\dot{Q}^{T}].

(2.56)

Next, we define
H
as

\displaystyle Q\,H\,Q^{T}=\dot{Q}\,B\,Q^{T}+Q\,B\,\dot{Q}^{T}\qquad\implies\qquad H=Q^{T}\,\dot{Q}\,B+B\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,Q

(2.57)

and insert this in (
2.55
) which afterwards can be inserted into (
2.56
) to obtain

\displaystyle\dot{Q}\,\sigma(B)\,Q^{T}+Q\,\sigma(B)\,\dot{Q}^{T}=Q\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[Q^{T}\,\dot{Q}\,B+B\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,Q]\,Q^{T}\,.

(2.58)

Multiplying this equation by
Q^{T}
from the left and
Q
from the right results in

\displaystyle Q^{T}\,\dot{Q}\,\sigma(B)+\sigma(B)\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,Q=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[Q^{T}\,\dot{Q}\,B+B\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,Q]

(2.59)

so that, by defining the skew-symmetric spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q^{T}\,\dot{Q}
, we finally obtain (
2.53
).

2.2
Positive definiteness of the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
: the corotational stability postulate

[formula]
[formula]
stiffening
softening
corotational
stability
[formula]
corotational
instability
[formula]
“two intrinsic
failure conditions” :
but
[formula]
or
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 1:
Illustration of our interpretation of Drucker’s stability inequality for isotropic nonlinear elasticity as
“corotational stability postulate”
. In the
\sigma
-softening regime we expect convergence problems and non-well-posedness.

To motivate the meaningfulness of the analysis of the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
, we take a look at isotropic linear elasticity, i.e. we consider the linear elastic law

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\sym\,\mathrm{D}u\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma_{\lin}(\varepsilon)=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.\varepsilon.

(2.60)

Here, stability is expressed as the
positive definiteness
of
\mathbb{C}^{\iso}\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
:

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.\varepsilon,\varepsilon\rangle\geq c^{+}\,\lVert\varepsilon\rVert^{2}

(2.61)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mu>0,\quad 2\,\mu+3\,\lambda>0.

This is almost equivalent to the
invertibility
of
\varepsilon\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma_{\lin}(\varepsilon)
, for which we note that

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{C}^{\rm iso}\neq 0\iff\mu\neq 0,\ \ 2\mu+3\lambda\neq 0.

(2.62)

The linear constitutive law can also be written in rate-format as

\displaystyle\dot{\sigma}=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.\dot{\varepsilon}\,.

(2.63)

Then the positive definiteness of
\mathbb{C}^{\iso}
is equivalently described through

\displaystyle\langle\dot{\sigma},\dot{\varepsilon}\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,\dot{\varepsilon}\in\Sym(3),\;\dot{\varepsilon}\neq 0.

(2.64)

We refer to (
2.61
) and (
2.64
) as
linear stability postulate
. In isotropic linear elasticity, the linear stability postulate is equivalent to the convexity of the elastic energy
\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.\varepsilon,\varepsilon\rangle
in the displacement gradient
\mathrm{D}u
which is the same as positivity of the second order work (see also the Appendix
A.6
).

Next, consider an isotropic tensor function
\varepsilon\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(\varepsilon)
, not necessarily linear in
\varepsilon
, and impose

\displaystyle\langle\dot{\sigma},\dot{\varepsilon}\rangle>0\quad\iff

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\sigma(\varepsilon(t))],\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\varepsilon(t)]\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\varepsilon(t)]\neq 0

(2.65)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}\sigma(\varepsilon(t)).\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\varepsilon(t)],\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\varepsilon(t)]\rangle>0\iff\quad\sym\mathrm{D}\sigma(\varepsilon(t))\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\langle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(\varepsilon),\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varepsilon\rangle>0\qquad\textnormal{classical ``{Drucker-stability}'' for linearized kinematics}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\langle\sigma(\varepsilon_{1})-\sigma(\varepsilon_{2}),\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}\rangle>0\qquad\implies\qquad\varepsilon\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(\varepsilon)\quad\textnormal{is invertible.}

Now, the rate-form stability requirement (
2.64
) can naturally be generalized to the hypo-elastic setting by replacing the material time derivative of
\sigma
by the corotational Zaremba-Jaumann rate and the time derivative of
\varepsilon
by the Eulerian strain rate, i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\sigma]\quad\textnormal{by}\quad\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\varepsilon]\quad\textnormal{by}\quad D=\sym\,\mathrm{D}v,

(2.66)

so that (
2.64
) turns into

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}.

(2.67)

In analogy to (
2.64
) we call (
2.67
) the
“corotational stability postulate” CSP
. It is evident that (
2.67
) is frame-indifferent, while the “second order work”
\langle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\sigma],D\rangle
is not, cf. Appendix
A.6
. Also note that CSP is in general not related to a convexity type condition (e.g. rank-one convexity) in
F=\mathrm{D}\varphi
.

Since by (
2.16
) we have the relation
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D
, the requirement (
2.67
) turns further into the positive definiteness condition for the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0\quad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\langle\sym\,\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0\quad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}

(2.68)

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\sym\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6).

Note that since
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
is not necessarily major symmetric (i.e. self adjoint), we obtain only the positive definiteness of the symmetric part of
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
, i.e.
\sym\,\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
.

2.3
Invertibility of the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)

In Section
2.1
we have derived the formulas for the induced tangent stiffness tensors
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
under the assumption that
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
is invertible, i.e. there is a function
\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)
with
B=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)
for every
B\in\Sym^{++}(3)
. Under this assumption it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\rm ZJ}(\sigma).D=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,D+D\,B]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathbb{H}^{\rm GN}(\sigma).D=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[2\,V\,D\,V].

(2.69)

Let us first check, as a minimal requirement usually made (cf. e.g. Ericksen
[
19
]
or Romano
[
105
]
), under which condition on the given constitutive law the induced tangent stiffness tensors
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
will be invertible, considered as
6\times 6
matrices so that one could also speak about the induced compliance tensor
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{H}^{-1}
. In this respect we can formulate the

Proposition 2.9

(Invertibility of
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
)
.

The induced tangent stiffness tensors
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
are invertible if and only if
\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)
is invertible, i.e.

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{r}\det\mathbb{H}^{\rm ZJ}(\sigma)\neq 0\\
\det\mathbb{H}^{\rm GN}(\sigma)\neq 0\end{array}\right\}\quad\iff\quad\det\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)\neq 0\qquad\iff\qquad\det\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\neq 0.

Proof.

It is well-known (cf. Sidoroff
[
113
]
or Scheidler
[
111
]
) that the linear mappings

\displaystyle D\mapstochar\rightarrow B\,D+D\,B\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad D\mapstochar\rightarrow V\,D\,V

(2.72)

are invertible for
V^{2}=B\in\Sym^{++}(3)
. Thus, from the representation of
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
in (
2.69
) it is clear that
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
are invertible if and only if
\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)
is invertible. Finally, since
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\log B
is invertible and
\det\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B>0
(cf. Appendix
A.27
), the same holds true if and only if
\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
is invertible, by the standard chain rule.
∎

The different conditions
\det\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)>0
and
\sym\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
can be used as novel stability criteria, see Figure
1
and compare with
[
48
]
. In general, we know that

\displaystyle\sym\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\quad

\displaystyle\implies\quad\langle(\sym\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)).D,D\rangle=\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0\quad\forall D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}

(2.73)

\displaystyle\implies\quad\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}\quad\forall D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}\quad\implies\quad\det\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\neq 0.

In this respect it is useful to recall the Bendixson-inequality
[
7
]

\lambda_{\min}(\sym\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ})\leq\textnormal{Re}(\lambda_{i}(\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}))
showing that the positive definiteness of
\sym\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}
implies
\det\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)>0
in general. A detailed exposition of these stability requirements for nonlinear elasticity will be pursued in a future contribution.

Remark 2.10
.

It is useful to remember that the global invertibility of
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B),\;\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
does not already imply that
\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)
is invertible everywhere, as the simple one-dimensional example

f\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\;f(t)=t^{3}
demonstrates:

f
is strictly monotone throughout and globally invertible, but
\mathrm{D}f(0)=0
.

3
Corotational stability results and conjectures

We recall the main result from
[
14
]
as it is the starting point for the upcoming considerations.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\;B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
be the constitutive expression of the Cauchy stress tensor for isotropic nonlinear elasticity. Assume that
\sigma\in C^{1}(\Sym^{++}(3),\Sym(3))
. Then the following equivalence holds:

\displaystyle 0\;<\;\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,D+D\,B],D\rangle

(3.1)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]

\displaystyle=\mathrlap{\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle}\hphantom{\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]}\qquad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathrlap{B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\quad\textnormal{is strongly monotone in $\log B$ (TSTS-M${}^{++}$)}}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\,,}

which implies

\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{2}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle>0\qquad\quad\forall\,B_{1}\neq B_{2},\quad B_{1},B_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3)\,.

(3.2)

Remark 3.2
.

The novel point of departure is to view the CSP characterization now from the angle of properties of the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,D+D\,B]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]
, giving directly algebraic conditions on the Cauchy stress
\sigma
.

Remark 3.3
.

The strong Hilbert-monotonicity reads

\displaystyle\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\qquad\iff\qquad\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\,,

(3.3)

which can be equivalently expressed in principal Cauchy stresses (see the Appendix, Remark
A.5
)

\displaystyle\forall\,(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}:\qquad\Lambda_{ij}=\sym\,\frac{\partial\widehat{\sigma}_{i}(\log\lambda_{1},\log\lambda_{2},\log\lambda_{3})}{\partial\log\lambda_{j}}\,\in\Sym^{++}(3).

(3.4)

For the proof we refer to
[
14
]
.

It is important to point out that strict Hilbert monotonicity of
\sigma(V)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)
in
V
and in
\log V
(in
B
and in
\log B
), respectively, are two completely unrelated characteristics of a constitutive Cauchy law
V\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(V)
. In fact, neither implies the other, as shown explicitly in Appendix
A.5
.

3.1
Some instructive examples

Recall from formula (
2.38
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-W\,\sigma+\sigma\,W=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D

(3.5)

and from (
2.50
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega-\Omega\,\sigma=2\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[V\,D\,V]=\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D

(3.6)

for the Zaremba-Jaumann and the Green-Naghdi derivative, respectively.

Example 3.4
.

We reconsider the stress response
\sigma(B)=\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})
, which is a primary matrix function of
B
, for
\mu>0
, so that
\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).H=\mu\,H
. Then, denoting by
\lambda_{\min}
the smallest eigenvalue of its argument
21
21

21

Note that the relation
\lambda_{\min}(B)=\lambda_{\min}^{2}(V)
holds.

,
we obtain
22
22

22

Note carefully (cf. Remark
3.6
) that the mapping
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)=\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})
is not invertible in this case and that
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
according to (
3.7
) and (
3.7
) only corresponds to the Cauchy-elastic constitutive law if
\sigma
is in the range of the stress response mapping.

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle=\mu\,\langle B\,D+D\,B,D\rangle=2\,\mu\,\langle D\,B,D\rangle\geq 2\,\mu\,\lambda_{\rm min}(B)\,\|D\|^{2}>0

(3.7)

as well as

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D,D\rangle=2\,\mu\,\langle[V\,D\,V],D\rangle=2\,\mu\,\langle V\,D\,V,D\rangle\geq 2\,\mu\,\lambda_{\min}^{2}(V)\,\lVert D\rVert^{2}>0\,,

(3.8)

showing (directly) that for both rates the corotational stability postulate (CSP) is satisfied, which is equivalent to strong monotonicity of
\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
as shown in Theorem
3.1
. Independently, we see that
\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
is also strictly Hilbert-monotone in
\log B
, by observing that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{2})

\displaystyle,\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle

(3.9)

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma(B_{1})-\sigma(B_{2}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle=\mu\,\langle B_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-(B_{2}-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mu\,\langle B_{1}-B_{2},\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle=\mu\,\langle\log B_{1}-\log B_{2},B_{1}-B_{2}\rangle>0,

since
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\log B
is a strongly monotone primary matrix function, as shown in Lemma
A.27
in the Appendix.

Example 3.5
.

We next show positive definiteness of the induced tangent stiffness tensors
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma)
for the constitutive Cauchy-elastic law (a non-primary isotropic matrix function)

\displaystyle\sigma(B)=\frac{\mu}{2}(B-B^{-1})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\,\textnormal{tr}(\log B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

(3.10)

Therefore, we first calculate
\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).H
, by making use of the equality
\mathrm{D}_{B}[B^{-1}]=-B^{-1}\,H\,B
, which leads to

\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).H=\frac{\mu}{2}(H+B^{-1}\,H\,B^{-1})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\langle B^{-1},H\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

(3.11)

Inserting the increment
H=[B\,D+D\,B]
(not to be confounded with
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,D+D\,B]=\frac{\mu}{2}\,(B\,D+D\,B+B^{-1}\,D+D\,B^{-1})+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

(3.12)

and similarly, for
H=[V\,D\,V]
with
\langle B^{-1},VDV\rangle=\langle VB^{-1}V,D\rangle=\textnormal{tr}(D)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D=2\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[V\,D\,V]=\mu\,(V\,D\,V+V^{-1}\,D\,V^{-1})+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

(3.13)

From this we find

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{2}\,(2\,\langle B\,D,D\rangle+2\,\langle B^{-1}\,D,D\rangle)+\lambda\,\langle\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},D\rangle

(3.14)

\displaystyle=\mu\,(\langle B\,D,D\rangle+\langle B^{-1}\,D,D\rangle)+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}^{2}(D)

\displaystyle\geq\mu\,\left(\lambda_{\min}(B)+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\max}(B)}\right)\,\lVert D\rVert^{2}+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}^{2}(D)\geq c^{+}(\mu,\lambda)\,\lVert D\rVert^{2}

as well as

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D,D\rangle

\displaystyle=\mu\,\langle V\,D\,V,D\rangle+\mu\,\langle V^{-1}\,D\,V^{-1},D\rangle+\lambda\,\langle{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},D\rangle\,\textnormal{tr}(D)

(3.15)

\displaystyle\geq\mu\,(\lambda_{\min}^{2}(V)\,\lVert D\rVert^{2}+\lambda_{\min}^{2}(V^{-1})\,\lVert D\rVert^{2})+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}^{2}(D)\,,

which are both non-negative if we assume
\mu,\lambda\geq 0
, thus showing that CSP is fulfilled and
\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
is strongly monotone in
\log B
according to Theorem
3.1
.

Dividing (
3.10
) into three parts

\displaystyle\sigma=\frac{\mu}{2}(B-B^{-1})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\,\textnormal{tr}(\log B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\,\smash{\underbrace{\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\sigma_{1}}+\underbrace{\mu\,({\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-B^{-1})}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\sigma_{2}}+\underbrace{\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(\log B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\sigma_{3}}}\,\right\}\,,\vphantom{\underbrace{\mu\,({\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-B^{-1})}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\sigma_{2}}+\underbrace{\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(\log B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\sigma_{3}}}

(3.16)

we can easily check (directly) that
\sigma
in (
3.16
) is monotone as a function of
\log B
, where we suppose that
\mu,\,\lambda>0
. The term
B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
is monotone in
\log B
as shown in Example
3.4
. Furthermore, it is easy to see that
\textnormal{tr}(\log B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
is monotone in
\log B
. It remains to check the term
\sigma=\frac{1}{\mu}\sigma_{2}={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-B^{-1}
. We find

\displaystyle\langle\sigma(B_{1})-\sigma(B_{2})

\displaystyle,\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle

(3.17)

\displaystyle=\langle{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-B_{1}^{-1}-({\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-B_{2}^{-1}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle=\langle-B_{1}^{-1}-(-B_{2}^{-1}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-B_{1}^{-1}+B_{2}^{-1},-\log B_{1}^{-1}-(-\log B_{2}^{-1})\rangle=\langle B_{2}^{-1}-B_{1}^{-1},\log B_{2}^{-1}-\log B_{1}^{-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\log X-\log Y,X-Y\rangle\,>\,0\,,\quad X=B_{2}^{-1},\quad Y=B_{1}^{-1},

since
\log
is strongly monotone in its argument, showing independently that
\sigma
given by (
3.16
) is strictly Hilbert-monotone in
\log B
.

Remark 3.6
.

Let us consider, for the previous two examples, the corresponding one-dimensional Cauchy stresses. On the one hand we have
\sigma_{a}
, shown in Figure
3
, corresponding to the three-dimensional Cauchy-elastic (but not hyperelastic) constitutive law
\sigma_{\alpha}
, with

\displaystyle\sigma_{a}(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{1}{2}(\lambda^{2}-1),\quad\qquad\sigma_{\alpha}(B)=\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})=2\mu\,\sym\mathrm{D}u+\textnormal{h.o.t.}=2\mu\,\varepsilon+\textnormal{h.o.t.},

(3.18)

and on the other hand the one-dimensional Cauchy stress
\sigma_{b}
(cf. Figure
3
) with the corresponding three-dimensional counterpart
\sigma_{\beta}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{1}{5}(\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{-2}+\log\lambda)\,,\quad\qquad\sigma_{\beta}(B)=\frac{\mu}{2}(B-B^{-1})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\,\textnormal{tr}(\log B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,,

(3.19)

respectively. In the one-dimensional setting, both give rise to monotone functions
\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(\lambda)
.
23
23

23

Both constitutive laws are isotropic and objective, therefore they can be used, in principle, for small strain and large rotations. However, only
\sigma_{b}
is suitable for large strains, since
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma_{b}(B)
is invertible while
\sigma_{a}
does not respond properly for extreme stretches
B\rightarrow 0
.

[formula]
[formula]

Figure 2:
Picture of the monotone Cauchy stress
\sigma_{a}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda^{2}-1)
.

[formula]
[formula]

Figure 3:
Monotone and bijective Cauchy stress
\sigma_{b}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{5}(\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{-2}+\log\lambda)
. Idealized nonlinear elastic response: “
stress increases with strain
”.

Figure
3
demonstrates that for the choice
\sigma=\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})
, the stress response is not surjective; in particular, there does not exist any
B\in\Sym(3)^{++}
such that
\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})=\sigma
if
\sigma_{i}\leq-\mu
for any eigenvalue
\sigma_{i}
of
\sigma
. Therefore, the relation

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D=\mu\,(D\,B+B\,D)

(3.20)

is only well-defined for
\sigma
in the range of the stress response mapping, since otherwise, the left Cauchy-Green tensor
B
on the right-hand side is not well defined.
However, we may still talk about
conditional positive definiteness
of
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
, i.e.
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
is positive definite whenever there is a function
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym^{++}(3)
such that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle=\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)).D,D\rangle=\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(B).D,D\rangle\qquad\textnormal{for}\qquad B=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}(3)\,.

(3.21)

Note also that the relation

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D=2\,\mu\,D+D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D\,,

(3.22)

which is equivalent to (
3.20
) for
\sigma=\mu\,(B-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})
if such a
B\in\Sym^{++}(3)
exists, properly defines a stiffness tensor for any
\sigma\in\Sym(3)
and thus (globally) constitutes a hypoelastic law.

An example for an
invertible
Cauchy stress response
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
that generates an induced fourth order tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
that is
not positive definite
throughout is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\NH}(F)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{2}\,\left(\frac{\lVert F\rVert^{2}}{(\det F)^{\frac{2}{3}}}-3\right)+\kappa\,\mathrm{e}^{(\log\det F)^{2}},

(3.23)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\NH}(B)

\displaystyle=\mu\,(\det B)^{-\frac{5}{6}}\,\dev_{3}B+\kappa\,(\det B)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(\log\det B)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log\det B)^{2}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,,

as discussed in Appendix
A.4
.

3.2
Conjectures - a far reaching generality for the CSP

In the previous examples, we observe that whenever
\log B\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
is monotone, the equivalence CSP
\iff
TSTS-M
++
holds not only for the corotational Zaremba-Jaumann rate, but also for the corotational Green-Naghdi rate. The authors have not yet found any isotropic Cauchy-elastic law for which this observation is not true, giving reason to propose the following conjecture (compare Theorem
3.1
).

Conjecture 3.7

(Corotational stability for the Green-Naghdi rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}
)
.

Let
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\;B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
be the constitutive expression of the Cauchy stress tensor for isotropic nonlinear elasticity. Assume that
\sigma\in C^{1}(\Sym^{++}(3),\Sym(3))
. Then the following equivalence holds:

\displaystyle 0\;<\;\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[2\,VD\,V],D\rangle

(3.24)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[2\,V\,D\,V]

\displaystyle=\mathrlap{\langle\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D,D\rangle}\hphantom{\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[2\,V\,D\,V]}\qquad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathrlap{B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\quad\textnormal{is strongly monotone in $\log B$ (TSTS-M${}^{++}$)}}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\,,}

implying

\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{2}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle>0\qquad\quad\forall\,B_{1}\neq B_{2},\quad B_{1},B_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3).

(3.25)

In fact, we suspect that the equivalence between CSP and TSTS-M
++
may even hold for a larger class of objective corotational rates
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
, the
positive corotational rates
(cf. Section
4.3
, Definitions
4.13
and
4.15
). For an in-depth analysis of positive corotational rates we refer to the development in
[
80
]
.

Conjecture 3.8

(Corotational stability for positive corotational rates
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
)
.

Let
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\;B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
be the constitutive expression of the Cauchy stress tensor for isotropic nonlinear elasticity. Assume that
\sigma\in C^{1}(\Sym^{++}(3),\Sym(3))
. Let
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
