belong to the subclass of positive, objective and corotational derivatives, i.e. for the fourth order stiffness tensor
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)
in

\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B).D

(3.26)

we require
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
. Then the following equivalence holds:

\displaystyle 0\;<\;\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right],D\rangle

(3.27)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrlap{\langle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle}\hphantom{\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]}\qquad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathrlap{B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\quad\textnormal{is strongly monotone in $\log B$ (TSTS-M${}^{++}$)}}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\,,}

implying

\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{2}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle>0\qquad\quad\forall\,B_{1}\neq B_{2},\quad B_{1},B_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3).

(3.28)

4
The CSP from a matrix-analysis viewpoint

It is clear that showing the far reaching generalizations in Conjecture
3.8
cannot be undertaken by mimicking the technical calculations in principal Lagrangean axis given in
[
14
]
. To this aim, we need a new perspective, as introduced in the following section.

4.1
Chain rule formula for arbitrary corotational rates

In order to further investigate the relation between CSP and TSTS-M
++
for the logarithmic rate, we derive here an important and useful chain rule formula for an arbitrary corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
.

4.1.1
Chain rule formula for primary matrix functions

In
[
133
, p. 19, Theorem 2]
,
[
26
, p. 7]
,
[
53
, p. 1066, Theorem 2.3]
and
[
86
, p. 10, Lemma 1]
, a chain rule like formula for corotational rates acting on
primary matrix functions
(cf. Appendix
A.1
)
is supplied. More precisely, they prove the following.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\sigma=\sigma(B)\in C^{1}(\Sym^{++}(3),\Sym(3))
be a primary matrix function (e.g.
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\log B
) and consider an arbitrary corotational rate

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(B)]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(B)]+\sigma(B)\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma(B)

(4.1)

defined by any spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)
. Then
\sigma(B)
obeys the chain rule

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(B)]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right].

(4.2)

Proposition
4.1
constitutes a partial chain rule result. Indeed, although being true for
any corotational rate
, it is
restricted to isotropic matrix functions that are primary matrix functions
. Nevertheless, we can e.g. already calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[V^{2}]=\mathrm{D}_{V}[V^{2}].\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[V]=V\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[V]+\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[V]\,V

(4.3)

so that
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[V]
is uniquely determined by
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]
since
V\in\Sym^{++}(3)
(cf.
[
86
, eq. 3.5b]
) and
X\mapstochar\rightarrow X\,V+V\,X
is invertible for
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[V]=X\in\Sym(3)
.

Next, we generalize Proposition
4.1
by first
directly
proving a chain rule formula for the corotational Zaremba-Jaumann rate and the corotational Green-Naghdi rate for
all isotropic tensor functions

\sigma(B)
, which will then be generalized to a
chain rule
for an
arbitrary corotational rate

\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
and all
isotropic tensor functions

\sigma(B)
.

4.1.2
Chain rule formulas for the corotational Zaremba-Jaumann and Green-Naghdi rates

We have seen in Section
2.1
that the relations

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[2\,V\,D\,V]

(4.4)

hold. For the choice
\sigma(B)=B
this yields
24
24

24

Recall that (
4.5
) can also directly be calculated. Surely, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}B+B\,W-W\,B=L\,B+B\,L^{T}+B\,W-W\,B=(D+W)\,B+B\,(D-W)+B\,W-W\,B=D\,B+B\,D.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\mathrm{D}_{B}B.[D\,B+B\,D]=\textnormal{id}.[D\,B+B\,D]=D\,B+B\,D

(4.5)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\mathrm{D}_{B}B.[2\,V\,D\,V]=\textnormal{id}.[2\,V\,D\,V]=2\,V\,D\,V,

(4.6)

implying that (
4.4
) can formally be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right].

(4.7)

If we assume that
\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
, application of the standard chain rule implies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\{\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\}.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]

(4.8)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\{\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\}.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right].

(4.9)

Additionally, applying formula (
4.4
) to the (primary matrix) function
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\log B
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right],

(4.10)

so that by using the standard chain rule again we can also write

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,D+D\,B]

(4.11)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B]\right],

where obviously the same holds true for the Green-Naghdi rate.

The transformation
\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
can be replaced by an arbitrary transformation of the type
\sigma(B)=\widetilde{\sigma}(g(B))
with a differentiable mapping
B\mapstochar\rightarrow g(B)
(neither
\widetilde{\sigma}
nor
g
need be monotone), yielding

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widetilde{\sigma}(g(B))]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\{\widetilde{\sigma}(g(B))\}.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{g(B)}\widetilde{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}g(B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{g(B)}\widetilde{\sigma}.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[g(B)]\right]

(4.12)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widetilde{\sigma}(g(B))]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\{\widetilde{\sigma}(g(B))\}.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{g(B)}\widetilde{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}g(B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{g(B)}\widetilde{\sigma}.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[g(B)]\right]

(4.13)

instead.

Let us independently check the validity of the equation (
4.8
) for the Zaremba-Jaumann and (
4.9
) for the Green-Naghdi derivative by a direct calculation. To this end, let
R\in{\rm O}(3)
be the orthogonal rotation tensor with
\dot{R}\,R^{T}=\Omega
and let
Q^{W}=Q=Q(t)\in{\rm O}(3)
be a matrix with
\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}=W
. Then we can express the corotational rates as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\,[Q^{T}\,\sigma\,Q]\,Q^{T}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=R\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\,[R^{T}\,\sigma\,R]\,R^{T}\,.

(4.14)

Since the upcoming calculation is the same for either rate (Zaremba-Jaumann with rotation matrix
Q
and Green-Naghdi with matrix
R
), we will only carry it out for the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative. Writing
\widehat{\sigma}=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
and recalling
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}B=L\,B+B\,L^{T}
we obtain together with the chain rule for the material derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}]

\displaystyle=Q\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,Q\,Q^{T}+Q\,Q^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]

(4.15)

\displaystyle=Q\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,Q\,Q^{T}+Q\,Q^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}B\right]

\displaystyle=W^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}+\widehat{\sigma}\,W+\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B}_{\mathrm{D}_{B}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]}.[L\,B+B\,L^{T}]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\sigma}\,W-W\,\widehat{\sigma}+\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{B}\widehat{\sigma}.[W\,B-B\,W]}_{\overset{\eqref{eqratetype4}}{=}W\,\widehat{\sigma}-\widehat{\sigma}W}+\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[D\,B+B\,D]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[D\,B+B\,D]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right].

4.1.3
Chain rule like formula for an arbitrary corotational rate

We now generalize both Proposition
4.1
and the above observations on the Zaremba-Jaumann and Green-Naghdi rates to a chain rule formula for arbitrary corotational rates and isotropic (not necessarily primary) matrix functions.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
be an arbitrary corotational rate with spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)
and an isotropic, differentiable function
\sigma=\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
. Then the chain rule

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B]

(4.16)

holds

Proof.

Recall the formula

\boxed{\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma(B)-\sigma(B)\,\Omega^{\circ}=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\Omega^{\circ}\,B-B\,\Omega^{\circ}],}

(4.17)

derived in Remark
2.7
, alternatively expressed as

\boxed{[\Omega^{\circ},\sigma(B)]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\Omega^{\circ},B]}

(4.18)

for an arbitrary corotational derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma+\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+[\sigma,\Omega^{\circ}]

(4.19)

with spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)
and an isotropic, differentiable function
\sigma=\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
. Repeating the calculations of (
4.15
) shows (with
\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}=\Omega^{\circ}
and
\widehat{\sigma}=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}]

\displaystyle=Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,Q]\,Q^{T}=Q\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,Q\,Q^{T}+Q\,Q^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]

(4.20)

\displaystyle=Q\,\dot{Q}^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,Q\,Q^{T}+Q\,Q^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}\,\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}B\right]

\displaystyle=(\Omega^{\circ})^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}+\widehat{\sigma}\,\Omega^{\circ}+\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B}_{\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)}.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]+\Omega^{\circ}\,B-B\,\Omega^{\circ}\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma+\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma.[\Omega^{\circ}\,B-B\,\Omega^{\circ}]}_{\overset{\eqref{eqboxedrateeq}}{=}\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma-\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}}}_{=\,0}+\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}B\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}.\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B],

where in
\eqref{eqallglong1}_{1}
we used the chain rule for the material derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]
, in
\eqref{eqallglong1}_{2}
that
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}B=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]+\Omega^{\circ}\,B-B\,\Omega^{\circ}
and in
\eqref{eqallglong1}_{4}
that
\log B
is a primary matrix function which admits a chain rule due to Proposition
4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

The last equation of (
4.20
) also shows that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B]^{-1}.\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B],

(4.21)

which extends results by Norris
[
86
, Lemma 2]
from primary matrix functions to arbitrary isotropic tensor functions.

We conjecture that the corotational stress rates are, in fact, the only objective rates that satisfy a classical chain rule.

Conjecture 4.4

(Chain rule and corotational rates)
.

Let
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}
be any objective rate. Assume that for all differentiable isotropic tensor functions
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\,B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
and all
B\in\Sym^{++}(3)
we have the chain rule

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(B)]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B].

(4.22)

Then
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}
is corotational.

Remark 4.5

(Leibniz-rule for derivations)
.

Any objective derivative can be seen as a covariant derivative
[
52
]
which automatically satisfies the Leibniz rule of differentiation (cf.
[
52
, p. 10]
). This means for
f\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
and differentiable
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3),\,B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[f(t)\,\sigma(t)]=f(t)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(t)]+f^{\prime}(t)\,\sigma(t).

(4.23)

We may check the latter for a general corotational rate:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[f\,\sigma]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[f\,\sigma]+(f\,\sigma)\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,(f\,\sigma)

(4.24)

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}\,\sigma+f\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+f\,(\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma)=f\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+f^{\prime}\,\sigma.

Proposition 4.6

(Product rule for corotational rates)
.

For two isotropic and differentiable tensor functions
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}:\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
we have the product rule in the form

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}(B)\,\sigma_{2}(B)]=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}(B)]\,\sigma_{2}(B)+\sigma_{1}(B)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}(B)]\,.

(4.25)

Proof.

The proof follows easily by application of the chain rule,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}]\overset{\textnormal{chain rule}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\mathrm{D}_{B}[\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}].\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\overset{\text{product rule}}{=}[\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma_{2}].\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]

(4.26)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma_{1}.\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right)\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\left(\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma_{2}.\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right)\overset{\textnormal{chain rule}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]\,,

or alternatively by direct computation (independent of the chain rule):

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}]+\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]+\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}\,\Omega^{\circ}-\sigma_{1}\,\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma_{2}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}

(4.27)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]+\sigma_{1}\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma_{1}\right)\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\left(\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]+\sigma_{2}\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma_{2}\right)=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]\,.\qed

Remark 4.7

(Leibniz and product rules for the Truesdell rate)
.

Recalling the definition of the Truesdell rate (cf.
[
123
, eq. 3]
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-L\,\sigma-\sigma\,L^{T}+\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,,

(4.28)

we observe that the Truesdell rate satisfies the Leibniz rule, i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[f(t)\,\sigma]

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}\,\sigma+f\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-L\,(f\,\sigma)-(f\,\sigma)\,L^{T}+(f\,\sigma)\,\textnormal{tr}(D)

(4.29)

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}\,\sigma+f\,\left(\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-L\,\sigma-\sigma\,L^{T}+\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\right)=f^{\prime}\,\sigma+f\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,.

However,
the product rule is not satisfied for the Truesdell rate
since

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]-L\,(\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2})-(\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2})\,L^{T}+(\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2})\,\textnormal{tr}(D)

(4.30)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]+\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]\,\sigma_{2}-L\,\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,L\,\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]+\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,L\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,L^{T}\,\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{2}]+\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{1}]\,\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1}\,\left(2\,D-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\right)\,\sigma_{2}.

This underlines again our choice for using only corotational rates
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
(cf.
[
80
]
).

Remark 4.8
.

The previously proven chain- and product rules are correct even if
\sigma
is not symmetric and
\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}\neq\sigma_{2}\,\sigma_{1}
, since symmetry is not required in any of the proofs.

Remark 4.9

(Perfect elastic fluid)
.

For a perfect elastic fluid (cf.
[
64
, p. 10]
) and an arbitrary corotational rate described by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma+\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}

(4.31)

with the spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)
, we already see that the corotational derivative reduces to the material time derivative
25
25

25

This feature is lost for only objective rates
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}
, such as the Truesdell rate.

of
\sigma
, i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\qquad\textnormal{for $\sigma=h^{\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}$}\,,

(4.32)

so that for
any corotational rate
,
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D
for the perfectly compressible fluid has the form (for more details about the calculation see the Appendix
A.8.8
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D=h^{\prime\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\sqrt{\det B}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,.

(4.33)

Checking positive definiteness of
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
leads to

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle=\langle h^{\prime\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\sqrt{\det B}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},D\rangle=h^{\prime\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\underbrace{\sqrt{\det B}\,\textnormal{tr}^{2}(D)}_{\geq\;0}

(4.34)

so that
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
if and only if
h(x)
is convex. This result remains true for the Cauchy-elastic case.

4.2
Corotational stability for the logarithmic rate

In Xiao et al.
[
130
,
131
,
132
,
133
]
the authors present the unique corotational rate in the family of corotational rates with spin tensors
\Omega^{\circ}
of the form

\displaystyle\Omega^{\circ}

\displaystyle=W+\widetilde{\Upsilon}(B,D)=W+\nu_{1}\,\textnormal{skew}(B\,D)+\nu_{2}\,\textnormal{skew}(B^{2}\,D)+\nu_{3}\,\textnormal{skew}(B\,D\,B^{2}),

(4.35)

called the “logarithmic rate” (cf.
[
137
]
). Here, each coefficient
\nu_{k}
is an isotropic invariant of
B
, i.e.
\nu_{k}=\nu_{k}(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
with
I_{1}=\textnormal{tr}B,\;I_{2}=\textnormal{tr}(\Cof B),\;I_{3}=\det B
. The logarithmic rate is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega^{\log}-\Omega^{\log}\,\sigma,\qquad\Omega^{\log}=\Omega^{\log}(W,B,D)\in\mathfrak{so}(3),

(4.36)

with the “logarithmic spin”
\Omega^{\log}
(discovery roughly at the same time by different groups, cf. Lehmann, Guo and Liang
[
61
]
, Reinhardt and Dubey
[
99
]
, Xiao, Bruhns and Meyers
[
132
]
and Zhilin et al.
[
137
]
) that satisfies the defining relation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log V]=D\qquad\Bigl(\textnormal{and thus }\;\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B]=2\,D\Bigr).

(4.37)

This shows conclusively that the Eulerian stretching
D=\sym\mathrm{D}v
is a true rate of the spatial logarithmic strain tensor
\log V
. While the analytical expression for the spin tensor
\Omega^{\log}
(cf. Footnote
in the Appendix) is involved, we can use the characterization (
4.37
) to show now easily that

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle>0\quad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}\qquad\iff\qquad\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).D,D\rangle>0\quad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\},

(4.38)

i.e. the equivalence of CSP with TSTS-M
++
also for the logarithmic rate.

Proposition 4.10
.

(Equivalence of CSP with TSTS-M
++
for the logarithmic rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}
)

Let
\sigma=\sigma(B)\in C^{1}(\Sym^{++}(3),\Sym(3))
be an isotropic tensor function. Then for the logarithmic rate

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega^{\log}-\Omega^{\log}\,\sigma

(4.39)

with the logarithmic spin
\Omega^{\log}=\dot{Q}^{\log}\,(Q^{\log})^{T},\;Q^{\log}\in{\rm O}(3)
we have CSP
\iff
TSTS-M
++
.

Proof.

We write
\sigma(B)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
and calculate with the chain rule for corotational rates (Proposition
4.2
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}(\log B)\overset{\eqref{eqreallograte}}{=}2\,\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).D\,.

(4.40)

In particular,

\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)].D,D\rangle>0\quad\iff\quad\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).D,D\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}\,,

(4.41)

which immediately implies the equivalence between CSP and
\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

The induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\log}(\sigma)
for the logarithmic rate takes an exceptionally simple form. Indeed, since

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B]\right]=2\,\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).D

(4.42)

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\log}(\sigma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2\,\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)=\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)

(4.43)

which can be truly called “
logarithmic tangent stiffness
”. Moreover,

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{H}^{\log}(\sigma)\neq 0\qquad\iff\qquad\det\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\neq 0\qquad\iff\qquad\det\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)\neq 0,

(4.44)

complementing Proposition
2.9
.

Furthermore, the corotational stability postulate takes a suggestive form for the logarithmic derivative (cf. (
2.65
)). Indeed,

\displaystyle 0<\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle

\displaystyle{=}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log V]\rangle=\langle Q^{\log}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\log})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\log}]\,Q^{\log},Q^{\log}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\log})^{T}\,(\log V)\,Q^{\log}]\,Q^{\log}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\log})^{T}\,\sigma\,Q^{\log}],\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\log})^{T}\,(\log V)\,Q^{\log}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\log})^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\,Q^{\log}],\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\log})^{T}\,(\log V)\,Q^{\log}]\rangle

(4.45)

\displaystyle{=}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}((Q^{\log})^{T}\,(\log V)\,Q^{\log})],\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\log})^{T}\,(\log V)\,Q^{\log}]\rangle

\displaystyle{=}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\underbrace{\log((Q^{\log})^{T}\,V\,Q^{\log})}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\,\widetilde{\varepsilon}})],\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\underbrace{\log((Q^{\log})^{T}\,V\,Q^{\log})}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\,\widetilde{\varepsilon}}]\rangle=\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\widetilde{\varepsilon})],\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\widetilde{\varepsilon}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}(\widetilde{\varepsilon}).\dot{\widetilde{\varepsilon}},\dot{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}\rangle\,.

Thus

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\textnormal{CSP}\;\;\iff\;\;\sym\,\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}(\widetilde{\varepsilon})\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\;\;\iff\;\;\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\;\;\iff\;\;\textnormal{TSTS-M${}^{++}$},

(4.46)

arriving, once again, at the correspondence (now for the
logarithmic rate
):

For an isotropic Cauchy-elastic material, the corotational stability postulate (CSP)
\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle>0
is equivalent to the strong Hilbert-monotonicity of
\widehat{\sigma}
in
\log V
, which is TSTS-M
++
.

4.3
Material spins, invertibility considerations and positive corotational rates

The material and exposition in this section is needed as a preparatory step for tackling Conjecture
3.8
in terms of matrix analysis instead of the cumbersome method of Lagrangean axes. The bulk of this work is displayed in
[
80
]
. Here, we will solely give a short overview of the general concept and its implications on invertibility for a fourth order tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
induced by an arbitrary corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
.

Therefore, we begin by asking the leading question, whether or not it is always possible to write an induced fourth-order tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
in the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]\overset{\text{chain rule}}{=}\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B).D\,,

(4.47)

as this identity would require the corotational derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
to have the representation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]+B\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,B=\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B).D\,.

(4.48)

In an attempt to satisfyingly answer this question, we resort to the already mentioned (cf. (
3.8
)) class of objective corotational rates with so-called
material spins
. In this regard, we have the following Theorem
[
133
, p.22]
and Definition
[
133
, p.25]
from Xiao et al.:

Theorem 4.12
.

Let the spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}
of an objective, corotational rate be associated with the deformation and rotation of a deforming material body as indicated by

\displaystyle\Omega^{\circ}=\Upsilon(B,D,W)\,.

(4.49)

Moreover, let the tensor function
\Upsilon
be continuous with respect to the argument
B
. Then the corotational rate of any Eulerian strain measure
e
defined by
\Omega^{\circ}
is objective if and only if

\displaystyle\Omega^{\circ}=W+\widetilde{\Upsilon}(B,D),

(4.50)

where
\widetilde{\Upsilon}
is an isotropic skew symmetric tensor-valued function of
B
and
D
that is continuous with respect to the argument
B
.

Proof.

See proof of Theorem 4 in
[
133
, p. 22]
.
∎

Definition 4.13

(
Material spins
)
.

An objective corotational rate of the form (
2.52
) with
\Omega^{\circ}
given by (
4.50
) is called a
material spin
if
\widetilde{\Upsilon}(B,D)
is
linear in
D
,
isotropic in
B
and
D
and satisfies the
homogeneity condition
\widetilde{\Upsilon}(\alpha\,B,D)=\widetilde{\Upsilon}(B,D)
for all
\alpha>0
.

This set defines a physically reasonably large subclass of spin tensors for objective corotational rates which includes all known corotational rates. Additionally, these objective corotational rates
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
admit the general structure (cf.
[
80
]
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]

\displaystyle=D\,B+B\,D+\nu_{1}\,(B\,\textnormal{skew}(B\,D)-\textnormal{skew}(B\,D)\,B)+\nu_{2}\,(B\,\textnormal{skew}(B^{2}\,D)-\textnormal{skew}(B^{2}\,D)\,B)

(4.51)

\displaystyle+\nu_{3}\,(B\,\textnormal{skew}(B^{2}\,D\,B)-\textnormal{skew}(B^{2}\,D\,B)\,B)\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B).D\,,

so that (
4.48
) is satisfied and
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
can indeed be expressed in the form (
4.47
), i.e.

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B).D=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B).D

(4.52)

with
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)
given in (
4.51
), extending the result in Norris
[
86
, Lemma 1]
from primary matrix functions to arbitrary isotropic tensor functions. Additionally, it is shown in
[
80
]
that the tensor
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)
is
minor and major symmetric
. Evaluation of (
4.52
) at
B={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
with stress free initial state (
\sigma({\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})=0
) gives the linear elastic-like relation

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(0).D=2\,\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(0).D=\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(0).D=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D=2\,\mu\,D+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,.

(4.53)

We would also like to point out the following result regarding the invertibility of
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)
, further complementing Proposition
2.9
:

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\subset\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
be an invertible, isotropic and differentiable tensor function. Consider
any
corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
with material spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}
of the form (
4.50
) and let
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
be the induced tangent stiffness tensor. If
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)
is invertible, then we have the equivalence

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)\neq 0\quad\iff\quad\det\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\neq 0\quad\iff\quad\det\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)\neq 0

(4.54)

where
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)
is given in (
4.51
).

Proof.

This follows instantly by the representation formula (
4.52
) on noting that
\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
.
∎

Lastly, in view of the corotational stability postulate, it now appears reasonable to focus the attention on
positive corotational derivatives
, a subclass of corotational derivatives with material spins given by

Definition 4.15

(Positive corotational derivatives)
.

Positive corotational derivatives
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
are those objective corotational derivatives with material spins given by (
4.50
) such that for
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)
defined by
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B).D
we have
\mathbb{A}^{\circ}(B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
.

In the sense of this definition,
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t},\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}
and
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}
qualify as positive corotational rates. As mentioned in Conjecture
3.8
we intend to show CSP
\iff
TSTS-M
++
for all positive corotational rates in a future contribution without resorting to the burdensome principal axis calculus given in
[
14
]
. The result for the logarithmic rate in Proposition
4.10
shows that this result is plausible.

5
Synthesis of the results

5.1
Connection to the BCH-formula

A much needed perspective for the obtained result will be tentatively presented next. We have seen in Theorem
3.1
by lengthy calculations for the Zaremba-Jaumann rate (cf.
[
14
]
) that for an isotropic tensor function
\widehat{\sigma}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
satisfying
\widehat{\sigma}(Q^{T}\,S\,Q)=Q^{T}\,\widehat{\sigma}(S)\,Q
for all
Q\in{\rm O}(3)
,

\displaystyle\hskip-8.0pt\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B],D\rangle>0\quad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}\quad\iff\quad\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).D,D\rangle>0.

(5.1)

Thus, this equivalence would have been an easy observation if
\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]=2\,D
, which is, however
false
if
B\,D\neq D\,B
(cf. (
A.172
)). Instead, the commutator
[B,D]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}B\,D-D\,B
naturally needs to be taken into account. In the following table we gather some pertinent observations in this direction.

On the one hand…

but still…

as seen in…

\exp(B+D)\neq\exp(B)\,\exp(D)

if
B\,D=D\,B
then

\exp(B+D)=\exp(B)\,\exp(D)

(
A.183
)

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+B\,D]\neq 2\,D

if
B\,D=D\,B
then

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]=2\,D

Proposition
A.31

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H\neq B^{-1}H

if
B\,H=H\,B
then

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H=B^{-1}\,H

(
A.174
)

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H\neq B^{-1}\,H

in general if
BH\neq HB

\mathrm{D}_{B}[\widehat{\mathrm{W}}(\log B)]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}(\log B)\,B^{-1}

for an isotropic and scalar valued function
\mathrm{W}

Richter-Valleé

representation

cf. Appendix
A.8.11

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+B\,D]\neq 2\,D

\textnormal{tr}(\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B])=\textnormal{tr}(2\,D)

(
A.86
)

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+B\,D]\neq 2\,D

\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B],D\rangle\geq c^{+}\,\lVert 2\,D\rVert^{2}

Lemma
A.26

\exp(B+D)\neq\exp(B)\,\exp(D)

in general if
B\,D\neq D\,B

\textnormal{tr}(\exp(B+D))\leq\textnormal{tr}(\exp(B)\,\exp(D))

Golden-Thompson

inequality

cf. Appendix
A.8.12

\exp(B+D)\neq\exp(B)\,\exp(D)

in general if
B\,D\neq D\,B

\textnormal{tr}(\log(\exp(B)\,\exp(D)))=\textnormal{tr}(B)+\textnormal{tr}(D)

implication of BCH
26
26

26

In general the
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff
(BCH) formula (cf.
[
42
, p.861]
) determines the expression
Z=Z(B,D)
so that

\displaystyle\exp(B)\,\exp(D)=\exp(Z(B,D))\qquad\iff\qquad\log(\exp(B)\,\exp(D))=Z(B,D)

and
Z(B,D)
involves iterated commutator-brackets, i.e.

\displaystyle Z(B,D)=\log(\exp(B)\,\exp(D))=B+D+\frac{1}{2}\,[B,D]+\frac{1}{12}\,[B,[B,D]]-\frac{1}{12}\,[D,[B,D]]+\textnormal{h.o.t.}

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]\neq 2\,D

\widehat{\sigma}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
isotropic tensor function

\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).(\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]),D\rangle>0

\iff\quad\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).2\,D,D\rangle>0,\quad\forall D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}

\iff\quad\sym\,\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)

CSP
\iff
TSTS-M
+

“corotational stability

postulate”

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]\neq 2\,D

\widehat{\tau}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
isotropic tensor function

\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\tau}(\log B).(\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]),D\rangle>0

\iff\quad\langle\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\tau}(\log B).2\,D,D\rangle>0,\quad\forall D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}

\iff\quad\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\tau}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)

hyperelastic

Hill’s inequality

cf.
[
14
]

Note that this set of properties does have a common theme. Indeed, isotropic functions or operators can make up for lack of commutation (i.e.
[B,D]\neq 0
) in the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH)
[
42
, p.861]
formula if inequalities are concerned and our statement in Theorem
3.1
is just another instance of this general observation.

5.2
Continuum mechanics perspective - constitutive requirements

From a continuum mechanics perspective with a view on constitutive requirements we can summarize our results in the following diagram (the result holds as well with
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
replaced by
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}
).
\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{llc}&\Bigg\{&\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{corotational stability postulate (CSP)}\\
\textbf{for the Cauchy stress $\sigma$}\\
\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle=\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0\\
\end{array}\\
\iff&\Bigg\{&\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{TSTS-M${}^{++}$}:\\
\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\\
\implies\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle>0\end{array}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\vtop{$\not\phantom{"}$\cr$\iff$\crcr}}}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\textbf{Legendre-Hadamard ellipticity}:\\
\mathrm{D}_{F}^{2}\mathrm{W}(F).(\xi\otimes\eta,\xi\otimes\eta)\geq c^{+}\,|\xi|^{2}\cdot|\eta|^{2}\\
\langle S_{1}(F+\xi\otimes\eta)-S_{1}(F),\xi\otimes\eta\rangle\geq c^{+}\,|\xi|^{2}\,|\eta|^{2}\end{array}\right.\vphantom{\begin{array}[]{llc}&\Bigg\{&\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{corotational stability postulate (CSP)}\\
\textbf{for the Cauchy stress $\sigma$}\\
\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle=\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0\\
\end{array}\\
\iff&\Bigg\{&\begin{array}[]{c}\textbf{TSTS-M${}^{++}$}:\\
\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\\
\implies\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle>0\end{array}\end{array}}

\displaystyle\textnormal{BE}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{j})\,(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})\geq 0\vphantom{\textnormal{TE}:\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\partial\sigma_{i}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}{\partial\lambda_{i}}\geq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\iff\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\partial\widehat{\sigma}_{i}(\log\lambda_{1},\log\lambda_{2},\log\lambda_{3})}{\partial\log\lambda_{i}}\geq 0}

\displaystyle\textnormal{TE}:\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\partial\sigma_{i}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}{\partial\lambda_{i}}\geq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\iff\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\partial\widehat{\sigma}_{i}(\log\lambda_{1},\log\lambda_{2},\log\lambda_{3})}{\partial\log\lambda_{i}}\geq 0

Note that TSTS-M
++
implies the tension-extension (TE) inequality since the positive definiteness of (the symmetric part of)
\frac{\partial\widehat{\sigma}_{i}(\log\lambda_{1},\log\lambda_{2},\log\lambda_{3})}{\partial\log\lambda_{j}}
implies that the diagonal entries
\frac{\partial\widehat{\sigma}_{i}}{\partial\log\lambda_{i}}
are positive.

For purely volumetric energy functions (elastic fluids, cf.
[
64
, p. 10]
and the Appendix
A.8.6
), the equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}(F)=h(\det F)

\displaystyle\textnormal{CSP}\quad\iff\quad\textnormal{TSTS-M${}^{++}$}\quad\iff\quad

\displaystyle\textnormal{LH-ellipticity}\iff\quad\mathrm{W}\;\textnormal{polyconvex}\quad\iff\quad h\;\textnormal{convex}

hold, which gives support to using exclusively the Cauchy stress
\sigma
in setting up the CSP-condition. Indeed, if we would use the spatial Kirchhoff stress
\tau=J\,\sigma
instead, a non-convex
h
would be permitted, e.g.
h(\det F)=(\log\det F)^{2}=\textnormal{tr}^{2}(\log V)
, which is unphysical.

In this way, we answer the issues concerning the use of hypo-elasticity to discern constitutive properties for nonlinear elasticity as raised in the introduction as follows:

1a)

which rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}
?
\rightarrow
objective, corotational rates
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
only

1b)

which corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
?
\rightarrow
presumably largely irrelevant for the equivalence CSP
\iff
TSTS-M
++
(any positive corotational rate might do, cf. Section
4.3
and
[
80
]
)

2)

which stress?
\rightarrow
only the true/Cauchy stress
\sigma

3)

which tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}
?
\rightarrow
only the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)

for the corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
.

Expressed by the logarithmic strain tensor
\log V
, we obtain for the idealized isotropic nonlinear elastic constitutive law given as a mapping
\log V\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)=\sigma(V)
in terms of a rate-formulation in the spatial setting the following concordance:

\displaystyle\log V

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\qquad

\displaystyle\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\quad\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(B)\,,

\displaystyle\log V

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\;\textnormal{invertible}\qquad

\displaystyle\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\quad\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\,,

\displaystyle\log V

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\;\textnormal{invertible and}\det\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\neq 0\qquad

\displaystyle\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\quad\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\;\textnormal{invertible}\,,

\displaystyle\log V

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\;\textnormal{strongly monotone}\qquad

\displaystyle\!\!\iff\quad\sym\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\,.

(5.2)

The last requirement (
5.2
) represents our proposal for the notion that
stress increases with strain
in idealized isotropic nonlinear elasticity, generalizing Hooke’s linear elasticity law
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{ut\;tensio\smash{,}\,\,sic\;vis}}
.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Notation

The deformation
\varphi(x,t)
, the material time derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}
and the partial time derivative
\partial_{t}

In accordance with
[
64
]
we agree on the following convention regarding an elastic deformation
\varphi
and time derivatives of material quantities:

Given two sets
\Omega,\Omega_{\xi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
we denote by
\varphi:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega_{\xi},x\mapstochar\rightarrow\varphi(x)=\xi
the deformation from the
reference configuration

\Omega
to the
current configuration

\Omega_{\xi}
. A
motion
of
\Omega
is a time-dependent family of deformations, written
\xi=\varphi(x,t)
. The
velocity
of the point
x\in\Omega
is defined by
\overline{V}(x,t)=\partial_{t}\varphi(x,t)
and describes a vector emanating from the point
\xi=\varphi(x,t)
(see also Figure
4
). Similarly, the velocity viewed as a function of
\xi\in\Omega_{\xi}
is denoted by
v(\xi,t)
.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
curve
[formula]
for fixed
[formula]

Figure 4:
Illustration of the deformation
\varphi(x,t):\Omega_{x}\rightarrow\Omega_{\xi}
and the velocity
\overline{V}(x,t)=v(\xi,t)
.

Considering an arbitrary material quantity
Q(x,t)
on
\Omega
, equivalently represented by
q(\xi,t)
on
\Omega_{\xi}
, we obtain by the chain rule for the time derivative of
Q(x,t)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}q(\xi,t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[Q(x,t)]=\mathrm{D}_{\xi}q(\xi,t).v+\partial_{t}q(\xi,t)\,.

(A.1)

Since it is always possible to view any material quantity
Q(x,t)=q(\xi,t)
from two different angles, namely by holding
x
or
\xi
fixed, we agree to write

•

\dot{q}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[q]
for the material (substantial) derivative of
q
with respect to
t
holding
x
fixed and

•

\partial_{t}q
for the derivative of
q
with respect to
t
holding
\xi
fixed.

For example, we obtain the velocity gradient
L:=\mathrm{D}_{\xi}v(\xi,t)
by

\displaystyle L=\mathrm{D}_{\xi}v(\xi,t)=\mathrm{D}_{\xi}\overline{V}(x,t)

\displaystyle{=}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\varphi(x,t)=\mathrm{D}_{\xi}\partial_{t}\varphi(\varphi^{-1}(\xi,t),t)=\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{x}\varphi(\varphi^{-1}(\xi,t),t)\,\mathrm{D}_{\xi}\big(\varphi^{-1}(\xi,t)\big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{x}\varphi(\varphi^{-1}(\xi,t),t)\,(\mathrm{D}_{x}\varphi)^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(\xi,t),t)=\dot{F}(x,t)\,F^{-1}(x,t)=L\,,

(A.2)

where we used that
\partial_{t}=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}
are all the same, if
x
is fixed.

As another example, when determining a corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
we write

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma=\dot{\sigma}+\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma\,.

(A.3)

However, if we solely work on the current configuration, i.e. holding
\xi
fixed, we write
\partial_{t}v
for the time-derivative of the velocity (or any quantity in general).

Inner product

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we let
\langle{a},{b}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
denote the scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with associated vector norm
\lVert a\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{2}=\langle{a},{a}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. We denote by
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
the set of real
n\times n
second order tensors, written with capital letters. The standard Euclidean scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is given by

\langle{X},{Y}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}=\textnormal{tr}{(XY^{T})}
, where the superscript
T
is used to denote transposition. Thus the Frobenius tensor norm is
\lVert X\rVert^{2}=\langle{X},{X}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}
, where we usually omit the subscript
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
in writing the Frobenius tensor norm. The identity tensor on
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
will be denoted by
{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
, so that
\textnormal{tr}{(X)}=\langle{X},{{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}}\rangle
.

Frequently used spaces

•

\Sym(n),\rm\Sym^{+}(n)
and
\Sym^{++}(n)
denote the symmetric, positive semi-definite symmetric and positive definite symmetric second order tensors respectively. Note that
\Sym^{++}(n)
is considered herein only as an algebraic subset of
\Sym(n)
, not endowed with a Riemannian geometry
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
52
]
.

•

{\rm GL}(n)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\;|\det{X}\neq 0\}
denotes the general linear group.

•

{\rm GL}^{+}(n)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\;|\det{X}>0\}
is the group of invertible matrices with positive determinant.

•

{\rm SL}(n)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{X\in{\rm GL}(n)\;|\det{X}=1\}
.

•

\mathrm{O}(n)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{X\in{\rm GL}(n)\;|\;X^{T}X={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\}
.

•

{\rm SO}(n)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{X\in{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{R})\;|\;X^{T}X={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},\;\det{X}=1\}
.

•

\mathfrak{so}(3)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\;|\;X^{T}=-X\}
is the Lie-algebra of skew symmetric tensors.

•

\mathfrak{sl}(3)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\;|\;\textnormal{tr}({X})=0\}
is the Lie-algebra of traceless tensors.

•

The set of positive real numbers is denoted by
\mathbb{R}_{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(0,\infty)
, while
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Frequently used tensors

•

F=\mathrm{D}\varphi(x,t)
is the Fréchet derivative (Jacobean) of the deformation
\varphi(\,,t)\colon\Omega_{x}\rightarrow\Omega_{\xi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
\varphi(x,t)
is usually assumed to be a diffeomorphism at every time
t\geq 0
so that the inverse mapping
\varphi^{-1}(\,,t)\colon\Omega_{\xi}\rightarrow\Omega_{x}
exists.

•

C=F^{T}\,F
is the right Cauchy-Green strain tensor.

•

B=F\,F^{T}
is the left Cauchy-Green (or Finger) strain tensor.

•

U=\sqrt{F^{T}\,F}\in\Sym^{++}(3)
is the right stretch tensor, i.e. the unique element of
{\rm Sym}^{++}(3)
with
U^{2}=C
.

•

V=\sqrt{F\,F^{T}}\in\Sym^{++}(3)
is the left stretch tensor, i.e. the unique element of
{\rm Sym}^{++}(3)
with
V^{2}=B
.

•

\log V=\frac{1}{2}\,\log B
is the spatial logarithmic strain tensor or Hencky strain.

•

L=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}=\mathrm{D}_{\xi}v(\xi)
is the spatial velocity gradient.

•

v=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\varphi(x,t)
denotes the Eulerian velocity.

•

D=\sym\,L
is the spatial rate of deformation, the Eulerian strain rate tensor.

•

W=\textnormal{skew}\,L
is the vorticity tensor.

•

We also have the polar decomposition
F=R\,U=VR\in{\rm GL}^{+}(3)
with an orthogonal matrix
R\in{\rm O}(3)
(cf. Neff et al.
[
81
]
), see also
[
59
,
84
]
.

Frequently used rates

•

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}
denotes an arbitrary objective derivative,

•

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}\begin{array}[]{l}\text{denotes an arbitrary corotational}\\
\text{derivative,}\end{array}

•

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}\begin{array}[]{l}\text{denotes the Zaremba-Jaumann}\\
\text{derivative,}\end{array}

•

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}
denotes the Green-Naghdi derivative.

•

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}
denotes the logarithmic derivative.

•

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}
denotes the material derivative.

Calculus with the material derivative – some examples

Consider the spatial Cauchy stress

\displaystyle\sigma(\xi,t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Sigma(B)=\Sigma(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t))=\Sigma(F(\varphi^{-1}(\xi,t),t)\,F^{T}(\varphi^{-1}(\xi,t),t))\,.

(A.4)

Then, on the one hand we have for the material derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{\xi}\sigma(\xi,t).v(\xi,t)+\partial_{t}\sigma(\xi,t)\cdot 1

(A.5)

and on the other hand equivalently

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\Sigma(F(x,t)\,F(x,t)^{T})]\overset{(1)}{=}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[\Sigma(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t))]

(A.6)

\displaystyle\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{standard}\\
\text{chain rule}\end{subarray}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\mathrm{D}_{B}\Sigma(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t)).\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}[(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t))]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\Sigma(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t)).(\dot{F}\,F^{T}+F\,\dot{F}^{T})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{B}\Sigma(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t)).[\dot{F}\,F^{-1}\,F\,F^{T}+F\,F^{T}\,F^{-T}\,\dot{F}^{T}]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\Sigma(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t)).[L\,B+B\,L^{T}]\,.

In
\eqref{eqmat03}_{1}
we have used the fact that there is already a material representation which allows to set
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}
. Of course, (
A.5
) is equivalent to (
A.6
). From the context it should be clear which representation of
\sigma
(referential or spatial) we are working with and by abuse of notation we do not distinguish between
\sigma
and
\Sigma
.

The same must be observed when calculating with corotational derivatives

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega^{\circ}-\Omega^{\circ}\,\sigma,\qquad\Omega^{\circ}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}Q^{\circ}(x,t)\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}Q^{\circ}(x,t)\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,.

(A.7)

Here, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle{=}Q^{\circ}(x,t)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}(x,t)]\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)

(A.8)

\displaystyle=Q^{\circ}(x,t)\,\left\{\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}(x,t)+(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{\circ}(x,t)+(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,\sigma\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}Q^{\circ}(x,t)\right\}\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)

\displaystyle=Q^{\circ}(x,t)\,\bigg\{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,\sigma\,Q^{\circ}(x,t)+(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]}_{(\ast\ast\ast)}\,Q^{\circ}(x,t)+(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)\,\sigma\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}Q^{\circ}(x,t)\bigg\}\,(Q^{\circ})^{T}(x,t)

and we can decide for
(\ast\ast\ast)
to continue the calculus with (
A.5
) or (
A.6
). In either case one has to decide viewing the functions as defined on the reference configuration
\Omega
or in the spatial configuration
\Omega_{\xi}
.

In (
A.8
) we used
Q=Q(x,t)
and
\Omega=\Omega(x,t)
. This means that the “Lie-type” representation
(\ast\,\ast)
necessitates the definition of a reference configuration, so that we can switch between
\xi=\varphi(x,t)
and
x
.

The interpretation
(\ast\,\ast)
is most clearly represented for the Green-Naghdi rate, in which the spin
\Omega^{\GN}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}R(x,t)\,R^{T}(x,t)=\dot{R}(x,t)\,R^{T}(x,t)
is defined according to the polar decomposition
F=R\,U
and in

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega^{\GN}-\Omega^{\GN}\,\sigma=R\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[R^{T}\,\sigma\,R]\,R^{T}

(A.9)

the term
[R^{T}\,\sigma\,R]
is called
corotational stress tensor
(cf.
[
64
, p. 142]
).

Tensor domains

Denoting the reference configuration by
\Omega_{x}
with tangential space
T_{x}\Omega_{x}
and the current/spatial configuration by
\Omega_{\xi}
with tangential space
T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}
as well as
\varphi(x)=\xi
, we have the following relations (see also Figure
5
):

Figure 5:
Illustration of the curve
s\mapstochar\rightarrow\varphi(\gamma(s),t_{0}),\;\gamma(0)=x
for a fixed time
t=t_{0}
with vector field
s\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\varphi(\gamma(s),t)\in T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}
.

•

F\colon T_{x}\Omega_{x}\rightarrow T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}

•

Q\colon T_{x}\Omega_{x}\rightarrow T_{\xi}\Omega\xi

•

F^{T}\colon T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}\rightarrow T_{x}\Omega_{x}

•

Q^{T}\colon T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}\rightarrow T_{x}\Omega_{x}

C=F^{T}\,F\colon T_{x}\Omega_{x}\rightarrow T_{x}\Omega_{x}

•

B=F\,F^{T}\colon T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}\rightarrow T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}

\sigma\colon T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}\rightarrow T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}

•

\tau\colon T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}\rightarrow T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}

S_{2}\colon T_{x}\Omega_{x}\rightarrow T_{x}\Omega_{x}

•

S_{1}\colon T_{x}\Omega_{x}\rightarrow T_{\xi}\Omega_{\xi}

•

Q^{T}\,\sigma\,Q\colon T_{x}\Omega_{x}\rightarrow T_{x}\Omega_{x}

The strain energy function
\mathrm{W}(F)

We are only concerned with rotationally symmetric functions
\mathrm{W}(F)
(objective and isotropic), i.e.

\mathrm{W}(F)={\mathrm{W}}(Q_{1}^{T}\,F\,Q_{2}),\qquad\forall\,F\in{\rm GL}^{+}(3),\qquad Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\rm SO}(3).

Primary matrix functions

We define primary matrix functions as those functions
\Sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
, such that

\displaystyle\Sigma(V)=\Sigma(Q^{T}\,\textnormal{diag}_{V}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,Q)=Q^{T}\Sigma(\textnormal{diag}_{V}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}))\,Q=Q^{T}\,\begin{pmatrix}f(\lambda_{1})&0&0\\
0&f(\lambda_{2})&0\\
0&0&f(\lambda_{3})\end{pmatrix}\,Q

(A.10)

with one given real-valued scale-function
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Any primary matrix function is an isotropic matrix function but not vice-versa as shows e.g.
\Sigma(V)=\det V\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
.

List of additional definitions and useful identities

•

For two metric spaces
X,Y
and a linear map
L:X\rightarrow Y
with argument
v\in X
we write
L.v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L(v)
. This applies to a second order tensor
A
and a vector
v
as
A.v
as well as a fourth order tensor
\mathbb{C}
and a second order tensor
H
as
\mathbb{C}.H
. Sometimes we may emphasize the usual matrix product of two second order tensors
A,B
as
A\cdot B
.

•

We define
J=\det{F}
and denote by
\Cof(X)=(\det X)X^{-T}
the cofactor of a matrix in
{\rm GL}^{+}(3)
.

•

We define
\sym X=\frac{1}{2}\,(X+X^{T})
and
\textnormal{skew}X=\frac{1}{2}\,(X-X^{T})
as well as
\dev X=X-\frac{1}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(X)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
.

•

For all vectors
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have the tensor product
(\xi\otimes\eta)_{ij}=\xi_{i}\,\eta_{j}
.

•

S_{1}=\mathrm{D}_{F}\mathrm{W}(F)=\sigma\,\Cof F
is the non-symmetric first Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor.

•

S_{2}=F^{-1}S_{1}=2\,\mathrm{D}_{C}\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(C)
is the symmetric second Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor.

•

\sigma=\frac{1}{J}\,S_{1}\,F^{T}=\frac{1}{J}\,F\,S_{2}\,F^{T}=\frac{2}{J}\mathrm{D}_{B}\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(B)\,B=\frac{1}{J}\mathrm{D}_{V}\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(V)\,V=\frac{1}{J}\,\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}(\log V)
is the symmetric Cauchy stress tensor.

•

\sigma=\frac{1}{J}\,F\,S_{2}\,F^{T}=\frac{2}{J}\,F\,\mathrm{D}_{C}\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(C)\,F^{T}
is the “
Doyle-Ericksen formula
”
[
18
]
.

•

For
\sigma\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
we denote by
\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B)
with
\sigma(B+H)=\sigma(B)+\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).H+o(H)
the Fréchet-derivative. For
\sigma\colon\Sym^{+}(3)\subset\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
the same applies. Similarly, for
\mathrm{W}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we have
\mathrm{W}(X+H)=\mathrm{W}(X)+\langle\mathrm{D}_{X}\mathrm{W}(X),H\rangle+o(H)
.

•

\tau=J\,\sigma=2\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(B)\,B
is the symmetric Kirchhoff stress tensor.

•

\tau=\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}(\log V)
is the “
Richter-formula
”
[
100
,
101
]
.

•

\sigma_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\frac{\partial g(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}{\partial\lambda_{i}}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}\displaystyle\frac{\partial g(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}{\partial\lambda_{i}},\ \ i\neq j\neq k\neq i
are the principal Cauchy stresses (the eigenvalues of the Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
), where
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the unique function of the singular values of
U
(the principal stretches) such that
\mathrm{W}(F)=\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(U)=g(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
.

•

\sigma_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}\frac{\partial\widehat{g}(\log\lambda_{1},\log\lambda_{2},\log\lambda_{3})}{\partial\log\lambda_{i}}
, where
\widehat{g}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the unique function such that

\widehat{g}(\log\lambda_{1},\log\lambda_{2},\log\lambda_{3})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}g(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
.

•

\tau_{i}=J\,\sigma_{i}=\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\frac{\partial g(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}{\partial\lambda_{i}}=\frac{\partial\widehat{g}(\log\lambda_{1},\log\lambda_{2},\log\lambda_{3})}{\partial\log\lambda_{i}}
.

Conventions for fourth order symmetric operators, minor and major symmetry

Fourth order tensors are written as
\mathbb{H}
or
\mathbb{C}
. For a fourth order linear mapping
\mathbb{C}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
we agree on the following convention.

We say that
\mathbb{C}
has
minor symmetry
if

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}.S\in\Sym(3)\qquad\forall\,S\in\Sym(3).

(A.11)

This can also be written in index notation as
C_{ijkm}=C_{jikm}=C_{ijmk}
. If we consider a more general fourth order tensor
\mathbb{C}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
then
\mathbb{C}
can be transformed having minor symmetry by considering the mapping
X\mapstochar\rightarrow\sym(\mathbb{C}.\sym X)
such that
\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
is minor symmetric, if and only if
\mathbb{C}.X=\sym(\mathbb{C}.\sym X)
.

We say that
\mathbb{C}
has
major symmetry
(or is
self-adjoint
, respectively) if

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{C}.S_{1},S_{2}\rangle=\langle\mathbb{C}.S_{2},S_{1}\rangle\qquad\forall\,S_{1},S_{2}\in\Sym(3).

(A.12)

Major symmetry in index notation is understood as
C_{ijkm}=C_{kmij}
.

The set of positive definite, major symmetric fourth order tensors mapping
\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
is denoted as
\Sym^{++}_{4}(9)
, in case of additional minor symmetry, i.e. mapping
\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
as
\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
. By identifying
\Sym(3)\cong\mathbb{R}^{6}
, we can view
\mathbb{C}
as a linear mapping in matrix form
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}:\mathbb{R}^{6}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{6}
.

If
H\in\Sym(3)\cong\mathbb{R}^{6}
has the entries
H_{ij}
, we can write

\displaystyle h=\textnormal{vec}(H)=(H_{11},H_{22},H_{33},H_{12},H_{23},H_{31})\in\mathbb{R}^{6}\qquad\textnormal{so that}\qquad\langle\mathbb{C}.H,H\rangle_{\Sym(3)}=\langle\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}.h,h\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}.

(A.13)

If
\mathbb{C}:\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
, we can define
\bfsym\mathbb{C}
by

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{C}.H,H\rangle_{\Sym(3)}=\langle\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}.h,h\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}=\langle\sym\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}.h,h\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\langle\bfsym\mathbb{C}.H,H\rangle_{\Sym(3)},\qquad\forall\,H\in\Sym(3).

(A.14)

Major symmetry in these terms can be expressed as
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}\in\Sym(6)
.
In this text, however, we omit the tilde-operation and
{\bf sym}
and write in short
\sym\mathbb{C}\in{\rm Sym}_{4}(6)
if no confusion can arise.
In the same manner we speak about
\det\mathbb{C}
meaning
\det\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}
.

A linear mapping
\mathbb{C}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
is positive definite if and only if

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{C}.H,H\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,H\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\qquad\iff\qquad\mathbb{C}\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(9)

(A.15)

and analogously it is positive semi-definite if and only if

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{C}.H,H\rangle\geq 0\qquad\forall\,H\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\qquad\iff\qquad\mathbb{C}\in\Sym^{+}_{4}(9).

(A.16)

For
\mathbb{C}:\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
, after identifying
\Sym(3)\cong\mathbb{R}^{6}
, we can reformulate (
A.15
) as
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}\in\Sym^{++}(6)
and (
A.16
) as
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}\in\Sym^{+}(6)
.

A.2
Hilbert-monotonicity

Regarding Hilbert-monotonicity, we recall the following properties from Ghiba et al.
[
31
]
.

Definition A.1
.

[
66
]

A tensor function
\Sigma_{f}:{\rm Sym}^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym^{++}\,
is called
strictly Hilbert-monotone
if

\displaystyle\langle\Sigma_{f}(U)-\Sigma_{f}(\overline{U}),\,U-\overline{U}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}>0\qquad\forall\,U\neq\overline{U}\in{\rm Sym}^{++}(3)\,.

(A.17)

We refer to this inequality as strict
Hilbert-space matrix-monotonicity
of the tensor function
\Sigma_{f}
.

Similarly,
\Sigma_{f}(U)
is
strongly Hilbert-monotone
if for all
H\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}
we have
\langle\mathrm{D}_{U}\Sigma_{f}(U).H,H\rangle>0
or equivalently
\sym\mathrm{D}_{U}\Sigma_{f}(U)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
.

Definition A.2
.

[
66
]

A vector function
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
strictly vector monotone
if

\displaystyle\langle f(\lambda)-f(\overline{\lambda}),\,\lambda-\overline{\lambda}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}>0\qquad\forall\lambda\neq\overline{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}.

(A.18)

Definition A.3
.

A differentiable function
f
between finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces is called
strongly monotone
if
\mathrm{D}f
is positive definite everywhere. Thus “stronlgy” implies “strictly”, see e.g. Remark
A.7
.

Note that for an arbitrary vector function
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
{\rm D}f\,(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
in itself might not be symmetric. One main goal of a forthcoming paper
[
67
]
is to proof the following result, thereby elucidating on Ogden’s work
[
87
, last page in Appendix]
, based on the seminal contributions of Hill
[
38
,
39
,
40
]
:

Theorem A.4
.

A symmetric function
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is strictly (strongly) vector-monotone if and only if
\Sigma_{f}
is strictly (strongly) matrix-monotone.

Remark A.5
.

Theorem
A.4
is decisive for the equivalence

\displaystyle\sym\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\qquad\iff\qquad\sym\frac{\partial\widehat{\sigma}_{i}}{\partial\log\lambda_{j}}\in\Sym^{++}(3),

(A.19)

where the
\widehat{\sigma}_{i}
are the principal Cauchy stresses expressed as function of the principle logarithmic strains
\log\lambda_{i}
.

A.3
True-Stress-True-Strain monotonicity (TSTS-M)

Definition A.6
.

We define three notions of
True-Stress-True-Strain monotonicity
as follows

\displaystyle\textnormal{TSTS-M:}\qquad

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq 0,\qquad\forall\,V_{1},V_{2}\in{\rm Sym}^{++}(3),\ V_{1}\neq V_{2},

(A.20)

\displaystyle\textnormal{TSTS-M${}^{+}$:}\qquad

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle>0,\qquad\forall\,V_{1},V_{2}\in{\rm Sym}^{++}(3),\ V_{1}\neq V_{2},

\displaystyle\textnormal{TSTS-M${}^{++}$:}\qquad

\displaystyle\sym\,\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6).

Note that this is equivalent to monotonicity of
\widehat{\sigma}
in
\log B
since
\log B=2\,\log V
.

Regarding TSTS-M
+
, we have the following properties from
[
78
]
.

Remark A.7
.

Sufficient for TSTS-M
+
is Jog and Patil’s
[
48
]
constitutive requirement that

\displaystyle\textnormal{TSTS-M${}^{++}$:}\qquad\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sym\,\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\,\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6),

(A.21)

i.e. in their notation (see also
[
78
, Remark 4.1]
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\langle\mathbb{Z}.H,H\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,H\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}.

(A.22)

Proof.

Let us remark that for all
B_{1},B_{2}\in{\rm Sym}^{++}(3)
and
0\leq t\leq 1
, we have
2\,\log V_{1}=\log B_{1},\,2\,\log V_{2}=\log B_{2}
and
t\,(\log V_{1}-\log V_{2})+\log V_{2}\in{\rm Sym}(3)
, where
V_{1}^{2}=B_{1},\,V_{2}^{2}=B_{2}
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{1})

\displaystyle-\widehat{\sigma}(\log B_{2}),\log B_{1}-\log B_{2}\rangle=2\,\langle\widehat{\sigma}(2\,\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(2\,\log V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\,\left\langle\left[\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\rm d}{\rm dt}\,\widehat{\sigma}\bigg(2\,t\,(\log V_{1}-\log V_{2})+2\,\log V_{2}\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right],\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\right\rangle

(A.23)

\displaystyle=4\,\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle\left[\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\,\widehat{\sigma}\bigg(2\,t\,(\log V_{1}-\log V_{2})+2\,\log V_{2}\bigg).\,(\log V_{1}-\log V_{2})\right],\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\right\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\,.

\displaystyle=4\,\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle\left[\sym\left(\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\,\widehat{\sigma}\bigg(2\,t\,(\log V_{1}-\log V_{2})+2\,\log V_{2}\bigg)\right).\,(\log V_{1}-\log V_{2})\right],\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\right\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\,.

Where the last equation of (
A.23
) is due to the fact that
for any skew symmetric matrix
A\in\mathfrak{so}(3)
, it always holds

\left\langle A.v,v\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}=0

for every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Using that the integrand is non-negative, due to the assumption that
\Lambda=\sym\,\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)
is positive definite, the TSTS-M
+
condition
follows.
∎

Remark A.8
.

As an easy consequence of the previous remark we obtain the implications

\displaystyle\textnormal{TSTS-M${}^{++}$}\qquad\implies\qquad\textnormal{TSTS-M${}^{+}$}\qquad\implies\qquad\textnormal{TSTS-M}

(A.24)

as well as the equivalence

TSTS-M
++

\iff
corotational stability postulate (CSP).

A.3.1
TSTS-M
++
for the exponentiated Hencky energy
V\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\mu}{k}\,e^{k\,\|\log\,V\|^{2}}+\frac{\lambda}{2\widehat{k}}\,e^{\widehat{k}\,[{\rm tr}(\log\,V)]^{2}}

Since our examples in the main part of this work regarding monotonicity of the Cauchy stress
\sigma
as a function of the logarithmic strain
\log V
are all
not hyperelastic
, we show in this Appendix that this monotonicity requirement is nevertheless in principle consistent with hyperelasticity. Note, however, that the classical compressible Neo-Hooke or compressible Mooney-Rivlin models do not satisfy TSTS-M (example given in Appendix
A.4
). For incompressible response, the situation is different. As further references for the exponentiated Hencky energy see also
[
73
,
76
,
135
]
.

Proposition A.9
.

The Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
corresponding to the energy
V\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\mu}{k}\,e^{k\,\|\log\,V\|^{2}}
satisfies TSTS-M for
k\geq\frac{3}{8}
and
TSTS-M
++
for
k>\frac{3}{8}
.

Proof.

In order to show this, let us remark that for the energy
V\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\mu}{k}\,e^{k\,\|\log\,V\|^{2}}
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}(\log\,V)

\displaystyle=\,2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|\log\,V\|^{2}}\,\log\,V,\qquad\widehat{\sigma}(\log\,V)=\,2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|\log\,V\|^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(\log V)}\,\log\,V.

(A.25)

We compute

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{X}\widehat{\sigma}(X).\,H,H\rangle=

\displaystyle\,2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|X\|^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}[2k\langle X,H\rangle-\textnormal{tr}(H)]\langle X,H\rangle+2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|X\|^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\|H\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|X\|^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\{2\,k\,\langle X,H\rangle^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(H)\langle X,H\rangle+\|H\|^{2}\}.

(A.26)

If
\textnormal{tr}(H)\langle X,H\rangle<0
, then obviously
\langle\mathrm{D}_{X}\widehat{\sigma}(X).\,H,H\rangle>0
. Otherwise, for
k\geq\frac{3}{8}
it follows

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{X}\widehat{\sigma}(X).\,H,H\rangle\geq

\displaystyle\,2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|X\|^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\{2k\langle X,H\rangle^{2}-2\,\sqrt{\frac{2k}{3}}\textnormal{tr}(H)\langle X,H\rangle+\|H\|^{2}\}

(A.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|X\|^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\langle H-\sqrt{\frac{2\,k}{3}}\langle X,H\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},H-\sqrt{\frac{2k}{3}}\langle X,H\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2\,{\mu}\,e^{k\,\|X\|^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\left\|H-\sqrt{\frac{2\,k}{3}}\langle X,H\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\right\|^{2}\geq 0.

Moreover, for
k>\frac{3}{8}
we have
\langle\mathrm{D}_{X}\widehat{\sigma}(X).\,H,H\rangle>0
and the proof is complete.
∎

Corollary A.10
.

The Cauchy stress tensor corresponding to the energy
V\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\mu}{k}\,e^{k\,\|\log\,V\|^{2}}+\frac{\lambda}{2\widehat{k}}\,e^{\widehat{k}\,[{\rm tr}(\log\,V)]^{2}}
satisfies TSTS-M for
k\geq\frac{3}{8}
,
\widehat{k}\geq\frac{1}{8}
and
\mu,\lambda>0
and TSTS-M
++
for
k>\frac{3}{8}
,

\widehat{k}\geq\frac{1}{8}
(or
k\geq\frac{3}{8}
,
\widehat{k}>\frac{1}{8}
) and
\mu,\lambda>0
.

Proof.

From direct calculations we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{X}e^{\widehat{k}\,(\textnormal{tr}(X))^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\,\textnormal{tr}(X)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.\,H,H\rangle=e^{\widehat{k}\,(\textnormal{tr}(X))^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\{2\,\widehat{k}\,[\textnormal{tr}(X)]^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)+1\}\,[\textnormal{tr}(H)]^{2}.

(A.28)

Thus, if
\widehat{k}\geq\frac{1}{8}
, then

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{X}e^{\widehat{k}\,(\textnormal{tr}(X))^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\,\textnormal{tr}(X)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.\,H,H\rangle\geq e^{\widehat{k}\,(\textnormal{tr}(X))^{2}-\textnormal{tr}(X)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\,\textnormal{tr}(X)-1\right)^{2}[\textnormal{tr}(H)]^{2}\geq 0.

(A.29)

The above inequality is strict for
\widehat{k}>\frac{1}{8}
. The rest of the proof follows from the previous theorem.
∎

Conclusion.
For the exponentiated Hencky energy
V\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\mu}{k}\,e^{k\,\|\log\,V\|^{2}}+\frac{\lambda}{2\widehat{k}}\,e^{\widehat{k}\,[{\rm tr}(\log\,V)]^{2}}
our results are applicable, i.e. we have corotational stability

\displaystyle\sym\,\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}_{\exp-\textnormal{Hencky}}(\sigma)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\qquad\iff\qquad\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}_{\exp-\textnormal{Hencky}}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0\,.

(A.30)

However, the exponentiated Hencky energy is in general
not polyconvex

[
4
]
and
not LH-elliptic
. Hence it remains a
major open question
(cf.
[
68
]
) to

find a polyconvex (or LH-elliptic), isotropic and objective elastic energy such that the corotational stability postulate CSP is satisfied everywhere for the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
.

A.4
Hypoelasticity for a slightly compressible Neo-Hooke type model

Consider a
polyconvex
slightly compressible Neo-Hooke type solid with elastic energy in volumetric-isochoric decoupled form (the vol-iso split also goes back to Richter
[
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
]
and not to Flory
[
29
]
), i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\NH}(F)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{2}\,\left(\frac{\lVert F\rVert^{2}}{(\det F)^{\frac{2}{3}}}-3\right)+\kappa\,\mathrm{e}^{(\log\det F)^{2}},

(A.31)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\NH}(B)

\displaystyle=\mu\,(\det B)^{-\frac{5}{6}}\,\dev_{3}B+\kappa\,(\det B)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(\log\det B)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log\det B)^{2}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},

with the shear modulus
\mu>0
, the bulk modulus
\kappa
and
\dev_{3}X=X-\frac{1}{3}\textnormal{tr}(X)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
. Then the constitutive law for
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma_{\NH}(B)
is invertible, i.e. there is a function
\mathcal{F}^{-1}:\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym^{++}(3),\;\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma_{\NH})=B
. For the proof of this statement we use the following

Lemma A.11
.

Let
S
be a symmetric and trace free matrix, and
a>0
, then the system

\displaystyle\dev_{n}B=S,\qquad\det B=a,

(A.32)

has a unique solution
B\in{\rm Sym}^{++}(n)
for
n=2,3
.

Proof.

The proof will be given in
[
67
]
.
∎

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 6:
Picture of the one-dimensional slightly compressible Neo-Hooke type energy
W_{\NH}(\lambda)=\frac{9}{42}(\lambda^{\frac{4}{3}}+2\,\lambda^{-\frac{2}{3}}+\mathrm{e}^{(\log\lambda)^{2}}-4)
and the one-dimensional invertible Neo-Hooke Cauchy stress
\sigma_{\NH}(\lambda)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}W_{\NH}(\lambda)
, obtained from (
A.31
), by setting
F=\diag(\lambda,1,1)\implies B=\diag(\lambda^{2},1,1)
.

Next, we write

\displaystyle\sigma_{\NH}(B)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mu\,(\det B)^{-\frac{5}{6}}\,\dev_{3}B}_{I_{B}}+\underbrace{\kappa\,(\det B)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(\log\det B)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log\det B)^{2}}}_{II_{B}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},

(A.33)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\NH}(B)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\dev_{3}X}_{I_{X}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(X)}_{II_{X}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

and compare the components

\displaystyle I:

\displaystyle\qquad\mu\,(\det B)^{-\frac{5}{6}}\,\dev_{3}B

\displaystyle=\dev_{3}X\,,

(A.34)

\displaystyle II:

\displaystyle\qquad\kappa\,(\det B)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(\log\det B)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log\det B)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(X)\,.

Defining the function
