\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad f(t)=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(\log t)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log t)^{2}}\quad\textnormal{with}\quad f^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\,t^{-\frac{3}{2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log t)^{2}}\,\left(\left(\log t-\frac{1}{2}\right)+\frac{7}{4}\right)>0

(A.35)

we see (cf. Figure
7
), that
f
is bijective as function from
\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Thus, we obtain a unique positive value
\det B=g_{1}(X)
for any choice
X\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
in
\eqref{eqcomponents01}_{2}
. Inserting this “solution” for
\det B
into
\eqref{eqcomponents01}_{1}
determines
\dev_{3}B=g_{2}(X)
as function of
X
. Thus we search for solutions to the system

\displaystyle\dev_{3}B=g_{2}(X),\qquad\det B=g_{1}(X)>0,\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\textnormal{tr}(g_{2}(X))=0,

(A.36)

which by Lemma
A.11
has a unique solution, proving the invertibility of
\sigma_{\NH}(B)
. Note that we have not yet proven that
\det\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\neq 0
. This is true nevertheless but will be skipped here.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 7:
Illustration of the bijectivity of
f:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\;f(t)=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(\log t)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log t)^{2}}
.

Let us also check positive definiteness for
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma_{\NH})
. The Zaremba-Jaumann derivative of (
A.31
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma_{\NH}]=\mu\,(\det B)^{-\frac{5}{6}}

\displaystyle\left\{-\frac{5}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,\dev_{3}B+\dev_{3}(B\,D+D\,B)\right\}

(A.37)

\displaystyle+\kappa\,(\det B)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}\,(\log\det B)^{2}}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,\left(\left(\log\det B-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{7}{4}\right)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma_{\NH}).D\,,

where we used the formula (cf. (
2.38
))
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]
for its calculation.

In the upcoming calculation we prove that the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma_{\NH})
is not positive definite. It suffices to consider

\displaystyle\langle-\frac{5}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)

\displaystyle\dev_{3}B+\dev_{3}(B\,D+D\,B),D\rangle

(A.38)

\displaystyle=-\frac{5}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\left(B-\frac{1}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(B)\right)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+B\,D+D\,B-\frac{1}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(B\,D+D\,B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},D\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{5}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,\langle B,D\rangle+\frac{5}{9}\,\textnormal{tr}(B)\,(\textnormal{tr}(D))^{2}+2\,\langle B\,D,D\rangle-\frac{2}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(B\,D)\,\textnormal{tr}(D)

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{9}\,\textnormal{tr}(B)\,(\textnormal{tr}(D))^{2}+2\,\langle B\,D,D\rangle-\frac{7}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(B\,D)\,\textnormal{tr}(D),

since we will show next, that this expression is not bounded from below. For simplicity let us assume that
B
and
D
are only two-dimensional, as the three-dimensional case would follow similarly. Let
B=\diag(\alpha,\beta)
and
D=\diag(a,b)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{5}{9}\,\textnormal{tr}(B)\,(\textnormal{tr}(D))^{2}+2\,\langle B\,D,D\rangle-\frac{7}{3}\,\textnormal{tr}(B\,D)\,\textnormal{tr}(D)=\frac{5}{9}\,(\alpha+\beta)\,(a+b)^{2}+2\,(\alpha\,a^{2}+\beta\,b^{2})-\frac{7}{3}\,(a+b)(a\,\alpha+b\,\beta)

(A.39)

Next, we choose
a=10^{-10},\,b=9\,10^{-10}
and obtain

\displaystyle\frac{5}{9}\,(\alpha+\beta)

\displaystyle(a+b)^{2}+2\,(\alpha\,a^{2}+\beta\,b^{2})-\frac{7}{3}\,(a+b)(a\,\alpha+b\,\beta)

(A.40)

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{9}\,(\alpha+\beta)\,10^{-18}+2\,(\alpha\,10^{-100}+\beta\,81\,10^{-100})-\frac{7}{3}\,10^{-9}\,(10^{-10}\,\alpha+9\,10^{-10}\,\beta).

Assuming further, that
\alpha>0
is chosen small enough to be neglected, we are left with an expression in
\beta>0
, given by

\displaystyle\frac{5}{9}\,10^{-18}\,\beta+\underbrace{162\,10^{-100}}_{<(4/9)\,10^{-18}}\,\beta-21\,10^{-19}\,\beta<10^{-18}\,\beta-2.1\,10^{-18}\,\beta\xrightarrow{\beta\rightarrow\infty}-\infty,

(A.41)

so that by taking
\beta>0
sufficiently large, we can guarantee, that
\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma_{\NH}).D,D\rangle<0
for any choice of
\mu,\kappa>0
.

This shows that invertibility of
B\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(B)
and positive definiteness of the corresponding induced stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
are not related and positivity of
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
is even violated for the slightly compressible Neo-Hooke model (
A.31
) which happened to be polyconvex and LH-elliptic.
29
29

29

Note that LH-ellipticity alone is only sufficient for injectivity of the Cauchy stress tensor along rank-one connected lines
[
71
,
72
,
82
]
.

Remark A.12
.

Altmeyer et al.
[
1
, eq. (11)]
use their consistent hypo-elastic framework to provide a rate-formulation of a slightly compressible Mooney-Rivlin material with Lie-derivative
\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}
(the Truesdell rate), i.e. their constitutive law reads
[
1
, eq. 42]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}(\sigma)=\mathbb{H}^{\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}}(B).D\qquad\iff\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{\TR}(B).D,

(A.42)

where
\mathbb{H}^{\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}}
is derived from hyperelasticity by hand. No investigation of invertibility or positive definiteness of
\mathbb{H}^{\mathcal{L}_{v_{\varphi}}}=\mathbb{H}^{\TR}
is undertaken. In
[
23
]
, starting from hyperelasticity [cf.
[
90
,
108
]
], the rate-formulation for the Truesdell-rate in explicit form, also for the Mooney-Rivlin model, is presented, yielding

\displaystyle\hskip-12.0pt\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-L\,\sigma-\sigma\,L^{T}+\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]-(D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D)+\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\TR}(B).D\,.

(A.43)

Here, we can observe that the Truesdell rate, which is
non-corotational
, will not allow for a chain rule like formula (cf. Section
4.1
). Indeed, with
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=B\,\textnormal{tr}(D)
the chain rule would imply

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]-(D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D)+\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\overset{!}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\right]=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,\textnormal{tr}(D)]

(A.44)

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D-B\,\textnormal{tr}(D)]

\displaystyle=D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D-\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)

Inserting e.g.
\sigma(B)=B^{2}
and
D
with
\textnormal{tr}(D)=0
would then yield with
\mathrm{D}_{B}(B^{2}).H=B\,H+H\,B

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D-B\,\textnormal{tr}(D)]

\displaystyle=B\,[D\,B+B\,D]+[D\,B+B\,D]\,B

(A.45)

\displaystyle\neq D\,B^{2}+B^{2}\ D=D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D-\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,.

Thus a similar chain rule as in Section
4.1
does not apply to the Truesdell derivative. This implies that the possible positive definiteness of
\mathbb{H}^{\TR}(\sigma)
is, in general, unrelated to the positive definiteness of
\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)
, since

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)]=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\sigma}(\log B).

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]

(A.46)

\displaystyle-[D\,\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)+\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\,D]-\textnormal{tr}(D)\,\widehat{\sigma}(\log B)\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\TR}(B).D\,,

which supports, again, our choice of only considering corotational rates
\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
.

A.5
Monotonicity in
V
versus monotonicity in
\log V

In a uniaxial situation, we can always consider

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}(\log\lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sigma(\lambda),\qquad\sigma\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\widehat{\sigma}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\textnormal{differentiable}.

(A.47)

Then it is clear that
\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow\sigma(\lambda)
is invertible if and only if
\log\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log\lambda)
is invertible and

\displaystyle\lambda\rightarrow\sigma(\lambda)\ \ \textnormal{monotone}\ \ \iff\ \ \log\lambda\rightarrow\widehat{\sigma}(\log\lambda)\ \ \textnormal{monotone},\qquad\textnormal{since}\quad\sigma^{\prime}(\lambda)=\widehat{\sigma}^{\prime}(\log\lambda)\,\frac{1}{\lambda},\quad\lambda>0.

(A.48)

However, in the matrix setting the latter simple correspondence is lost as is shown next. This shows that the statement in
[
48
, eq. 50]
is incorrect, cf. the discussion in
[
66
]
.

A.5.1
Example:
\sigma
monotone in
V
does not imply
\widehat{\sigma}
monotone in
\log V

Consider the elastic energy (see also
[
100
]
)
\mathrm{W}(F)=2\,\mu\,\det V\,\bigl\{\textnormal{tr}(V)-4\bigr\}
, leading to the hyperelastic Cauchy stress

\sigma(V)\ =\ 2\,\mu\,\{(V-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})+\textnormal{tr}(V-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\}.

(A.49)

Obviously, this law is monotone in
V-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
since
\textnormal{tr}(\,)
is a linear function and thus it is also monotone in
V
.

Rewriting
\sigma(V)
in terms of
\log V
we obtain

\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)=\sigma(V)\ =V-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\textnormal{tr}(V-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\ =\ \exp(\log V)-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\textnormal{tr}\bigl(\exp(\log V)-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\bigr)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,.

(A.50)

Hence, for
S\in\Sym(3)
we define
\widetilde{\sigma}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
by

\widehat{\sigma}(S)=\exp(S)-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\textnormal{tr}\bigl(\exp(S)-{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\bigr)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,,

(A.51)

so that monotonicity of
\widehat{\sigma}
in
\log V
is now equivalent to monotonicity of
\widehat{\sigma}
in
S
, i.e.

\langle\widehat{\sigma}(S_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(S_{2}),S_{1}-S_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}>0\,.

(A.52)

Condition (
A.52
) implies monotonicity in principal Cauchy stresses versus principal (
\log
)-strains, so that we have

{\scriptsize\langle\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\sigma}_{1}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})&0&0\\
0&\!\!\!\!\!\widehat{\sigma}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\!\!\!\!\!&0\\
0&0&\widehat{\sigma}_{3}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\sigma}_{1}(\overline{s}_{1},\overline{s}_{2},\overline{s}_{3})&0&0\\
0&\!\!\!\!\!\widehat{\sigma}_{2}(\overline{s}_{1},\overline{s}_{2},\overline{s}_{3})\!\!\!\!\!&0\\
0&0&\widehat{\sigma}_{3}(\overline{s}_{1},\overline{s}_{2},\overline{s}_{3})\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}-\overline{s}_{1}&0&0\\
0&\!\!\!\!\!s_{2}-\overline{s}_{2}\!\!\!\!\!&0\\
0&0&s_{3}-\overline{s}_{3}\end{pmatrix}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}\ >\ 0}\,.

(A.53)

We may rewrite (
A.53
) in the form

\langle\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\sigma}_{1}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{3}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\sigma}_{1}(\overline{s}_{1},\overline{s}_{2},\overline{s}_{3})\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{2}(\overline{s}_{1},\overline{s}_{2},\overline{s}_{3})\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{3}(\overline{s}_{1},\overline{s}_{2},\overline{s}_{3})\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}-\overline{s}_{1}\\
s_{2}-\overline{s}_{2}\\
s_{3}-\overline{s}_{3}\end{pmatrix}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\ >\ 0\,.

(A.54)

Here, in terms of principal Cauchy stresses we have by setting
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\log\lambda_{1},\;y\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\log\lambda_{2},\;z\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\log\lambda_{3}

\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\sigma}_{1}(x,y,z)\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{2}(x,y,z)\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{3}(x,y,z)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{x}-1\\
e^{y}-1\\
e^{z}-1\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}e^{x}+e^{y}+e^{z}-3\\
e^{x}+e^{y}+e^{z}-3\\
e^{x}+e^{y}+e^{z}-3\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}2\,e^{x}+e^{y}+e^{z}-4\\
2\,e^{y}+e^{x}+e^{z}-4\\
2\,e^{z}+e^{x}+e^{y}-4\end{pmatrix}.

(A.55)

The Jacobian of
\widehat{\sigma}
is

\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}(x,y,z)\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}2\,e^{x}&e^{y}&e^{z}\\
e^{x}&2\,e^{y}&e^{z}\\
e^{x}&e^{y}&2\,e^{z}\end{pmatrix}\ \not\in\Sym(3)\,.

(A.56)

From (
A.54
) we imply by application of the mean value theorem the inequality (with
\delta_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}s_{i}-\overline{s}_{i},\,i=1,2,3
)

\langle\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}(x,y,z).\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}\\
\delta_{2}\\
\delta_{3}\end{pmatrix}\!,\!\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}\\
\delta_{2}\\
\delta_{3}\end{pmatrix}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\ >\ 0\qquad\iff\qquad\langle\bigl[\sym\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}(x,y,z)\bigr].\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}\\
\delta_{2}\\
\delta_{3}\end{pmatrix}\!,\!\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}\\
\delta_{2}\\
\delta_{3}\end{pmatrix}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\ >\ 0\,,

(A.57)

since the skew-symmetric part of a matrix
X
always fulfills
\langle\textnormal{skew}(X).v,v\rangle=0
for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Hence, if
\widehat{\sigma}
was monotone in
S
, it should hold that
\sym\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}(x,y,z)
is positive definite, where

\sym\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}(x,y,z)\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}2\,e^{x}&\frac{1}{2}(e^{y}+e^{x})&\frac{1}{2}(e^{z}+e^{x})\\
\frac{1}{2}(e^{y}+e^{x})&2\,e^{y}&\frac{1}{2}(e^{z}+e^{y})\\
\frac{1}{2}(e^{x}+e^{z})&\frac{1}{2}(e^{y}+e^{z})&2\,e^{z}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(A.58)

However, according to the Sylvester criterion for positive definiteness of (
A.58
) is

\displaystyle 2\,e^{x}

\displaystyle>0,

\displaystyle\det\begin{pmatrix}2\,e^{x}&\frac{1}{2}(e^{y}+e^{x})\\
\frac{1}{2}(e^{y}+e^{x})&2\,e^{y}\end{pmatrix}\ =4\,e^{x}\,e^{y}-\frac{1}{4}[e^{y}\,e^{y}+2\,e^{y}\,e^{x}+e^{x}\,e^{x}]

\displaystyle>0,

(A.59)

\displaystyle\det\sym\mathrm{D}\widehat{\sigma}\

\displaystyle>0,

so that, by choosing
y=0
and
x=\log 20
, we have that

\displaystyle 4\,e^{x}\,e^{y}-\frac{1}{4}[e^{y}\,e^{y}+2\,e^{y}\,e^{x}+e^{x}\,e^{x}]=80-\frac{1}{4}[1+40+400]<80-\frac{1}{4}\,400<0

(A.60)

and therefore the positive definiteness of
\sym\mathrm{D}\widetilde{\sigma}(x,y,z)
is violated, which is equivalent to the statement that
\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)
is not monotone in
\log V
.

A.5.2
Example:
\widehat{\sigma}
monotone in
\log V
does not imply
\sigma
monotone in
V

Consider the constitutive Cauchy stress law
[
36
]

\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)\ =\ 2\,\mu\,\log V+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(\log V)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,.

(A.61)

For
\mu>0
,
\lambda>0
, this expression is clearly strictly monotone in
\log V
, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}\ >\ 0\qquad\forall\,V_{1},V_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3),\qquad V_{1}\neq V_{2}\,.

(A.62)

However,
\sigma(V)=\widehat{\sigma}(\log V)
is not monotone in
V
, i.e.

\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{2}),V_{1}-V_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}\ =\ \langle\sigma(V_{1})-\sigma(V_{2}),V_{1}-V_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}\ >\ 0

(A.63)

does not hold
for every
V_{1},V_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3),\;V_{1}\neq V_{2}
, if
\mu,\lambda>0
.

To see this, assume that
\mu
is small
0<\mu\ll 1
and let
\lambda>0
be arbitrary. Then we can, for now, neglect the first term in (
A.61
) by smallness of
\mu
, so that (
A.63
) corresponds approximately to

\langle\textnormal{tr}(\log V_{1})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-\textnormal{tr}(\log V_{2})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},V_{1}-V_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}\ \geq\ 0\,.

(A.64)

By choosing
V_{1}=\diag(\beta,\frac{1}{\beta},1),\;V_{2}=\diag(\overline{\lambda}_{1},\overline{\lambda}_{2},1)
we get

\displaystyle\langle\textnormal{tr}(\log V_{1})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

\displaystyle-\textnormal{tr}(\log V_{2})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},V_{1}-V_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}\ =\ \langle 0-\log(\overline{\lambda}_{1}\,\overline{\lambda}_{2}\,1)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},V_{1}-V_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}

(A.65)

\displaystyle\quad=

\displaystyle-\log(\overline{\lambda}_{1}\,\overline{\lambda}_{2})\,\bigl[\textnormal{tr}(V_{1})-\textnormal{tr}(V_{2})\bigr]\ =\ -\log(\overline{\lambda}_{1}\,\overline{\lambda}_{2})\,\bigl[\beta+\frac{1}{\beta}+1-(\overline{\lambda}_{1}+\overline{\lambda}_{2}+1)\bigr]

\displaystyle\quad=

\displaystyle-\log(\overline{\lambda}_{1}\,\overline{\lambda}_{2})\,\bigl[\underbrace{\beta+\frac{1}{\beta}}_{\geq 2}\ -\ (\overline{\lambda}_{1}+\overline{\lambda}_{2})\bigr]\,.

Now we choose
\beta=3
,
\overline{\lambda}_{1}=1
,
\overline{\lambda}_{2}=2
in (
A.65
) and obtain

\langle\textnormal{tr}(\log V_{1})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}-\textnormal{tr}(\log V_{2})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},V_{1}-V_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}\ =-\bigl[3+\frac{1}{3}-1-2\bigr]\log 2\,=\ -\frac{1}{3}\,\log 2\ <\ 0\,.

(A.66)

Furthermore, calculating the
\mu
-part of (
A.61
), which we neglected so far, yields

\displaystyle\langle\log V_{1}-\log V_{2},V_{1}-V_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}

\displaystyle=(\log\beta-\log\overline{\lambda}_{1})(\beta-\overline{\lambda}_{1})+(-\log\beta-\log\overline{\lambda}_{2})\left(\frac{1}{\beta}-\overline{\lambda}_{2}\right)

(A.67)

\displaystyle=2\,\log 3-\left(\frac{1}{3}-2\right)\log 6=2\,\log 18-\frac{1}{3}\log 6.

Thus, for this choice, we have

\langle\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{1})-\widehat{\sigma}(\log V_{2}),V_{1}-V_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}}=2\,\mu\left(2\log 18-\frac{1}{3}\log 6\right)-\lambda\,\frac{1}{3}\log 2,

(A.68)

which is negative if
2\,\mu<\lambda\,\frac{\log\sqrt[3]{2}}{\log\frac{18^{2}}{\sqrt[3]{6}}}
, contradicting (
A.63
).

A.6
Second order work condition versus corotational stability

In linear elasticity, the stored elastic energy can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)=\int_{V_{0}}\mathrm{W}^{\lin}(\varepsilon(t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}=\int_{V_{0}}\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\mathbb{C}.\varepsilon(t),\varepsilon(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}\qquad\textnormal{``work''}.

(A.69)

Taking time derivatives yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{E}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\mathrm{D}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{W}^{\lin}(\varepsilon(t)),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\sigma(t),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}=\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{int}}^{\lin}\qquad\textnormal{``internal power''}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\mathcal{E}(t)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textnormal{``second}\\
\textnormal{order work''}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\dot{\sigma}(t),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle+\langle\sigma(t),\ddot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}\overset{\sigma\in\Sym(3)}{=}\int_{V_{0}}\langle\dot{\sigma}(t),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle+\langle\sigma(t),\mathrm{D}u_{tt}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}

(A.70)

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\dot{\sigma}(t),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle-\langle\underbrace{\textnormal{Div}\,\sigma(t)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\;0\;\textnormal{in}\\
\textnormal{equilibrium}\end{subarray}},u_{tt}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\dot{\sigma}(t),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\mathbb{C}.\dot{\varepsilon}(t),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}\geq c^{+}\,\lVert\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rVert^{2}_{V_{0}}.

Hence positive “second order work” in linear elasticity

\displaystyle\int_{V_{0}}\langle\dot{\sigma}(t),\dot{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}>0

(A.71)

is sufficient for having a stable (local) equilibrium and expresses nothing else than
\mathbb{C}\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
for the constitutive law
\sigma=\mathbb{C}.\varepsilon
.

In nonlinear elasticity we have
\mathcal{E}(t)=\int_{V_{0}}\mathrm{W}(F(t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}
with (recall
\sigma=\frac{1}{J}\,S_{1}\,F^{T}
and
D=\sym L=\sym(\dot{F}\,F^{-1})
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{E}(t)=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\mathrm{D}_{F}\mathrm{W}(F(t)),\dot{F}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{0}}\langle S_{1}(t),\dot{F}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}=\int_{V_{0}}\langle S_{1}\,F^{T},\dot{F}\,F^{-1}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}

(A.72)

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{0}}\langle\sigma,L\rangle\,\underbrace{J\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}}_{=\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}}\overset{\sigma\in\Sym(3)}{=}\int_{V_{t}}\langle\sigma,D\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}=\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{int}}

and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\mathcal{E}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{t}}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D(t)\rangle+\langle\sigma(t),\dot{D}(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}=\int_{V_{t}}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D(t)\rangle+\langle\sigma(t),\mathrm{D}_{\xi}v_{,t}(\xi,t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}

(A.73)

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{t}}\underbrace{\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D(t)\rangle}_{\textnormal{not objective!}}-\langle\underbrace{\textnormal{Div}_{\xi}\,\sigma(t)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\;0\;\textnormal{in spatial}\\
\textnormal{equilibrium}\end{subarray}},v_{,t}(\xi,t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}=\int_{V_{t}}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}.

Thus, we observe the concordance

linear elasticity

nonlinear elasticity

\displaystyle\int_{V_{0}}\langle\sigma,\varepsilon\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}\quad

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow\quad\int_{V_{0}}\mathrm{W}(F(t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}

“energy/work” (objective)

\displaystyle\int_{V_{0}}\langle\sigma,\dot{\varepsilon}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}\quad

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow\quad\int_{V_{t}}\langle\sigma,D\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}=\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{int}}

“internal power”, “rate of work” (objective)

\displaystyle\int_{V_{0}}\langle\dot{\sigma},\dot{\varepsilon}\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{0}\quad

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow\quad\int_{V_{t}}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma],D\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}

“second order work” (not objective).

Therefore, the “second order work” condition
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\mathcal{E}(t)>0
in equilibrium for finite strain can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\mathcal{E}(t)=\int_{V_{t}}\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(t)],D(t)\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}>0

(A.74)

and “almost” looks like

\displaystyle\int_{V_{t}}\underbrace{\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(t)],D(t)\rangle}_{\textnormal{objective!}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}V_{t}>0.

(A.75)

The local corotational stability requirement

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(t)],D(t)\rangle>0\qquad\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\setminus\{0\}

(A.76)

must therefore not be confused with
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\mathcal{E}(t)>0
, the positive second order work.

A.7
Further calculus with the Zaremba-Jaumann rate

Recall the formulas

\displaystyle\boxed{\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,W-W\,\sigma=Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q^{T}\,\sigma\,Q]\,Q^{T}\quad\textnormal{for $Q(t)\in{\rm O}(3)$ so that}\;W=\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)}

(A.77)

(where we set
Q\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q^{W}
by abuse of notation for simplicity) and

\displaystyle\boxed{\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-W\,\sigma+\sigma\,W\overset{\eqref{eqratetype5}}{=}\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[D\,B+B\,D]=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(B).D}

(A.78)

for the derivation of the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative, which are valid for any isotropic tensor function
\sigma\colon\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\Sym(3)
.

The representation (
A.78
) can be used to show the frame-indifference of the Zaremba-Jaumann rate in the following manner.

Lemma A.13

(Frame-indifference of the Zaremba-Jaumann rate)
.

The Zaremba-Jaumann rate is frame-indifferent, i.e. under the transformation
F(t)\mapstochar\rightarrow Q(t)\,F(t)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q(t)\,\sigma(t)\,Q^{T}(t)]=Q(t)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}(t).

(A.79)

Proof.

We denote the image of the transformation
F(t)\mapstochar\rightarrow F^{*}(t)=Q(t)\,F(t)
by a star, for example
\sigma^{*}=Q\,\sigma\,Q^{T}
. Then we first observe that for
L=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}
we obtain

\displaystyle L^{*}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}F^{*}\,(F^{*})^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q\,F]\,(F^{-1}\,Q^{T})=(\dot{Q}\,F+Q\,\dot{F})\,F^{-1}\,Q^{T}=\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+Q\,L\,Q^{T}

(A.80)

so that for the skew-symmetric part
W=\textnormal{skew}L
of
L
we obtain the identity

\displaystyle W^{*}=\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+Q\,W\,Q^{T}=\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+Q\,W\,Q^{T}\qquad\iff\qquad W^{*}-\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}=Q\,W\,Q^{T}\,,

(A.81)

since
\textnormal{skew}(\,)
is an isotropic tensor function. Then a direct computation shows

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma^{*}(t)]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q(t)\,\sigma(t)\,Q^{T}(t)]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q\,\sigma\,Q^{T}]-W^{*}\,\sigma^{*}+\sigma^{*}\,W^{*}

\displaystyle=\dot{Q}\,\sigma\,Q^{T}+Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}+Q\,\sigma\,\dot{Q}^{T}-W\,\sigma^{*}+\sigma^{*}\,W

\displaystyle=\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}\,\underbrace{Q\,\sigma\,Q^{T}}_{\sigma^{*}}+Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}+\underbrace{Q\,\sigma\,Q^{T}}_{\sigma^{*}}\,\underbrace{Q\,\dot{Q}^{T}}_{=\;-\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}}-W^{*}\,\sigma^{*}+\sigma^{*}\,W^{*}

(A.82)

\displaystyle=Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}-(W^{*}-\dot{Q}\,Q^{T})\,\sigma^{*}+\sigma^{*}\,(W^{*}-\dot{Q}\,Q^{T})

\displaystyle{=}Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}-Q\,W\,\sigma\,Q^{T}+Q\,\sigma\,W\,Q^{T}\overset{\eqref{ZJrate01A}}{=}Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}\,.\qed

Corollary A.14
.

A corotational rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}
is objective if and only if the spin tensor
\Omega^{\circ}
transforms according to
\Omega^{\circ}\mapstochar\rightarrow\dot{Q}\,Q^{T}+Q\,\Omega^{\circ}\,Q^{T}
under a Euclidean transformation
F\mapstochar\rightarrow Q(t)\,F(t)
.

Furthermore, by applying (
A.78
) to the identity
\textnormal{id}:B\mapstochar\rightarrow B
we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\mathrm{D}_{B}B.[B\,D+D\,B]=\textnormal{id}.[B\,D+D\,B]=[B\,D+D\,B]=2\,\sym(B\,D),

(A.83)

leading to the estimate

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B],D\rangle=\langle B\,D+D\,B,D\rangle=2\,\langle B\,D,D\rangle\geq 2\,\lambda_{\min}(B)\,\lVert D\rVert^{2}.

(A.84)

Additionally, we note that

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B],D\rangle\geq c^{+}\,\lVert D\rVert^{2},

(A.85)

which has been shown in Lemma
A.26
. Another formula of interest is

\displaystyle\textnormal{tr}(\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B])=\textnormal{tr}\left(\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\log B]\right)=\textnormal{tr}(2\,D),

(A.86)

which is surprising, since we can show (see (
A.172
)), that in general

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]\neq 2\,D.

(A.87)

To prove the validity of (
A.86
), we use representation
\eqref{eqappendixowncalc001}_{4}
for
\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]
, which is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]=R\,\big[\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,D\,R)\big]\,R^{T}.

(A.88)

We readily check, that every diagonal element of
\mathcal{L}=X\,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{F}\,X
, where

\displaystyle X=\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2})\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}}&\qquad\textnormal{if}\quad i=j,\\
\frac{\log\lambda_{i}^{2}-\log\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}^{2}-\lambda_{j}^{2}}&\qquad\textnormal{if}\quad i\neq j,\end{array}\right.

has the value
2
, so that
\mathcal{L}\circ{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=2\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
. Then it easily follows

\displaystyle\textnormal{tr}(\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B])

\displaystyle=\langle R\,\big[\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,D\,R)\big]\,R^{T},{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,D\,R),{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\rangle

(A.91)

\displaystyle=\langle R^{T}\,D\,R,\mathcal{L}\circ{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\rangle=\langle R^{T}\,D\,R,2\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\rangle=\textnormal{tr}(2\,D).

A similar phenomenon involving the logarithmic strain is described by Lemma
A.32
and compare to Appendix
A.8.12
.

A.8
Supplementary material

In this supplementary part we gather results that are more or less known, but helpful for a better understanding of the paper.

A.8.1
Remarks about zero-grade hypo-elasticity

In a stress-free state of a hypo-elastic material, the Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
is equal to zero (
\sigma\equiv 0
). When loaded in this state, the following linear relationship
30
30

30

From Ludwig Prandtl
[
95
]
: “[the] elastic response depends only on the increment in the loading and not on prestresses” - nur “elastisch bestimmt” (elastic determination). This requirement of Prandtl may suitably be interpreted as zero-grade hypoelasticity, i.e.
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D
is
independent
of the current stress level
\sigma
. In order to be consistent with hyperelasticity, one has to use, however, the logarithmic rate and apply it to the Kirchhoff stress
\tau
, then
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\tau]=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D
integrates to

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\textnormal{Hencky}}(F)=\mu\,\lVert\log V\rVert^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\,\textnormal{tr}^{2}(\log V),\qquad\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\tau]=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D\qquad\textnormal{(cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{NeffGhibaLankeit}{}{}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{NeffGhibaPoly}{}{}]})}.

(A.92)

When using
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D
, then this constitutive prescription integrates to
\sigma=2\,\mu\,\log V+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(\log V)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
, which is not hyperelastic. This was already known to Hencky.

is assumed:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(0).D=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D=2\mu\,D+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

(A.93)

This expression is independent of the choice of the objective time derivative
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
used for the stresses
31
31

31

In the paper
[
107
]
the authors discuss the philosophical issues connected with the use of the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative and hypo-elasticity with respect to integrability from scratch. Here, to the contrary, for the choice
\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
no problems of appropriate choice of rates or conservativeness appear since we are always consistent with hyperelasticity or Cauchy-elasticity by definition of the induced tangent stiffness
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma)
.

(cf. Section
4.3
).
Since there is an isotropic tensor function on the right side of equation (
A.93
), the stresses evolve from a stress-free state under small deformations initially resembling those in a linear isotropic material. The model (
A.93
) is called hypo-elasticity of zero-grade (
\mathbb{H}(0)=
const.). Regarding zero-grade hypo-elasticity, we have the following excerpt from Truesdell
[
121
, p.123]
:

“The classical linear theory of elasticity is defined by the constitutive equation

\displaystyle\textnormal{small stress}\;=\;f\textnormal{(small strain from an unstressed state)}.

(A.94)

Here, as henceforth, we are taking twice the shear modulus
\mu
for the particular material as the unit of stress. The usual form of the classical finite strain theory extends (
A.94
) by the more general constitutive equation

\displaystyle\textnormal{stress}\;=\;f\textnormal{(strain from an unstressed state).}

(A.95)

While the last few years have brought physical confirmation to the finite strain theory for rubber, there remain many physical materials which are linearly elastic under small enough strain but which in large strain behave in a fashion the finite strain theory is not intended to represent. Now the linear theory of elasticity embodied in (
A.95
), while in one sense more general, in another is far more restrictive than that employed in (
A.94
). It asserts that no matter how violent the distortion, the body responds to it only with reference to the unstressed state. The body has a memory for its initial state only, being utterly oblivious of all intermediate stages. This is true of the linear theory, too, but with the restriction that the initial state must be a very near one. It is much less to expect of a material that it remember where it has just been than where it was long ago. Thus we may prefer to generalize (
A.94
) by

\displaystyle\textnormal{stress increment}\;=\;f\textnormal{(small strain from the immediately preceding state).}

(A.96)

When the immediately preceding state is unstressed, (
A.96
) reduces to (
A.94
). Thus (
A.96
) and (
A.95
) represent two essentially different ideas of spring, both yielding as a common first approximation the classical linear theory for small strains
[
64
]
from an unstressed state.

When we come to realize (
A.96
) mathematically, it is natural to begin with

\displaystyle\textnormal{rate of stress}\;=\;f\textnormal{(rate of deformation)''}.

(A.97)

Conversely, a material that reacts anisotropically rather than isotropically to small deformations from a stress-free state cannot be hypo-elastic in the above sense.

A.8.2
Some objective derivatives

Some possible objective derivatives
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
of the Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
, not necessarily corotational, but all invariant under a Euclidean transformation of the observer, are listed below:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,W-W\,\sigma=Q\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q^{T}\,\sigma\,Q]\,Q^{T}

(A.98)

\displaystyle\;\textnormal{for $Q(t)\in{\rm O}(3)$ so that}\;\dot{Q}=W\,Q\hskip 8.50012pt(\textnormal{corotational Zaremba-Jaumann derivative}),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\Old}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-(L\,\sigma+\sigma\,L^{T})\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{(non-corotational convective contravariant Oldroyd derivative \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{oldroyd1950}{}{}]})},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\textnormal{Hencky}}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,W-W\,\sigma+\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{(non-corotational Biezino-Hencky \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{biezeno1928}{}{}]} derivative,}

(A.99)

sometimes also called Hill-rate (cf.
[
55
]
)),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\TR}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-(L\,\sigma+\sigma\,L^{T})+\sigma\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{(non-corotational Truesdell derivative \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{truesdellremarks}{}{}, eq.\penalty\ 3]}),}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega-\Omega\,\sigma=R\,\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[R^{T}\,\sigma\,R]\,R^{T},\hskip 17.00024ptR\in{\rm O}(3),\hskip 17.00024ptF=R\,U

\displaystyle\;\textnormal{with the ``polar spin''}\,\Omega\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\dot{R}(t)\,R^{T}(t)\in\mathfrak{so}(3)\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{(corotational Green-Naghdiderivative [cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bellini2015}{}{}]}])},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\log}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\;\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,\Omega^{\log}+\Omega^{\log}\,\sigma\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{(corotational logarithmic derivative)}.

These objective time derivatives all satisfy frame-indifference in the sense that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[Q(t)\,\sigma(t)\,Q(t)]=Q(t)\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\sharp}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]\,Q^{T}(t),\hskip 17.00024ptF\mapstochar\rightarrow Q(t)\,F(t),

(A.100)

while the material rate
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
does not satisfy this transformation law.

A.8.3
From hyperelasticity to hypo-elasticity

For isotropic hyperelastic materials, the Cauchy stress
\sigma
can be expressed in terms of the invariants of the left Cauchy-Green deformation tensor B (or right Cauchy-Green deformation tensor). If the strain energy density function is
\mathrm{W}(F)=\widehat{\mathrm{W}}(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
then we can write e.g.

\displaystyle\sigma(B)=2\sqrt{I_{3}}\,\frac{\partial\widehat{\mathrm{W}}}{\partial I_{3}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{I_{3}}}\left(\frac{\partial\widehat{\mathrm{W}}}{\partial I_{1}}+I_{1}\frac{\partial\widehat{\mathrm{W}}}{\partial I_{2}}\right)\,B-\frac{2}{\sqrt{I_{3}}}\frac{\partial\widehat{\mathrm{W}}}{\partial I_{2}}\,B^{2}=\varphi_{0}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\varphi_{1}\,B+\varphi_{2}\,B^{2}\,,

(A.101)

where
I_{k},\,k=1,2,3
denote the principal invariants of the left Cauchy-Green tensor
B\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}F\,F^{T}
. The coefficients
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
are scalar, isotropic functions of the principal invariants or other invariants of the left Cauchy-Green tensor
B
. Due to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}(F(x,t)\,F^{T}(x,t))=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}\,F\,F^{T}+F\,F^{T}\,F^{-T}\,\dot{F}^{T}=L\,B+B\,L^{T},\hskip 17.00024ptL\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\dot{F}\,F^{-1},

(A.102)

we have

\displaystyle\dot{\varphi}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\varphi_{i}=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{i},\dot{B}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{i},(L\,B+B\,L^{T})\rangle

(A.103)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{i}\,B,L\rangle+\langle B\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{i},L^{T}\rangle\overset{\textnormal{isotropy}}{=}\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{i}\,B,L\rangle+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{i}\,B,L^{T}\rangle=2\,\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{i}\,B,D\rangle.

The derivative of the Cauchy stress tensor
\sigma
with respect to time is therefore obtained as:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\dot{\varphi}_{0}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\dot{\varphi}_{1}\,B+\varphi_{1}\,\dot{B}+\dot{\varphi_{2}}\,B^{2}+\varphi_{2}\,\dot{B}\,B+\varphi_{2}\,B\,\dot{B}

(A.104)

\displaystyle=2\,\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{0}(B),D\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+2\,\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{1}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B+\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})

\displaystyle+2\,\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{2}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B^{2}+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})\,B+\varphi_{2}\,B\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})

\displaystyle=2\,\underbrace{\{\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{0}(B),D\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{1}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{2}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B^{2}\}}_{\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}A}

\displaystyle+\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})\,B+\varphi_{2}\,B\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})

\displaystyle=2A+\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B^{2}+B\,L^{T}\,B+B\,L\,B+B^{2}\,L^{T})

\displaystyle=2A+\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B^{2}+B^{2}\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(B\,L^{T}\,B+B\,L\,B)

\displaystyle=2A+\underbrace{\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B^{2}+B^{2}\,L^{T})}_{=L\,\sigma+\sigma\,L^{T}-2\varphi_{0}\,D}+2\varphi_{2}\,B\,D\,B\,,

where we used that from
\sigma=\varphi_{0}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\varphi_{1}\,B+\varphi_{2}\,B^{2}
, we also have

\displaystyle L\,\sigma+\sigma\,L^{T}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{0}\,L+\varphi_{1}\,L\,B+\varphi_{2}\,L\,B^{2}+\varphi_{0}\,L^{T}+\varphi_{1}\,B\,L^{T}+\varphi_{2}\,B^{2}\,L^{T}

(A.105)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{0}\,(L+L^{T})+\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B^{2}+B^{2}\,L^{T})=2\,\varphi_{0}\,D+\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B^{2}+B^{2}\,L^{T})

and thus

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\,(L\,B+B\,L^{T})+\varphi_{2}\,(L\,B^{2}+B^{2}\,L^{T})=L\,\sigma+\sigma\,L^{T}-2\varphi_{0}\,D.

(A.106)

This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-(L\,\sigma+\sigma\,L^{T})

\displaystyle=2\,\{\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{0}(B),D\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{1}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{2}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B^{2}\}-2\varphi_{0}\,D+2\varphi_{2}\,B\,D\,B,

(A.107)

which means

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\Old}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=2\,\{\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{0}(B),D\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{1}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{2}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B^{2}\}-2\varphi_{0}\,D+2\varphi_{2}\,B\,D\,B\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(B).D\,.

(A.108)

If the stress-strain relationship is invertible, i.e.
\sigma=\mathcal{F}(B)\iff B=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)
we can write

\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\Old}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sigma)).D\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\sigma).D\,,

so that an isotropic, Cauchy-elastic material is also hypo-elastic in this case, as first demonstrated by Walter Noll
[
85
]
. Other procedures to obtain consistent rate equations from hyperelasticity are e.g. presented in Altmeyer et al.
[
1
]
, Panicaud et al.
[
90
]
and Romenskii
[
108
]
.

In a final step, for the reader’s convenience, we convert the Oldroyd rate further into the Zaremba-Jaumann rate.

A.8.4
Converting the Oldroyd rate into the corotational Zaremba-Jaumann rate

Recall that

\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\Old}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(t)]=\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-L\sigma-\sigma L^{T}}_{\textnormal{Oldroyd rate}}\implies\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\Old}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+L\sigma+\sigma L^{T},\hskip 17.00024pt\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(t)]=\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma W-W\sigma}_{\textnormal{Zaremba-Jaumann rate}}.

(A.109)

and therefore with
L=D+W

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\Old}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(t)]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-L\sigma-\sigma L^{T}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-(D+W)\sigma-\sigma(D+W)^{T}=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-W\sigma-\sigma W^{T}-D\sigma-\sigma D

(A.110)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma W-W\sigma-(D\sigma+\sigma D)=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-(D\sigma+\sigma D).

Hence, from
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\Old}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\sigma).D
we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]-(D\sigma+\sigma D)=\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\sigma).D\hskip 8.50012pt\iff\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\sigma).D+(D\sigma+\sigma D)\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D.

(A.111)

or equivalently, by using (
A.108
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)=2\,\{\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{0}(B),D\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{1}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B+\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\varphi_{2}(B)\,B,D\rangle\,B^{2}\}-2\varphi_{0}\,D+2\varphi_{2}\,B\,D\,B+D\,\sigma+\sigma\,D\,.

(A.112)

Observe, that it is virtually impossible to derive any constitutive conclusion from (
A.112
) combined with
\langle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D,D\rangle>0

\forall\,D\in\Sym(3)\!\setminus\!\{0\}
.

It is also worth emphasizing that invertibility of e.g.
\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\sigma)
at given
\sigma
is in general independent of the invertibility of
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
. By this we mean
\det\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\sigma)\neq 0\mathrel{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\not\phantom{"}$\cr$\iff$\crcr}}}}\det\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)\neq 0
. Similarly, the positive definiteness of
\mathbb{H}^{\Old}(\sigma)
is not connected to the positive definiteness of
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
.

A.8.5
Physical inadequacy of zero-grade hypo-elasticity in simple shear for the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative

In a first example we recall that zero-grade hypo-elasticity results in oscillating Cauchy shear-stress when using the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative. This was first observed by Dienes
[
16
]
. It is included here for the interested reader. The point to retain is that zero-grade hypo-elasticity is in general inconsistent but this inconsistency is not due to the Zaremba-Jaumann rate.

Example A.15

(Physical inadequacy of the zero-grade hypo-elasticity formulation)
.

Consider the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,W-W\,\sigma,

(A.113)

with the assumption of zero-grade hypo-elasticity

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{*}(\sigma).D=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D=2\,\mu\,D+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D),\hskip 8.50012pt\mu,\lambda>0,\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},

(A.114)

leading to the equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]+\sigma\,W-W\,\sigma=2\mu\,D+\lambda\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=\mathbb{C}^{\iso}.D\,.

(A.115)

Then we calculate for simple shear

\displaystyle F(t)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\,t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\hskip 17.00024pt\dot{F}(t)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\hskip 17.00024ptF^{-1}(t)=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\gamma\,t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle L(t)=\dot{F}\,F^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\hskip 17.00024ptD(t)=\sym L(t)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\
\gamma&0\end{pmatrix},\hskip 17.00024pt\mathrm{W}(t)=\textnormal{skew}\,L(t)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\
-\gamma&0\end{pmatrix},

so that
\textnormal{tr}(D)=0
. Now, we can determine
\sigma(t)=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{12}&\sigma_{22}\end{pmatrix}
by solving the system of ordinary differential equations given by (
A.115
):

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=2\mu\,D+W\,\sigma-\sigma\,W

(A.116)

\displaystyle\iff\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}^{\prime}&\sigma_{12}^{\prime}\\
\sigma_{12}^{\prime}&\sigma_{22}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mu\,\gamma\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\,\gamma\,\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{12}&\sigma_{22}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{12}&\sigma_{22}\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\,\gamma\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\,\gamma\,\begin{pmatrix}2\,\sigma_{12}&\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{11}\\
\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{11}&-2\,\sigma_{12}\end{pmatrix}.

This leads to the system of ordinary differential equations

\displaystyle\sigma_{11}^{\prime}=\gamma\,\sigma_{12},\hskip 17.00024pt\sigma_{22}^{\prime}=-\gamma\,\sigma_{12},\hskip 17.00024pt\sigma_{12}^{\prime}=\mu\,\gamma+\frac{1}{2}\,\gamma\,(\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{11}),

(A.117)

with the initial conditions (stress-free initial state)
\sigma_{11}(0)=\sigma_{12}(0)=\sigma_{22}(0)=0
. Adding the first two equations leads to

\displaystyle(\sigma_{11}+\sigma_{22})^{\prime}=0\hskip 17.00024pt\implies\hskip 17.00024pt\sigma_{11}+\sigma_{22}=\textnormal{const.}\hskip 17.00024pt\overset{\sigma_{11}(0)=\sigma_{22}(0)=0}{\implies}\hskip 17.00024pt\sigma_{11}(t)=-\sigma_{22}(t).

(A.118)

Inserting this into the third equation of (
A.117
) and differentiating the result yields

\displaystyle\sigma_{12}^{\prime\prime}=\gamma\,\sigma_{22}^{\prime}=-\gamma^{2}\,\sigma_{12}\hskip 17.00024pt\implies\hskip 17.00024pt\sigma_{12}^{\prime\prime}+\gamma^{2}\,\sigma_{12}=0,

(A.119)

which is well-known to have a trigonometric solution of the form
\sigma_{12}(t)=a\,\sin(\gamma\,t)+b\,\cos(\gamma\,t)
. Since
\sigma_{12}(0)=0
, we also must have
b=0
, so that the general solution is given by
\sigma_{12}(t)=a\,\sin(\gamma\,t)
for arbitrary
a\in\mathbb{R}
. This shows that the assumption of
zero-grade hypo-elasticity
together with the Zaremba-Jaumann derivative is
in general not sound
, since it produces oscillating Cauchy shear stress for monotone increasing simple shear.
35
35

35

Several authors have nevertheless insisted on using zero-grade hypo-elasticity by modifying the formulation. More precisely, they consider the Kirchhoff stress
\tau
and propose to use an adapted corotational rate, the so called logarithmic rate
[
131
,
132
,
134
]
, in order to obtain an integrable hypo-elastic formulation, leading exclusively to the Hencky-energy
[
77
]
. Contrary to this, in our framework, there is no immediate need to depart from the Zaremba-Jaumann rate since we start from hyperelasticity or Cauchy-elasticity and consider solely the induced tangent stiffness tensor
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
which is consistent by definition.

\hfill{\square}

A.8.6
Logarithmic monotonicity of the Cauchy stress
\sigma
for volumetric functions

In this section we derive the criterion for the monotonicity of the Cauchy stress
\sigma(V)
in
\log V
for a “perfectly compressible fluid”, i.e. we show that
\sigma(V)
satisfies

\displaystyle\langle\sigma(V_{1})-\sigma(V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle>0\hskip 17.00024pt\forall\,V_{1}\neq V_{2},\hskip 17.00024ptV_{1},V_{2}\in\Sym^{++}(3),

(A.120)

if the criterion is fulfilled. For such a perfect fluid, the energy function
\mathrm{W}
has the structure

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}(F)=\mathrm{W}_{\vol}(\det F)=h(\det F)

(A.121)

with a (smooth) function
h:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition A.16
.

Let
\mathrm{W}(F)=\mathrm{W}_{\vol}(\det F)=h(\det F)=\widehat{h}(\log\det F)
. Then the corresponding Cauchy stress
\sigma_{\vol}
satisfies the relation

\displaystyle\sigma_{\textnormal{vol}}\;\textnormal{monotone in}\;\log V\hskip 8.50012pt\iff\hskip 8.50012pt\widehat{h}^{\prime\prime}-\widehat{h}^{\prime}\geq 0\hskip 8.50012pt\iff\hskip 8.50012pth\;\textnormal{convex in}\;\det F.

(A.122)

Proof.

Since
F=V\,R
with
R\in{\rm O}(3)
, we have
\det F=\det V
so that
\mathrm{W}_{\vol}(\det F)=\mathrm{W}_{\vol}(\det V)
. Introducing
\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{\vol}(\log\det V)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{W}_{\vol}(\det V)
and
\widehat{h}(\log\det V)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}h(\det V)
respectively, we can derive
\sigma_{\vol}(V)
by the Richter formula
[
100
,
126
]

\displaystyle\sigma(V)=\sigma_{\vol}(\det V)=\frac{1}{\det V}\,\tau(\det V)=\frac{1}{\det V}\,\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{\vol}(\log\det V).

(A.123)

Since
\textnormal{tr}(\log V)=\log\det V
and
\mathrm{D}_{X}\textnormal{tr}(X)={\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
, we can calculate the derivative
\mathrm{D}_{\log V}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{\vol}(\log\det V)
explicitly by using
\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{\vol}(\log\det V)=\widehat{h}(\log\det V)=\widehat{h}(\textnormal{tr}(\log V))
, which yields

\displaystyle\sigma_{\vol}(\det V)=\frac{1}{\det V}\,\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\textnormal{tr}(\log V))\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

(A.124)

In a final step we set
\widehat{\xi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{tr}(\log V)
, introduce
\widehat{\sigma}_{\vol}(\log\det V)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sigma_{\vol}(\det V)
and observe

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{\vol}(\log\det V)=\widehat{\sigma}_{\vol}(\textnormal{tr}(\log V))=\widehat{\sigma}_{\vol}(\widehat{\xi})=\underbrace{\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{tr}(\log V)}}\,\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\textnormal{tr}(\log V))}_{=\;\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\widehat{\xi}}\;\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}\;g(\widehat{\xi})}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

(A.125)

With the help of (
A.125
) we can reformulate the left hand side of (
A.120
) (with
\widehat{\xi}_{i}=\textnormal{tr}(\log V_{i})
,
i=1,2
)

\displaystyle\langle\sigma(V_{1})-\sigma(V_{2}),\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle=\langle((g(\widehat{\xi}_{1})-g(\widehat{\xi}_{2}))\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},\log V_{1}-\log V_{2}\rangle=(g(\widehat{\xi}_{1})-g(\widehat{\xi}_{2}))\,(\widehat{\xi}_{1}-\widehat{\xi}_{2}),

(A.126)

showing that the monotonicity of
\sigma(V)=\sigma_{\vol}(\det V)=\widehat{\sigma}_{\vol}(\textnormal{tr}(\log V))
in
\log V
is equivalent to the monotonicity of the scalar function
g(\widehat{\xi})=\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\widehat{\xi}}
with respect to
\widehat{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}
. Calculating the derivative with respect to
\widehat{\xi}
results in the condition

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\widehat{\xi}}\left(\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\widehat{\xi}}\right)=(\widehat{h}^{\prime\prime}(\widehat{\xi})-\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}))\,\mathrm{e}^{-\widehat{\xi}}\geq 0\hskip 17.00024pt\iff\hskip 17.00024pt\widehat{h}^{\prime\prime}(\widehat{\xi})-\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi})\geq 0.

(A.127)

From this we can also derive a criterion for
h(\det V)=\widehat{h}(\log\det V)=\widehat{h}(\widehat{\xi})
. Setting
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\det V
, we obtain

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{h}(\log x)\hskip 17.00024pt\iff\hskip 17.00024pt\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}h(x)=h^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}\widehat{h}(\log x)=\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\log x)\,\frac{1}{x}

(A.128)

\displaystyle\iff\hskip 17.00024pth^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}\left(\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\log x)\,\frac{1}{x}\right)=\widehat{h}^{\prime\prime}(\log x)\,\frac{1}{x^{2}}-\widehat{h}^{\prime}\,\frac{1}{x^{2}}=\frac{1}{x^{2}}\,(\widehat{h}^{\prime\prime}(\log x)-\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\log x)).

Finally, inserting
x=\det V
we see that

\displaystyle\widehat{h}^{\prime\prime}(\widehat{\xi})-\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi})

\displaystyle\geq 0\hskip 17.00024pt\iff\hskip 17.00024pth^{\prime\prime}(\det V)\geq 0\qed

(A.129)

Remark A.17
.

Calculating the trace

\displaystyle\textnormal{tr}(\sigma_{\vol}(\det V))=\frac{3}{\det V}\,\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\textnormal{tr}(\log V))=3\,\widehat{h}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\widehat{\xi}},

(A.130)

we additionally see that for the mean pressure
\frac{1}{3}\textnormal{tr}(\sigma_{\vol})
the relation

\frac{1}{3}\textnormal{tr}(\sigma_{\textnormal{vol}})
invertible
\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma_{\vol}({\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}})\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)\\
\left.\right.\end{subarray}}{\iff}

\frac{1}{3}\textnormal{tr}(\sigma_{\textnormal{vol}})
monotone
\iff

h
convex in
\det V

holds true.

[formula]
[formula]
tr
[formula]

Figure 8:
Monotone volume-mean pressure
\alpha\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{1}{3}\textnormal{tr}(\sigma(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}))
. It is physically reasonable to expect that the mean pressure is an increasing function of the volumetric stretch for a stable elastic material.

Remark A.18
.

The condition of Legendre-Hadamard-ellipticity and monotonicity of
\sigma_{\vol}
in
\log V
coincide for a perfectly compressible fluid, i.e. in
\mathrm{W}_{\vol}(F)=h(\det F)
the function
h
must be convex, ensuring also the polyconvexity
[
4
]
of
\mathrm{W}_{\vol}
, see also Appendix
A.8.7
. This was already established by Leblond
[
60
]
. However, in general, polyconvexity and the corotational stability postulate (CSP) do not coincide as shown in
[
78
]
, see also the example concerning the exponential Hencky energy herein
[
78
, Section 4]
.

A.8.7
Legendre-Hadamard-ellipticity for functions of the type
F\mapstochar\rightarrow h(\det F)

This subsection is also taken from
[
78
]
.

We consider a function
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and analyze when the function
F\mapstochar\rightarrow h(\det F)
is LH-elliptic as a function of
F\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
. We recall that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}(\det F).H=\det F\,\textnormal{tr}(H\,F^{-1})=\langle\Cof F,H\rangle.

(A.131)

Using the first Frechét-formal derivative, we compute

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}(h(\det F)).(H,H)=h^{\prime}(\det F)\,\langle\Cof F,H\rangle

(A.132)

and the second derivative is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{2}(h(\det F)).(H,H)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime\prime}(\det F)\,\langle\Cof F,H\rangle^{2}+h^{\prime}(\det F)\langle\mathrm{D}(\Cof F).H,H\rangle

(A.133)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime\prime}(\det F)\,\langle\Cof F,H\rangle^{2}+h^{\prime}(\det F)\{\langle\langle\Cof F,H\rangle\,F^{-T},H\rangle+\det F\langle-F^{-T}H^{T}F^{-T},H\rangle\},

\displaystyle=h^{\prime\prime}(\det F)\,\langle\Cof F,H\rangle^{2}+h^{\prime}(\det F)\,\det F\{\langle F^{-T},H\rangle^{2}-\langle F^{-T}H^{T}F^{-T},H\rangle\}.

Hence, for
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{2}(h(

\displaystyle\det F)).((\xi\otimes\eta),(\xi\otimes\eta))

(A.134)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime\prime}(\det F)\,\langle\Cof F,(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle^{2}+h^{\prime}(\det F)\,\det F\{\langle F^{-T},(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle^{2}-\langle F^{-T}(\xi\otimes\eta)^{T}F^{-T},(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle\}.

Surprisingly, we also have the simplification

\displaystyle\langle F^{-T},(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle^{2}-

\displaystyle\langle F^{-T}(\xi\otimes\eta)^{T}F^{-T},(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle=\langle{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},F^{-1}(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle^{2}-\langle(\eta\otimes F^{-1}\xi),(F^{-1}\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},F^{-1}(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle^{2}-\langle(F^{-1}\xi\otimes\eta)^{T},(F^{-1}\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle=\langle F^{-1}\xi,\eta\rangle^{2}-\langle F^{-1}\xi,\eta\rangle^{2}=0,

leading to

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{2}(h(\det F)).

\displaystyle(\xi\otimes\eta,\xi\otimes\eta)=h^{\prime\prime}(\det F)\,\langle\Cof F,(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle^{2}.

(A.135)

In conclusion,
F\mapstochar\rightarrow h(\det F)
is LH-elliptic if and only if
t\mapstochar\rightarrow h(t)
is convex since
\langle\Cof F,(\xi\otimes\eta)\rangle^{2}
is
positive.

From
[
13
, page 213]
we additionally have for elastic fulids

Proposition A.19
.

For
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathrm{W}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be given by
\mathrm{W}(F)=h(\det F).
Then

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38109pt\mathrm{W}\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{polyconvex}\hskip 8.50012pt\iff\hskip 8.50012pt\mathrm{W}\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{quasiconvex}\hskip 8.50012pt\iff\hskip 8.50012pt\mathrm{W}\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{rank one convex}\hskip 8.50012pt\iff\hskip 8.50012pth\hskip 8.50012pt\textnormal{convex}.

(A.136)

A.8.8
Hypo-elasticity for the perfect elastic fluid

We will demonstrate by a direct calculation that
\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma)
is positive definite if and only if
h(x)
is a convex function.

Since
\det V=\sqrt{\det B}
, we have
\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(B)=h(\sqrt{\det B})
leading to the Cauchy stress

\sigma(B)=\frac{2}{J}\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(B)\,B=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\det B}}\,h^{\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\frac{1}{2\,\sqrt{\det B}}\,\det B\,B^{-1}\,B=h^{\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

(A.137)

Knowing
\sigma(B)
we can calculate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
according to formula (
2.38
) from Section
2.1
and we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\ZJ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[B\,D+B\,D]=\langle\frac{1}{2}\,h^{\prime\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\sqrt{\det B}\,B^{-1},B\,D+D\,B\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

(A.138)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\sqrt{\det B}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}.

Equivalently, we can use (
2.50
) to calculate
\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]
from
\sigma(B)
with
B=V^{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D=\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\GN}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma]

\displaystyle=2\,\mathrm{D}_{B}\sigma(B).[V\,D\,V]=2\,\langle\frac{1}{2}\,h^{\prime\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\sqrt{\det B}\,B^{-1},V\,D\,V\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

(A.139)

\displaystyle=h^{\prime\prime}(\sqrt{\det B})\,\sqrt{\det B}\,\textnormal{tr}(D)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}},

showing that
\mathbb{H}^{\GN}(\sigma).D=\mathbb{H}^{\ZJ}(\sigma).D
for the perfect elastic fluid and in fact the same representation is true for an arbitrary corotational rate
\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D
.

Remark A.20
.

If, in general, we have the Cauchy stress
\sigma(B)
given in the form
\sigma(B)=\alpha(B)\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
, where
\alpha:\Sym^{++}(3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a scalar-valued isotropic function, we obtain (with
\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]=L\,B+B\,L^{T}
) for any corotational rate

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\circ}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(B)]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[\sigma(B)]=\langle\mathrm{D}\alpha(B),\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}[B]\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=\langle\mathrm{D}\alpha(B),(L\,B+B\,L^{T})\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

(A.140)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}\alpha(B)\,B,L\rangle+\langle B\,\mathrm{D}\alpha(B),L^{T}\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\overset{\alpha\,\textnormal{isotropic}}{=}\langle\mathrm{D}\alpha(B)\,B,L\rangle+\langle\mathrm{D}\alpha(B)\,B,L^{T}\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{D}\alpha(B)\,B,L+L^{T}\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=2\,\langle\mathrm{D}\alpha(B)\,B,D\rangle\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}=\mathbb{H}^{\circ}(\sigma).D\,.

A.8.9
The Schur product

We recall
[
44
]
that the Schur (or Hadamard) product of two matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
is denoted by
(A\circ B)\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
and it is defined entrywise as
(A\circ B)_{ij}=A_{ij}\,B_{ij}
. This product will be used for the Daleckii-Krein formula in the upcoming section.

Lemma A.21

(Properties of the Schur product)
.

We have the following properties:

1.

symmetry: Let
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, then

\displaystyle A\circ B=B\circ A,\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 17.00024pt(A\circ B)^{T}=A^{T}\circ B^{T}\,.

(A.141)

2.

distributive property: Let
A,B,C\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, then

\displaystyle A\circ(B+C)=A\circ B+A\circ C,\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 17.00024pt(A+B)\circ C=A\circ C+B\circ C\,.

(A.142)

3.

positive definiteness: Let
A,B\in\Sym^{+}(n)
, then
A\circ B\in\Sym^{+}(n)
and

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(A\circ B)\geq\lambda_{\min}(B)\,\underset{1\leq i\leq n}{\min}\,a_{ii}\,.

(A.143)

4.

Let
\diag\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a diagonal matrix and
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, then

\displaystyle\diag\,(A\circ B)

\displaystyle=(\diag\,A)\circ B=A\circ(\diag B),\hskip 17.00024pt(A\circ B)\,\diag

\displaystyle=(A\,\diag)\circ B=A\circ(B\,\diag)

(A.144)

and

\displaystyle\diag\,(A\circ B)\,\diag

\displaystyle=(\diag\,A\,\diag)\circ B=A\circ(\diag\,B\,\diag),

(A.145)

\displaystyle\diag\,(A\circ B)\,\diag

\displaystyle=(\diag\,A)\circ(B\,\diag)=(A\,\diag)\circ(\diag\,B)\,.

(A.146)

5.

Let
A,B,\mathcal{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, then

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}\circ A,A\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{L}_{ij}\,A_{ij})\,A_{ij}\geq\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\underset{i,j}{\min}(\mathcal{L}_{ij})|A_{ij}|^{2}=\underset{i,j}{\min}(\mathcal{L}_{ij})\,\lVert A\rVert^{2},

(A.147)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}\circ A,B\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(\mathcal{L}_{ij}\,A_{ij})\,B_{ij}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A_{ij}\,(\mathcal{L}_{ij}\,B_{ij})=\langle A,\mathcal{L}\circ B\rangle.

(A.148)

6.

Let
R\in{\rm O}(3)
and
\mathcal{F}
be symmetric
(\mathcal{F}\in\Sym(3))
. Then for all
\mathrm{D}_{1},\mathrm{D}_{2}\in\Sym(3)
we have

\displaystyle\langle R\,\bigg[\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\circ[\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,\underbrace{R^{T}\,\mathrm{D}_{1}\,R}_{S_{1}}\,\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})]\bigg]\,R^{T},\mathrm{D}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}\circ[\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,S_{1}\,\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})],\underbrace{R^{T}\,\mathrm{D}_{2}\,R}_{S_{2}}\rangle

(A.149)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}\circ[\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,S_{1}\,\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})],S_{2}\rangle=\langle\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,S_{1}\,\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}),\mathcal{F}\circ S_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\langle S_{1},\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,(\mathcal{F}\circ S_{2})\,\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\rangle=\langle S_{1},\mathcal{F}\circ[\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,S_{2}\,\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})]\rangle

A.8.10
The Daleckii-Krein formula

The upcoming formula can be used to explicitly determine the Fréchet derivative of a primary matrix function
f(X)
(see Appendix
A.1
for a definition) at
X=A
, applied to a perturbation
H
.

Theorem A.22
.

(
Daleckii-Krein

[
15
]
). Let
A=Z\,D\,Z^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be a diagonalizable matrix, with
D
diagonal, and let
f
be continously differentiable on the spectrum of
A
. Then the Fréchet derivative of the primary matrix function
f(X)
at
X=A
, applied to the perturbation
H
, is equal to

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{X}f(A).H=Z\,(\mathcal{F}\circ(Z^{-1}\,H\,Z))\,Z^{-1}

(A.150)

where the symbol
\circ
denotes the Schur product and the matrix
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f^{\prime}(\mathrm{D}_{ii})&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti=j,\\
\frac{f(\mathrm{D}_{ii})-f(\mathrm{D}_{jj})}{\mathrm{D}_{ii}-\mathrm{D}_{jj}}&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti\neq j.\end{array}\right.

Corollary A.23
.

Let
V=R\,\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\,R^{T}\in\Sym^{++}(3)
with
R\in{\rm O}(3)
the orthogonal matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors of
V
. Then for the increment
H\in\Sym(3)
we have the representation of the derivative

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{V}\log V.H=R\,[\mathcal{F}\circ(R^{T}\,H\,R)]\,R^{T},\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 17.00024pt\mathcal{F}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti=j,\\
\frac{\log\lambda_{i}-\log\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti\neq j.\end{array}\right.

Corollary A.24
.

Let
B=R\,\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2})\,R^{T}\in\Sym^{++}(3)
with
R\in{\rm O}(3)
the orthogonal matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors of
B
. Then for the increment
H\in\Sym(3)
we have the representation of the derivative

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H=R\,[\mathcal{F}\circ(R^{T}\,H\,R)]\,R^{T},\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 17.00024pt\mathcal{F}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}}&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti=j,\\
\frac{\log\lambda_{i}^{2}-\log\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}^{2}-\lambda_{j}^{2}}&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti\neq j.\end{array}\right.

Corollary A.25
.

Let
D\in\Sym(3),\,B=R\,\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2})\,R^{T}\in\Sym^{++}(3)
with
R\in{\rm O}(3)
the orthogonal matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors of
B
. Set
X\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2})
and let
\mathcal{F}
be given as in the previous Corollary, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}}&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti=j,\\
\frac{\log\lambda_{i}^{2}-\log\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}^{2}-\lambda_{j}^{2}}&\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012pti\neq j.\end{array}\right.

Then we have the following alternative representation for
\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]:

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]

\displaystyle=R\,\big[\mathcal{F}\circ\{R^{T}\,[B\,D+D\,B]\,R)\}\big]\,R^{T}

(A.159)

\displaystyle=R\,\big[\mathcal{F}\circ\{X\,\underbrace{R^{T}\,D\,R}_{S}+\underbrace{R^{T}\,D\,R}_{S}\,X\}\big]\,R^{T}=R\,\big[\mathcal{F}\circ\{X\,S+S\,X\}\big]\,R^{T}

\displaystyle=R\,\big[\mathcal{F}\circ(X\,S)+\mathcal{F}\circ(S\,X)\big]\,R^{T}=R\,\big[(X\,\mathcal{F})\circ S+(\mathcal{F}\,X)\circ S\big]\,R^{T}

\displaystyle=R\,\big[\underbrace{(X\,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{F}\,X)}_{=\,\mathcal{L}\in\Sym(3)}\circ(R^{T}\,D\,R)\big]\,R^{T}=R\,\big[\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,D\,R)\big]\,R^{T}.

Furthermore, by the monotonicity of
\log(\,)
, every entry of
\mathcal{F}
and thus also every entry of
\mathcal{L}=X\,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{F}\,X
, where
X
was given by
X=\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2})
, is non-negative.

We may immediately use Corollary
A.25
to prove

Lemma A.26
.

For
D\in\Sym(3),\,B\in\Sym^{++}(3)
we have (while
\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H\neq B^{-1}\,H
in general)

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\ B],D\rangle>c^{+}(B)\,\lVert D\rVert^{2}.

(A.160)

Proof.

From Corollary
A.25
we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\ B],D\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle R\,\big[\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,D\,R)\big]\,R^{T},D\rangle=\langle\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,D\,R),(R^{T}\,D\,R)\rangle

(A.161)

\displaystyle\geq\underset{i,j}{\min}(\mathcal{L}_{ij})\,\lVert R^{T}\,D\,R\rVert^{2}=c^{+}(\mathcal{L})\,\lVert D\rVert^{2}\,.\qed

As a side-product of these considerations we also obtain

Lemma A.27

(
\log
is strongly Hilbert-monotone)
.

\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H,H\rangle>0\hskip 17.00024pt\forall\,H\in\Sym(3)\setminus\{0\}.

(A.162)

Proof.

Since by Corollary
A.25
every entry of
\mathcal{F}
is non-negative, we obtain, by using the Daleckii-Krein formula,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H

\displaystyle=R\,[\mathcal{F}\circ(R^{T}\,H\,R)]\,R^{T}

(A.163)

\displaystyle\implies\hskip 8.50012pt\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H,H\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{F}\circ(R^{T}\,H\,R),(R^{T}\,H\,R)\rangle\geq\underset{i,j}{\min}(\mathcal{F}_{i,j})\,\lVert R^{T}\,H\,R\rVert^{2}=c^{+}\,\lVert H\rVert^{2}.\qed

Corollary A.28
.

The previous Lemma additionally shows that
\det\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B>0
and
[\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B]^{-1}\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
.

Lemma A.29
.

D\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]
is self-adjoint (major symmetric), i.e. for
\mathrm{D}_{1},\mathrm{D}_{2}\in\Sym(3)

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,\mathrm{D}_{1}+\mathrm{D}_{1}\,B],\mathrm{D}_{2}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,\mathrm{D}_{2}+\mathrm{D}_{2}\,B],\mathrm{D}_{1}\rangle\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{holds.}

(A.164)

Proof.

We make use of Lemma
A.26
, which yields

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,\mathrm{D}_{1}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{D}_{1}\,B],\mathrm{D}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle R\,\big[\mathcal{L}\circ\underbrace{(R^{T}\,\mathrm{D}_{1}\,R)}_{S_{1}}\big]\,R^{T},\mathrm{D}_{2}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{L}\circ S_{1},\underbrace{R^{T}\,\mathrm{D}_{2}\,R}_{S_{2}}\rangle=\langle S_{1},\mathcal{L}\circ S_{2}\rangle\overset{\mathcal{L},S_{1},S_{2}\in\Sym(3)}{=}\langle\mathcal{L}\circ S_{2},S_{1}\rangle

(A.165)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,\mathrm{D}_{2}\,R),R^{T}\,\mathrm{D}_{1}\,R\rangle=\langle R\,\big[\mathcal{L}\circ(R^{T}\,\mathrm{D}_{2}\,R)\big]\,R^{T},\mathrm{D}_{1}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,\mathrm{D}_{2}+\mathrm{D}_{2}\,B],\mathrm{D}_{1}\rangle\qed

Remark A.30
.

Note that in general,
\mathrm{D}_{B}f(B)
is self adjoint for any primary matrix function
f
, which includes the case
f=\log
, since any such function admits a potential
[
66
]
. Moreover, for
f:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
strongly monotone,
\mathrm{D}_{B}f(B)\in\Sym^{++}_{4}(6)
.

Let us choose
D=\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}
and calculate

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log_{B}.[2\,B\,\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}]

\displaystyle=R\,\big[\mathcal{F}\circ\{2\,\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{3})\,\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\}\big]\,R^{T}=2\,R\,\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}\,\lambda_{1}^{2}\,\alpha&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}^{2}}\,\lambda_{2}^{2}\,\alpha&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{\lambda_{3}^{2}}\,\lambda_{3}^{2}\,\alpha\end{pmatrix}\,R^{T}=2\,\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}\,.

(A.166)

Hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]\big|_{D=\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}}=2\,D\big|_{D=\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}}\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{(where $B$ is arbitrary).}

(A.167)

The latter result is clear since
B\,D=D\,B
in this case (
\exp(B+D)=\exp(B)\,\exp(D)
if
B\,D=D\,B)

\mathrm{D}_{B}\exp(B).D=\exp(B).D
(Taylor expansion).

Hence, the question arises, if in general

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]=2\,D\,?

(A.168)

From (
A.159
) we must have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\circ(\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2})\,R^{T}\,D\,R+R^{T}\,D\,R\,\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2}))=2\,\underbrace{R^{T}\,D\,R}_{S\in\Sym(3)}

(A.169)

so that, by setting
S=R^{T}\,D\,R
, we need to have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\circ(\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2})\,S+S\,\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2}))\overset{?}{=}2\,S\hskip 17.00024pt\forall\,S\in\Sym(3).

(A.170)

Case
n=2
,
S=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
b&c\end{pmatrix}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\circ(\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2})\,S

\displaystyle+S\,\diag(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2}))

(A.171)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}&\frac{2(\log\lambda_{1}-\log\lambda_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2}}\\
\frac{2(\log\lambda_{1}-\log\lambda_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2}}&\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}^{2}}\end{pmatrix}\circ\begin{pmatrix}2\,\lambda_{1}^{2}\ a&(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})\,b\\
(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})\,b&2\,\lambda_{2}^{2}\,c\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}2a&\frac{2(\log\lambda_{1}-\log\lambda_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2}}\,(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})\,b\\
\frac{2(\log\lambda_{1}-\log\lambda_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2}}\,(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})\,b&2c\end{pmatrix}\neq\begin{pmatrix}2a&2b\\
2b&2c\end{pmatrix}=2S,

leading to the negative conclusion

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]\neq 2\,D\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{in general.}}

(A.172)

However, if
B\,D=D\,B
, we obtain

Proposition A.31
.

Under the assumption
B\,D=D\,B
we have

\displaystyle\boxed{B\,D=D\,B\hskip 17.00024pt\implies\hskip 17.00024pt\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]=2\,D.}

(A.173)

Proof.

We first prove, that

\displaystyle B\,H=H\,B\hskip 17.00024pt\implies\hskip 17.00024pt\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H=B^{-1}\,H

(A.174)

Therefore, observe that for
B\,H=H\,B

\displaystyle\exp(B+H)=\exp(B)\,\exp(H)=\exp(B)\,[{\boldsymbol{\mathbbm{1}}}+H+\mathcal{O}(H^{2})]=\exp(B)+\exp(B)\,H+\mathcal{O}(H^{2})

(A.175)

and also by expansion of
\exp(\,)

\displaystyle\exp(B+H)=\exp(B)+\mathrm{D}\exp(B).H+\mathcal{O}(H^{2})

(A.176)

so that by comparison of linear (in
H
) terms of (
A.175
) and (
A.176
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\exp(B).H=\exp(B)\,H,\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 8.50012ptB\,H=H\,B.

(A.177)

Applying the chain rule to
\exp(\log B)=B
then shows for
B\,H=H\,B

\displaystyle\exp(\log B)

\displaystyle=B\hskip 17.00024pt\implies\hskip 17.00024pt\mathrm{D}\exp(\log B).[\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H]=H

(A.178)

\displaystyle\implies\hskip 17.00024pt\underbrace{\exp(\log B)}_{B}\,[\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H]

\displaystyle=H\hskip 17.00024pt\implies\hskip 17.00024pt\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H=B^{-1}\,H.

Now assume that
D\,B=B\,D
. Then for
H=D\,B+B\,D
we also obtain

\displaystyle B\,H=B\,(D\,B+B\,D)=(D\,B+B\,D)\,B=H\,B

(A.179)

and thus we can use the rule
\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H=B^{-1}\,H
for
H=D\,B+B\,D
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.[B\,D+D\,B]

\displaystyle=B^{-1}\,(B\,D+D\,B)=2\,D,\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 17.00024ptB\,D=D\,B.\qed

(A.180)

A.8.11
Richter’s and Vallée’s formula

From
[
78
]
we have the following

Lemma A.32
.

(Vallée’s formula
36
36

36

In
[
127
]
Vallée et al. have given a proof without using a Taylor expansion.
(see
also
[
126
,
127
,
51
,
110
,
74
]
))

Let
\Psi:{\rm Sym}^{++}(3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{W}}\colon\Sym(3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be both differentiable isotropic scalar valued functions. Then for the function
\Psi(B)=\widehat{\mathrm{W}}(\log B)
we have still the chain rule

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{B}\Psi(B)=\mathrm{D}_{\log B}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}(\log B)\,B^{-1}\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{while}\hskip 17.00024pt\mathrm{D}_{B}\log B.H\neq B^{-1}\,H\,.

(A.181)

Proof.

For a simple proof using Taylor-expansion, see
[
78
]
.
∎

Yet another similar result is given by the

A.8.12
Golden-Thompson inequality

The Golden-Thompson inequality (cf. Golden
[
32
]
and Thompson
[
119
]
, see also Tao
[
117
]
) implies that for symmetric matrices
B,D\in\Sym(3)
the inequality

\displaystyle\textnormal{tr}(\exp(B+D))\leq\textnormal{tr}(\exp(B)\,\exp(D))

(A.182)

holds. It is motivated by the fact, that for scalar values
b,d\in\mathbb{R}
, we obviously have
\mathrm{e}^{b+d}=\mathrm{e}^{b}\,\mathrm{e}^{d}
. It is well known, that for
commuting matrices

B,D
the same equality holds, i.e.

\displaystyle\exp(B+D)=\exp(B)\,\exp(D),\hskip 17.00024pt\textnormal{if}\hskip 17.00024ptB\,D=D\,B.

(A.183)

However
, if
B
and
D
do not commute, the equation (
A.183
) does no longer hold. In fact, Petz
[
92
]
proved that for two symmetric matrices
B,D\in\Sym(3)
, it holds

\displaystyle\textnormal{tr}(\exp(B+D))=\textnormal{tr}(\exp(B)\,\exp(D))\hskip 17.00024pt\iff\hskip 17.00024ptB\,D=D\,B.

(A.184)
